on behalf of the left group. mr president, seven years after the troika's adjustment programmes, the european commission's technical teams are back. my country knows them well. the last time we were governed according to the guidelines of these technical teams, the european commission did not spare the right-wing government of the day any praise. the result was the greatest economic recession and the greatest social catastrophe in the history of our democracy. these teams recommended even greater deregulation of the labor market, which has skyrocketed unemployment, and they also recommended unprecedented cuts in our national health service, which has left it weakened to meet the challenges it faces today. they always say it's all very voluntary and now it comes masked with good intentions. but we've seen this movie before. and so i suggest that the commission leave its technicians in brussels, give the money to the member states and let those governments that european citizens have chosen, with the programmes that have been paid for, govern. it's called democracy.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, i thank marie-pierre vedrenne for her report and i would like to begin by reiterating the european parliament's full support for the rules-based multilateral trading system and, unequivocally, for the wto and its reform. however, in the face of the paralysis of the wto dispute settlement mechanism's appellate body, the european union acted swiftly to overcome the gaps in european legislation and ensure the ability to protect the interests of the union and its member states in international trade agreements. parliament wanted more and went further. we have extended the scope of the regulation to include services and intellectual property rights in the list of eu trade policy measures. both represent a significant and growing part of global trade. the credibility and deterrent effect of the regulation is thus strengthened, and the european union can also take action in these areas as quickly and effectively as in other areas such as goods or public procurement. parliament also succeeded in obtaining a joint declaration on the urgency of a new instrument to deter and counteract coercive actions by third countries. last but not least, i would like to stress the commitment that this regulation should ensure the most consistent application of the mechanism in trade disputes relating to international trade agreements, covering the trade and sustainable development chapter.
mr president, three points on this subject: what is the situation with regard to the increase in vaccine production capacity in the european union? this increase should have been applied to all contracted vaccines and thus avoided delays in their delivery, ensuring accessibility to all regions, including the most peripheral and remote regions, such as the outermost regions. as regards contracts, and repeating what has already been said by several of my colleagues from various political quarters, when will it be possible for members to consult the various contracts in their entirety and with few or no parts censored? and finally, in the interests of transparency, there is an urgent need to set up a european dashboard accessible to all, which will make the process of distributing vaccines and subsequent vaccination in the different member states and regions monitored on a continuous basis, in order to improve the transparency of this whole process.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, a year ago we started to learn about the virus and we saw the first images coming from wuhan, china. less than 12 months later we have safe and effective european vaccines and all member states have started vaccinating their citizens. the many human losses we mourn should not for one second cause us to give up or to discredit the path we have taken together in the health response, in the procurement of equipment, of vaccines and in the simultaneous distribution of vaccination. but we want more. we want the european union to move forward with a certificate that will allow vaccinated citizens to regain their freedom of movement in the european area, helping to restore normalcy to key industries such as tourism. we want a europe committed to science and to truth. it is not acceptable that in the most advanced economic and social area in the world we have several countries where more than half of the citizens refuse to be vaccinated. disinformation also kills. but if we can say that europe has been up to the task, we cannot always say the same about governments. incoherent and complex decisions and communications are hampering the fight against the virus, and the problems of organizing and distributing vaccines are not acceptable to those who have had so much time to prepare. if lack of resources is sometimes a problem, nothing has cost more than inconsistency, lack of planning and ideological blindness.
mr president, commissioner, there is no doubt that the coronavirus pandemic has hit the world in an unprecedented way. we were unprepared, undoubtedly vulnerable, and as the czech proverb says, time has slipped out of the joint. we have a chance to put this situation back on track. vaccines are undoubtedly a success of science and of course of the pharmaceutical companies, but also of the european union. the virus is here to stay, it's very unexplored. however, at least we have some form of defense. i will not be one of those who criticize the european commission's approach to vaccine procurement. i am convinced that the commission sought to eliminate the risk, based on the facts and data known at the time. what would we be witnessing if the commission had not acted in this way? undoubtedly nationalism and overpayment between member states. vaccines must be distributed equally and without discrimination to all member states and to vulnerable groups. we need more transparency, that's for sure. we need a positive campaign for vaccination and we also need, in my view, a strengthening of the european union's powers in the field of health. and the constant permanence of the production capacity. we cannot be dependent on other parts of the world and be so de facto vulnerable.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, i want to thank you first of all for a very interesting and very informative debate. a great deal has been discussed and mentioned and i will try to pick up as many points as possible. first of all, i just want to start by saying that we share the many concerns voiced by many of you here in terms of the covid—19 fatigue and the need for citizens to return to their normality and i wanted to pick up with you an issue that hasn’t been mentioned today but that is extremely important, that we are aware of and that we are looking at and that is the impact that covid—19 and this pandemic is also having on the mental health of each and every european citizen. many of you mentioned the issues of production. i just wanted to share with you that the bottleneck at the moment is not in the number of orders that has been placed but in the worldwide shortage of production capacity. because the strategy has been mentioned a number of times, i wanted to just say that in june we decided with the 27 member states that the eu would not put all its eggs in one basket and that we would really work with member states in order to have a diversified portfolio and that has been achieved because we now have six vaccines all with apas signed, and two so far being authorised as being safe and effective. this shows the power of unity, the power of joining forces and it is what everybody wanted and we were mandated to do: to carry out the negotiations with and on behalf of the 27 member states. all decisions on which vaccine candidates were taken to invest in and how was taken jointly with everyone agreeing on the approach of building a broad portfolio. in october, there was a communication by the commission asking all the member states to be prepared for their rollout for their vaccine strategies and i have said many times in this parliament that finding a safe and effective vaccine would not be a silver bullet; it would not be possible for us to end the pandemic in one day. we are working as closely as we can with all member states, of course, in order to be able to ensure that rollouts move as quickly as possible and in the communication that we issuing later on today we’re asking member states to send information to the european centre for disease prevention and control (ecdc) at least twice a week so we are able to monitor the situation. so we now have two vaccines, which have been approved and are being progressively distributed at increasing speed. every day and every vaccination counts and the stock of vaccines delivered need to be used as quickly as possible. we are working with member states in order to support them with this, but let’s not all forget that this is the first time in history that we are all undertaking such a mass vaccination campaign of this size and of this complexity and we do face already and they will be more challenges down the road, but we are there to support member states and solve these problems as they appear. some of you mentioned the situation that appeared a few days ago with biontech—pfizer. i just wanted to let you know that, from the moment that this situation appeared, i was in calls continuously with the health ministers and we called a meeting immediately of the steering committee together and we are working continuously with biontech—pfizer in order to support them to develop more production capacities in the eu. we are fully mobilised – both the european medicines agency and the commission – to support the ramping up of production as soon as possible. in terms of the importance of doing this together, i have said before that joining forces is the only way to ensure that all member states had access to so many different vaccines at the same time and, as many of you said, at the same price. but now we need to really work as quickly as possible to roll these out and fairness has always been a crucial principle in our strategy. we were very clear about this and we are now asking member states to move as quickly as possible to be able to reach citizens. you mentioned issues of bilateral contracts and parallel negotiations. i will repeat what i have said many times: the purpose and the guiding principle of our vaccine strategy is to avoid parallel negotiations. this would undermine the strategy’s effectiveness. we now have two approved vaccines. we have negotiated delivery of 300 million additional doses of biontech—pfizer. we are awaiting the scientific assessment of the astrazeneca vaccine at the end of this month and the johnson & johnson is under review. we therefore consider that, with these supplies, we will be able to address fully the needs of member states. all member states joined the eu strategy for the covid—19 vaccine and this implies both rights and obligations, and so far all member states are part of the contracts we have negotiated. so, it is not legally possible for them to negotiate bilateral contracts with the companies in the eu portfolio and i have reminded member states of these rights and obligations. many of you mentioned transparency. i will be as brief as possible. we are, as a commission, 100% in favour of transparency, we can show absolute flexibility on this. we have been working with companies; biontech—pfizer has agreed to release its contract and we are now working with the other companies to move forward but we cannot unilaterally decide to disclose confidential information, such as information on prices, under the contracts unless the companies agree. liability was used. liability under the conditional marketing authorisations remains with the manufacturers and holders and they are responsible for the products and the safe use and this is the importance of the conditional marketing authorisation that we had followed. in terms of the global aspect, i believe that when it comes to dealing with a global pandemic of these proportions, we should not be speaking about competition between countries. we saw from the beginning that the ‘first us’ approach does not work and we need to have global solidarity in order to really be a shield against covid—19. this is why we launched the coronavirus global response; this is why we are strong supporters of access to the covid—19 tools (act) accelerator. the issue of diagnostics and therapeutics was also mentioned. we need to work on that as well for all countries and not just vaccines. global access and equal access to vaccines is the reason why we have joined covax and we will continue to support it. in terms of delivery, in today’s communication we will be stressing that we need to work with companies to deliver a clear and transparent schedule for deliveries. i want to just say here that the practical arrangements of the deliveries are laid down in the contracts between the company and the member states and we are doing our best really to help this on a daily basis, constantly on the phone trying to help our member states with issues that appear. wherever feasible we do step in to offer support to the member states and to liaise with the company to help ensure that engagements are respected. the variants were mentioned. this is a very important issue, thank you for raising it. currently there is no evidence that the biontech—pfizer and moderna vaccines would not be effective against this variant but information is still being collected and analysed and ongoing. i’m in constant contact with the european medicines agency for this. typically, viruses mutate so we need to follow it up very carefully, and again today in the communication, we are going to be urging member states to do more genomic sequencing across the eu to identify variants. this is crucial in order for us to share this information so that we are able to follow it. there is no evidence so far that it does not cause more serious illness or that the vaccines are less effective but it does appear that there is evidence that they are transmitted more quickly. and, last but not least, vaccination certificates. this is a common approach across the eu to monitor vaccine effectiveness, it may be needed once citizens are vaccinated. this could reinforce the success of the vaccination programmes in member states. we are in active discussions with the member states on how we will move forward with this because they need to be recognised across all member states and we could also set a global example. we welcome all inputs from member states, including that we had recently from greece, it is an important issue and it is going to be one of the issues as well as the rollout strategies that will be discussed this week at the european council with the heads of state. i will end here. i want to thank you once again for a very important and informative discussion, and i want to say what i have been saying from the beginning, that we will leave no stone unturned until we exit this pandemic because we all understand how important it is that citizens and economies and societies are able to return to a way of life as we knew before. thank you again for the opportunity.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, i would like to thank honourable members for this debate. i took good note of positions, demands and suggestions, but i also heard, loud and clear, the expression of support for all of the european union to work united and at the eu level, together with the commission. since the start of this crisis, the european union and its member states have closely worked together and mobilised massive resources to fight against the virus. we have managed to develop, to distribute, and to start the process of vaccination. we need to continue, united and in coordination. to start, the vaccination campaign offers a glimpse of hope. we still have a long way to go before we can get to herd immunity so we need to continue to work. we need the commission to support us with the establishment of targets for all member states in terms of the vaccination. we need to have european recognition of tests, and the capacity to produce and discuss the question of the vaccine certificate. we need to continue the genomic sequencing of the virus. we need to produce more data that is compatible and that we can use among member states to get more information. we need transparency in all these processes. only in this way will we get the trust of our citizens. the heads of state and government will discuss all these matters at the meeting that will take place in two days. i think this debate was really informative and very important at this stage. we need the continued cooperation of all the eu institutions in these challenging times. united, we can make the european union and the entire world a safer place. we need to have a common goal in the european union and around the world to defeat this virus. it is clear that no country will be able to defeat the covid—19 pandemic. we need solidarity. we need to talk to other countries about covax and about how we can reinforce who technical cooperation and also work in the european team spirit. united, we can make the european union and the entire world a safer place to fight against this virus. together we stay stronger.
the debate is closed. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. the eu is right to apply an external dimension to its vaccination strategy, as we will not end the virus in europe until we have defeated it worldwide. we must stand together against the pandemic for medical efficacy, ethical principles and to show our value as partners. europe will give partner countries access to part of the 2.3 billion doses guaranteed by its strategy. it also donates €853 million to covax. however, developed vaccines have been monopolized through bilateral contracts by the wealthier countries (13 percent of the world's population), delaying their acquisition by the poorer countries until 2022. two million people have already died. one million in seven months and another in three months. the virus is spreading faster than our vaccine rollout. so we need to address scarcity and distribution. we need more producers developing the vaccine, especially in low- and middle-income countries. we must give them access to the knowledge that develops new and affordable vaccines for everyone. we must move forward along the path indicated by who and its covid-19 technology access fund, considering vaccines as global public goods.
in writing. in the current pandemic situation, vaccines are an important means of fighting the virus and saving lives. the speed with which these vaccines have been developed is remarkable and a demonstration of the enormous potential of science and technology in our day. we believe that vaccination plans cannot be subject to a rationing policy that serves the interests of multinational laboratories that want to have a monopoly on the sale of vaccines in the european union. these multinationals are resisting the subcontracting of other laboratories and the sharing of patents, despite the gravity of the current situation and despite all the aid and the large public financial resources and millions of dollars put at their disposal for vaccine research and production and with guaranteed sales. we cannot accept that the selfish interests of pharmaceutical monopolies should prevail over the right to health and life of the population, and therefore we must act to put pressure on the big laboratories and also find solutions to diversify the procurement of vaccines. because it seems obvious that the solution of joint vaccine purchasing does not allow mass vaccination of populations with the desirable speed.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is of the utmost importance to modernise and future-proof europe's multimodal transport infrastructure in order to ensure the functioning of the internal market and competitiveness, and to support the transition to more sustainable, more efficient and safer mobility. it is therefore very important that all member states have a well-developed network that takes into account territorial accessibility throughout the european union, in particular in the peripheral, island and outermost regions, in order to promote and boost european and international tourism. it is important to take account of the quality of connections, accessibility, infrastructure and prices to and from these regions. the principles of cohesion also require a good transport policy, which leaves no region behind.
in writing. it does not take elaborate or sophisticated arguments to stress the importance of the trans-european transport network. let's just talk about the present, let's just talk about this wretched pandemic that's plaguing us. let us reflect, simply and objectively, on the importance of european connections in the past year. i'm talking about supply chains of basic necessities, whether it's food or medical. i'm talking about the possibility of being transported. i even speak, to be sufficiently emphatic, of the distribution of vaccines. i would like to add another matter which is absolutely relevant: the environment. we must not forget that the european union is a major player in the world economy. we are also working towards the objectives of the european green deal. we therefore have a duty to strive for a rapid and effective transition to sustainable, smart and safe mobility by incorporating new technologies and strengthening the link between transport and infrastructure. the eu's regional cohesion, the cross-border mobility of goods and people, effective access to all european territories, will bring citizens closer together and strengthen economies and the single market. the future and success of the eu and the single market depend on a true transeuropean transport network.

the rapporteur. - madam president, mr high representative, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and all my colleagues for their intensive cooperation and dialogue. without this, we would not have been able to produce such a comprehensive and balanced report. the fact that this is the annual report does not and cannot make it a mere ritual. it is a document awaited by many people around the world whose rights are violated and so often denied, and who look to the european union for a sign of encouragement. throughout this report, compared with previous ones, we have seen that improvements are slow and the challenges are increasingly complex. the global threat to democracy and the rule of law, resulting from a combination of factors such as the rise of authoritarianism and populism, the increase in inequalities and poverty, the shrinking space for civil society, the malicious use of information technologies and political interference by external actors, challenges us at every turn. the european union is called upon to take increasingly more action to strengthen democratic institutions and transparent and credible electoral processes, to combat inequalities and to support civil society organisations. the fight against climate change and the defence of the environment and human rights are increasingly interlinked and require a response based on multilateralism. attacks on human rights defenders, environmentalists, indigenous peoples, journalists, political opponents and minorities continue to be a constant. to put an end to them and to combat the impunity enjoyed by perpetrators of human rights violations requires the convergence of states, not only in the adoption of firm conclusions by foreign affairs, but also in firm and assertive action. the setbacks in gender equality, sexual and reproductive health and rights in many parts of the world cannot be counted on our approval, as well as the stigmatization and persecution of lgbti people. child health and education, the eradication of child labour and the fight against all forms of child abuse continue to be battles that do not admit of truce. the need to respond to the plight of migrants and refugees requires determined, supportive and sustainable action, in line with the migration pact. the increase in international tensions and the effect of the activity of non-state armed groups and terrorist organisations on the growth of violence are sources of serious human rights violations and require an increasing effort in cooperation for peace and development. to overcome these and so many other challenges, it is imperative to put human rights and democracy and the rule of law at the heart of our foreign policy and at the centre of european policies. this means using all the instruments at our disposal, systematically introducing human rights clauses in all the union's international agreements, with a particular focus on trade and association agreements, making the implementation of the action plan for democracy and human rights 2020-2024 an open and participatory task, promoting consultation and structured dialogue with civil society and parliament, taking up the uncompromising defence of multilateralism and un reform and supporting the international criminal court, highlighting the effectiveness of our foreign policy through qualified majority voting in the council on human rights issues, moving forward with the establishment of an urgent due diligence mechanism, which removes from our supply chains products and raw materials whose procurement amounts to a violation of human rights, among others. the exceptional nature of the pandemic period between the time of this report and its presentation has meant that it has not been entirely untouched by the subject. we welcome the response to covid-19 by states that have made the right to life and health their priority, but we cannot but express our concern that in some countries covid-19 has led to a decline in standards of democracy and human rights. this crisis cannot be used to consolidate authoritarian powers or weaken the rule of law and all measures to respond to the pandemic must be based on human rights, on non-discrimination in any form and by any person. but if we are to face the future, it is important not to give in to the density of this whole picture and to grasp the signs of hope. the new global mechanism for sanctions for human rights violations, which has long been called for in this parliament, is a milestone in the fight against impunity and i hereby congratulate the high representative for his role in this matter. despite the persistent trend of shrinking civil society space, seeing citizens, especially young people, mobilizing around political and social causes, demanding more and better responses to climate change, in defense of democracy and human rights, shows us that another tomorrow is possible. the demands of the present time do not allow for our resignation. it's time to act. to paraphrase churchill, i would say that it is not enough to say that we are doing the best we can, we must be able to do what is necessary.

madam president, dear president ursula von der leyen, dear prime minister antónio costa, allow me to begin by saying a word of support to all professionals, especially healthcare professionals, who are working on the front lines of the fight against covid-19. but this pandemic didn't just happen. according to scientific data, it is a symptom of a failed and broken system. i am talking about the way we produce and consume food in a globalised world and, given the risks to public health, the impacts on biodiversity and climate change mitigation, as well as the welfare of millions of animals in europe, i ask the commission and the council presidency whether they will actively seek to ensure that the decisions resulting from the negotiations on the common agricultural policy, now in the trilogue phase, are based on the european plate-to-platter strategy and the strategy for the preservation of biodiversity. the common agricultural policy is not just 34% of the multiannual financial framework. it is a key policy to deliver on the european green deal and the best tool to implement resilient and regenerative production processes.
madam president, mr prime minister, the scale of the problems facing several countries in the european union, including portugal, requires the implementation of measures that are the opposite of those that have been at the root of the decline in labour and social rights, the growing social inequality and concentration of wealth, the weakening and privatisation of public services, the increase in dependency and indebtedness, and the disregard for sovereignty for decades. the gravity of the situation calls for a different approach to ensure employment with rights, the strengthening of public services and the social functions of the state, support for micro, small and medium-sized enterprises, investment in the strategic and productive sectors, ensuring the sovereign development of each country and convergence in economic and social progress. the so-called 'recovery fund' and the multiannual financial framework should promote the achievement of these objectives by ensuring a rapid mobilisation of resources, taking into account the economic and social reality and the specific needs of each country and without imposing conditionalities that interfere with sovereign decisions. the social summit announced today must not be reduced to a declaration of intentions and policies based on levelling, social regression or measures to mitigate the impact of a planned destruction of jobs. what is required is a pact for employment and social progress aimed at full employment, employment with rights, the eradication of precariousness, wage appreciation. a pact that promotes the defence and strengthening of public services in each country, ensuring the universality of rights such as the right to health, education, social security or housing.

on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, in a time of crisis where we need all the resources we can muster, starting with the financial resources, our national budgets cannot continue to lose 20% of corporate tax revenues every year by transferring them to tax havens. every penny we lose is needed to help rebuild and revive the economy and jobs. we need competitive european companies. it is businesses, especially smes, that are the engine of our recovery. it is therefore important to continue to rely on the internal market, with fair and loyal tax competition. it is precisely because we believe in this justice and fairness that we cannot compromise on the use of tax havens to conceal income and to avoid paying taxes that are due. the tax haven blacklist is an essential tool to flag states or regions, promote reforms in these jurisdictions and discourage their use by citizens and businesses. but we need more and better criteria for identifying tax havens. we need transparency and we need a list that effectively contributes to the fight against tax evasion, tax avoidance and money laundering. we need to stop this pandemic of tax evasion. this parliament has had the ambition to point the way. it takes courage for national governments to follow suit!
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner gentiloni, secretary of state ana paula zacarias, ladies and gentlemen, the difficulties faced by citizens in the current crisis make the injustice of our tax system even more glaring. citizens are making many sacrifices, small and medium-sized businesses, but at the same time some millionaires and many multinationals are taking advantage of tax havens to avoid paying taxes. this is simply unacceptable and the european union has to do a much better job of fighting tax havens. the lack of ambition, consistency and transparency of the tax haven blacklist must be overcome once and for all. as our subcommittee chairman and rapporteur paul tang has already pointed out, the cayman islands were recently removed from the list of tax havens. the citizens see, but they don't believe. it is estimated that this jurisdiction accounts for over 16% of global tax losses, a staggering figure, higher than the gdp of many member states. it is not admissible! we cannot continue in this way and we welcome the determination shown here by the presidency of the council. with this resolution we propose to revise the criteria for this list with greater transparency and democratic scrutiny, with a criterion to include in the list any country which simply does not collect taxes, as well as those which have very low taxes, below a minimum rate set by the union. but the union must also set an example and not allow similar internal practices in unfair competition, which always results in a race to the bottom in the financing of public policy. we therefore hope, and we shall be watching, that this resolution will merit a very broad consensus here in parliament, reflecting the commitment of all to these proposals.
in writing. we all agree that the fight against tax fraud and tax evasion is very important and that every effort should be made to encourage the observance of strict transparency rules in third countries included in the list of tax havens. we must therefore continue to apply pressure on those states which refuse to cooperate in tax matters. the report also calls for the creation of a european tax haven system, which would be a good way of ensuring that the taxpayer is able to pay the tax in the member state in which he is resident. tax avoidance should not be confused with tax competition, the legal means available to member states for reducing taxes. in the very difficult circumstances of the covid-19 crisis, we must see that reducing the tax burden on companies is the key to emerging from the crisis and to competitiveness today: this must of course be planned and implemented by member states along the lines of responsible fiscal management, depending on the capacity of the country to bear the burden. hungary is leading the way in this area, as we have continued to reduce taxes since the crisis, saving hundreds of thousands of jobs. the result is clear, with employment levels at the beginning of 2021 only one percent below pre-crisis levels. in the light of these extraordinary results, it is even more irresponsible, in the present circumstances, to adopt a decision whose proposals and conclusions are completely contrary to the objectives and essential requirements of economic crisis management.

president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, first i would like to express the council’s and the portuguese presidency’s attachment to the european public prosecutor’s office. ensuring that the eppo starts functioning at the earliest opportunity is a priority for us. in this perspective it’s also equally important that the eppo, with its important mandate of prosecuting the crimes which affect the financial interests of the union, is a solid and credible office. this is in the interests of all stakeholders, including the eu institutions and the eu citizens. we all hope that the eppo will start functioning as currently planned in march this year. in achieving this objective, the council has worked in close cooperation with this parliament, which resulted in the appointment in october 2019 of the chief european prosecutor. the council has actively worked on the establishment of the eppo with a series of legislative texts, aiming at implementing the eppo regulation. in 2019-2020 substantive work was done with the objective of appointing european prosecutors. in july 2020 the decision appointing european prosecutors – one for each of the 22 participating member states in this reinforced cooperation – was adopted by the council, and this allowed the eppo college to start functioning in september 2020. the work which led to the appointment of the european prosecutors was carried out in full transparency and in the full respect for the applicable rules. i want to underline here that the council implementing decision on the operating rules of the selection panel states, and i quote, ‘the selection panel shall rank the candidates according to their qualifications and experience. the ranking shall indicate the selection panel’s order of preference and shall not be binding on the council’. the council thoroughly analysed the selection panel’s opinion on every candidate and followed the proposed ranking on 19 nominations. in the other three it decided otherwise, making use of its discretionary power. moreover, in addition to the legislative acts published in the official journal, all documents pertaining to the process have been handed to the interested parties when requested and in accordance with the council’s rules on access to documents. i’m aware of the fact that some of the appointments have been contested and that there has been criticism regarding the designation of prosecutors of some member states, including my own. i speak here on behalf of the council and i do not want to go into detail at this stage. so i will listen to you carefully and i will be ready to answer your questions. but ladies and gentlemen, let me state that the establishment of the eppo has required much more time than expected, with huge delays linked particularly with the pandemic. it is now time to combine our efforts to allow the eppo to become operational and start its activities on the protection of the union’s financial interests against criminal offences. this is something that the parliament wants. this is something that the council wants. and we need to work together on this.

member of the commission. herr präsident, ladies and gentlemen, the covid-19 crisis, the pandemic, is probably a natural disaster. poverty, the fact that children are condemned to exclusion, is not a natural disaster. it is the result of deep-seated dysfunctions in our society. if this is the result of deep-seated dysfunctions in our society, we have the key to making amends and changing that. i agree: we need economic growth, sustainable growth, respectful of nature, respectful of ecological balances. respectful, too, of the work of women and men. we need innovation, and that's why we've been debating investing in human capital, in education. we need entrepreneurs who create wealth, who create businesses. but what we also need, and i repeat mrs lagarde's words, is for everyone to have a share in it, for everyone to feel that their work is honoured, that they work with dignity. this is the ultimate goal we must pursue, because what is at stake is the cohesion of our societies, the future of our young people, our children. but it is also the future of europe. because even here we hear sirens telling us that all this is, of course, europe's fault. this is why the porto summit, the implementation of an ambitious social action plan, is so important. yes, a minimum wage is essential. it is essential for all those who work and cannot live with dignity from their work. i appeal to the conscience of all those who have doubts, who have legal or other reservations about this minimum wage course. think for a moment of the millions of workers who sometimes work more than 40 hours a week and who cannot provide a decent life for their families. i believe that making progress through an adequate, appropriate minimum wage, which improves their living conditions, which also improves their working conditions, is the least that europe can offer them.

in writing. we are discussing the poverty of those who work in a worrying social context marked by the existence of millions of workers and their families at risk of poverty. this picture was exacerbated by the effects of the covid-19 outbreak, but was already highly concerning and unresolved. we recall that in 2017, three years before covid-19, 20.5 million workers and households were at risk of poverty in the eu28, workers whose wages were not enough to lift them out of poverty! the eu and its neoliberal policies bear responsibility for the worsening of poverty and social exclusion, and we should not forget this. in the present situation, in which thousands of workers have lost their jobs, thousands more have been laid off, thousands of selfemployed workers and micro and small entrepreneurs have been forced to cease their activity, it is estimated that the poverty of the working people has reached very worrying dimensions. it is urgent to respond to this scourge. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union's social policy. at the same time, there is an urgent need to invest in the social functions of the state, social security, health and education.
on behalf of the left group. other madam president, the mutualisation of the debt which underpins this recovery fund is good news for the european union, news which has taken a long time to come. i can still remember a time when such a proposal was considered to be totally irresponsible. but this recovery fund is still a debt, and we should not deceive the citizens in this respect. mutual debt is debt and it is being contracted on account of budgets and future cohesion policy if we do not finish the job of approving the own resources that can pay it. the second good news about this recovery fund is that the punitive regime which was approved by this parliament, negotiated by the socialists and the right and approved by the main groups in this house, has fallen in the council and this, at a time of back-slapping championships, is news which should embarrass this parliament. we have been in a position of pressure from the standpoint of european solidarity, and it is a pity that it was the council which showed this house the seriousness of this precedent. one very important aspect is that, despite the fall of the punitive regime, there remains an umbilical cord to the european semester and the threat of the return of the famous macroeconomic conditionality. and here it is not worthwhile to appeal to the commission not to repeat the mistakes it has made in the past, because we know that it will repeat them. the regulation creates that link and gives those powers to the commission and it is naive to think that the european commission will not use them. once again, it will be up to the member states to resist these impositions and to resist the repetition of the mistakes of the past.
mr. president, the recovery and resilience facility is the heart of our economic recovery. we have a great responsibility and we will be here to monitor and scrutinize the execution. it is now up to the member states to do their part and to apply the resources swiftly, competently and transparently. the big public health challenge is containment of the pandemic. the great challenge of recovery is to turn this plan into concrete results. but just as the blunders in the vaccination plans of some member states are incomprehensible, any carelessness or negligence in the application of this mechanism will be unacceptable. over the last year, we've learned that the time of the pandemic outweighs the time of politics. it's time to reverse the trend and replace the ads with solutions. europeans do not need propaganda from their governments, they need answers. they do not need captivations, but concrete investments. they do not need excuses, but transparency in the use of funds. they don't need money spent on bureaucracy, but they need it to get into the real economy: into families and businesses. what the many entrepreneurs and workers who have supported the european welfare state until now need is for it not to fail them now and to allow them to rebuild their lives, their businesses and thus be able to contribute again to a brighter future for all.
the multiannual financial framework and the next generation eu are essential pillars of the european response to an unprecedented crisis, because of their scale and because they are based on the basic principle of european solidarity, the principle which governs the issuance of common european debt, i.e. the mutualisation of this debt. a joint effort at a decisive step in european history. the recovery and resilience facility is a unique opportunity to bring about real change, enabling the european union and all member states to emerge from this crisis with stronger, more cohesive, more equal economies. a shift towards a green and sustainable transition, based on a digital transformation of citizens and businesses. but this requires complementarity: complementarity with the national reform plans, complementarity with the budget of the european union, from the structural funds to horizon europe, to investeu, or to the eu4health programme. together, all these instruments are the key to change. it is therefore essential that the next generation eu be ratified by national parliaments to have a fund, but it is also essential that partnerships between the european commission and the member states work to facilitate the adoption of national economic and social recovery plans. its rapid adoption and good implementation is the urgency of recovery. we all have to do our part.
in writing. it is essential to ensure that the recovery and resilience mechanism strictly fulfils its purpose of repairing the damage caused by the covid-19 crisis, both in the labour market and in other sectors. the facility should contribute to making eu economies more sustainable, more resilient and better prepared for the challenges posed by the green and digital transitions. to achieve this, the guidelines for member states need to be clear. it must also be ensured that the distribution of support is fair and responds to the challenges that the regions will face. in this respect, special attention is needed for the outermost regions, where unemployment has reached worrying levels due to the high dependence of some of these regions on sectors that have been severely affected, such as tourism and transport. it is of the utmost importance to ensure that access to this mechanism is facilitated and de-bureaucratized in order for its implementation to be successful. monitoring of implementation is essential to ensure that there is no overlap between the use of this mechanism and other funding tools. this mechanism should be inclusive and member states should ensure that no one is left behind.
in writing. of course i want to express my satisfaction with the absolutely meaningful vote that formalizes the recovery and resilience mechanism. the eu, in a proactive attitude and by resorting to new solutions, has created an instrument which, i am sure, will allow us to respond to the various dimensions of the crisis we are experiencing. it also proved that there is always an alternative, showing that solidarity and the use of new solutions can take us away from the prophets of the inevitability of the recessionary policies that many have advocated and, worse, put into practice in the last decade. responding to a crisis of this magnitude with a response of this magnitude gives us hope and at the same time allows us to show europeans that the institutions can take action that has a real impact on the lives of citizens. at the end of the day, we're doing what we were elected to do: making decisions and finding solutions that impact people's lives. i repeat a phrase i have already uttered here: let's never forget that there are people out there watching us.
madam president, commissioner, the fight for women's rights, labour rights and equality, but also for sexual and reproductive health rights, has been a decades-long struggle. in this debate, we want to highlight and value the struggle of women in poland, but not only in slovakia and other countries, who, even in times of pandemic, continue to fight in the streets not only for sexual and reproductive rights, including access to voluntary termination of pregnancy, but also to combat serious legal setbacks. portuguese women, in their struggle for the decriminalization of voluntary termination of pregnancy, have received the solidarity of women from various countries. we are now returning this support by giving a boost to an initiative in portugal in solidarity with the struggle of polish women, to which outstanding women with interventions in various areas have joined. we believe that legislative setbacks on voluntary termination of pregnancy are an attack on women's health, rights and dignity, with increased risks for women from the most disadvantaged social and economic groups.
mr president, madam secretary of state, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i have three points to make. how is post-vaccination and post-infection immunity being assessed? we need to know if, with vaccination, we are protecting people and for how long. my question is: when will these results be available and if these studies show that an annual booster of the vaccine will be needed, how is the commission preparing for a scenario where sufficient doses will have to be procured so that member states can vaccinate their population repeatedly to ensure immunity? they presented ambitious targets last month to vaccinate 70 percent of the adult population by the summer. considering the scarcity and capacity problems of industry that have already been addressed here, what are the strategies that they will implement to achieve these targets or whether these targets will be changed to be in line with current production capacity. i would like to end my speech with a plea from the citizens. the european union has been advancing this vaccination process in a structured and coordinated way, and has set up a platform to monitor the entire process in the member states. i would ask for joint dissemination work between the council and the commission to ensure that this information reaches all citizens. for them and for a true european health union.
in writing. the regulation of social networks is not an easy subject for debate, since diametrically opposed fundamental rights are at stake here, but this is a pressing reality from which we cannot escape. it is therefore for parliament, in its role as co-legislator, to weigh up the legal rights protected by freedom of expression and information in order to find a balance between conflicting rights and, above all, to safeguard the public interest. and this is just as true if we consider that on the one hand, social media can unite people around common goals (responses to the climate emergency, mobilizing for protests that demand political and social change, among many other examples), as it can, with equal ease, separate them, through misinformation campaigns, from the call to hate, violence, persecution. the need for regulation is therefore urgent, but the terms on which it will be carried out and the criteria it must meet must be carefully defined so that the difficult balance of values mentioned above is achieved, as has been said, in such a way as to safeguard the public interest at all times.
on behalf of the left group. madam president, whenever there are initiatives to deregulate finance, the talk is always the same, it is to help the economy, it is to help small and mediumsized enterprises and now, apparently, it is also to help the environment. but it's not very well understood, and more confusing than any information in any financial product prospectus, is the rationale for these two initiatives. if the information that is being cut from the prospectuses for financial products in this proposal is not relevant information, it is redundant, it only confuses, it is not very clear why this information is being removed permanently, and it is also not very clear how removing information from the prospectuses helps the economic recovery. if financial products are good, then all the information will only convince investors to bet on those financial products. if the products are not that good, then the doctrine that presides over this proposal is to deceive small investors to help the economy which is, as they say, to help the banks. financial deregulation has never served to recover from any financial crisis. how many financial crises will this commission help to provoke before it learns this simple rule? i come from a country where hundreds, in fact thousands of small investors were deceived by a bank, losing their life savings, precisely because of the lack of relevant information about the products they were subscribing to. the guilty of these crimes died unmarried. neither the european institutions nor the national institutions have been held responsible for these frauds and i would very much like to be able to tell these small investors that such things will not happen again. these proposals do not help to fulfill that promise.
mr president, as a response to the crisis and the need to facilitate diversified sources of finance for our smes, the commission proposed a quick fix to mifid to ease some of the requirements companies face to raise money on the markets on a temporary basis. unfortunately, a balanced solution was not the result of the negotiation here at parliament. the epp negotiator insisted on relaxing the rules too much, taking us back to a deregulation paradigm, similar to what we had before the financial crisis. for us that is not acceptable. we cannot live with insufficient transparency, clarity and investor protection from mis-selling. these are key elements in the market and we have the experience from the financial crisis a decade ago to know where deregulation leads. have we learned nothing from the past? madam commissioner, this reform has become dangerous; thus, our group does not support it. but we still count on you for a balanced structural revision of mifid this year.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank members for their contributions. it’s been a robust debate, which i think is really important. what i see and what you have spoken of today is a pandemic, a health crisis, a very difficult economic situation and businesses that are struggling to survive. and let me be very clear. the people who will benefit from the capital markets recovery package are small companies, renewable energy producers and retail investors. mr ferber and colleagues are debating – it’s excellent to hear it from this podium! but let me continue. our proposals to amend mifid and prospectus will make it easier for capital markets to support european businesses so that they can recover from the crisis by raising essential equity capital. they will also facilitate the involvement of investors in the capital markets, while preserving our high levels of investor protection. as i’ve already said, this package will also be a positive signal to the financial markets in general and companies in particular. this package shows that the eu can adapt quickly to changing circumstances to support the real economy. the proposed measures are important and i hope they will be adopted and take effect as soon as possible. but these amendments are not the end of the road. in particular, i’m ready, with my services, to engage with all of you on our retail investment strategy, where we will take a holistic view of concerns on investor protection raised by the application of mifid and other sectoral legislation. we will also closely cooperate with parliament on a broader review of mifir to enhance market transparency. i’d like to refer to some of the comments that were made. as i said, i enjoy a robust debate. this is a vital issue. we know that our capital markets are not fully developed in europe, we know that banks cannot be the only source of funding for our smes and our businesses, and therefore we need to work together to strengthen our capital markets. and yes, you are right to refer to crises of the past. i recall 2008, and many of us know what happened to families and businesses because of that, but in fact in today’s crisis, because we regulated the banks effectively, we now see that the banks and the capital markets can be part of the solution, continuing to lend to businesses. i therefore think that anyone who might suggest that this commission, or me as commissioner for financial stability, is on a mission to deregulate, please note that this is not the case. if anything, my experience of the past and my work in this parliament previously make me more determined to have effective legislation and regulation which protects investors and provides us with a strong capital market. so can i say very clearly there will be no deregulation on my watch. i know that i won’t persuade many of you to vote in favour of this, but i hope that you understand the sincerity of my contribution to this debate. you know we will come forward very soon and look again at mifid, so there will be an opportunity for us to work together. as we look forward, i hope we can bring our sides together because we need a better coordinated approach on these issues through the recovery, because if we don’t have that, our companies will struggle. we will not be able to recreate employment in sectors where it is absolutely essential. so, despite the diverse views and the deep divisions, i take from all of you a desire to work better together, to strengthen our resolve around capital markets, and to protect our smes and our workers, and i stand ready to do the same with you and cooperate fully.
