president-in-office of the council. – mr president, commissioner, honourable members, gender equality is a priority for the portuguese presidency, and we are determined to do everything we can to tackle the emerging challenges and to push this agenda forward. the 25th anniversary of the beijing platform for action was an opportunity to take stock of the progress achieved, to reconfirm the commitments undertaken, and to identify new challenges, reminding us that all member states have a clear obligation to eliminate discrimination against women, and ensure that they fully enjoy all human rights. much progress has been made over the past few decades to the benefit of women and girls in the european union and across the world. however, many challenges remain, and we must step up and step up gender equality policies. unfortunately, the pandemic has aggravated some of these challenges. we need therefore to make an assessment of the impact of covid-19 on gender equality – in particular on labour market participation, incomes, and reconciliation of work, personal and family life. the council has recently also focused on the issue of care work. during the current pandemic, more than ever, it is time we recognise and value the importance of unpaid care work, giving credit to those who perform it and keep our societies running. other challenges that need to be addressed comprise gender discrimination, especially against more vulnerable groups of women, threats against sexual and reproductive health and rights, violence against women, and issues arising from digitalisation, including cyber—violence. equally important is to tackle the practices, prejudices and customs within society, including those carried out by private persons and organisations that hinder the fulfilment of women’s human rights. the most notable ongoing policy development in the union regarding women’s rights, and in particular violence against women, is in relation to the istanbul convention. although the proceedings on the eu ratification of the convention are temporarily suspended, pending the european court of justice opinion, calls have been made at council level for ratification by member states that have not yet done so. such ratification will precede adoption by the eu. in this regard, the portuguese presidency will host in april a conference in lisbon to mark the 10th anniversary of the convention. coming back to women and the labour market, the presidency will also work to unblock the directive on gender balance on company boards, and stands ready to start negotiations on binding pay transparency measures, on the basis of the forthcoming commission proposal. we also need to keep abreast of technological developments, and make sure that women and girls can seize the opportunities offered by digitalisation. finally, we must ensure gender equality mainstreaming across the implementation of the european pillar of social rights. with that in mind, the upcoming informal employment, social policy, health and consumer affairs council (epsco) meeting will address the issue of gender equality in social europe. mr president, honourable members, commissioner, gender equality is also at the heart of our international engagement. the council is finalising an ambitious eu position paper in preparation for the 26th session of the un commission on the status of women. this will take place in new york next march. the priority theme for this year is women’s full and effective participation and decision—making in public life, as well as the elimination of violence for achieving gender equality and the empowering of women and girls. this topic is very timely in the covid context, not least against the backdrop of the shadow pandemic of violence against women during lockdowns, but also with a view to ensuring that women are in the forefront of developing solutions during the pandemic and their recovery phase. the rights of women and girls, as well as gender equality needs to be addressed in a cross—cutting manner in all the eu external action, on the basis of the eu consensus on development, and the rights—based approach to development. we must recognise that, in many parts of the world, girls and women continue to be systematically left behind and discriminated against. in this context, the portuguese presidency welcomes the third gender action plan presented by the commission and the high representative at the end of last year. we look forward to working with the member states, the commission, and the european external action service (eeas) as well as with the european parliament to implement and advance this ambitious and utterly necessary agenda. the same is true for csdp missions and operations, where women are still under—represented at most levels. we hope that the eeas will develop a dedicated strategy and action plan to provide the systemic mainstreaming of human rights and gender aspects, promoting a better representation of women in csdp missions. in conclusion, much has been done and much more remains to be done. in numbers and relevant sectors, we need to look ahead, and we need to use the relevant elements of the beijing platform for action. gender equality is, and must remain, at the very core of everything that we do at union level.
member of the commission. – mr president, i thank the honourable members for putting the 25th anniversary of the beijing declaration and platform for action on the agenda today. this anniversary provides us with an excellent opportunity to discuss the situation of gender equality and empowerment of women and girls in this particular time. it is important to reflect on where we are and where we are heading to. the beijing declaration and platform for action was adopted 25 years ago. it is the most inspiring global blueprint of gender equality and it still guides us today. the beijing conference was a fundamental step in the global commitment that we have jointly embraced since. progress has been made in these years but girls and women still face many hurdles and challenges that remain unaddressed. across the world, including in some of our own member states, we see a serious threat for, and backlash on, women’s rights. this backlash manifests itself in various forms and settings, like for instance in the opposition we have seen in the eu to adhere to legal frameworks to combat violence against women. this opposition is sometimes based on the mere fact that the word ‘gender’ is used. this directly endangers the monitoring of the eu’s commitments under the beijing platform for action, as well as the work the eu does in terms of gender equality. it leads to a situation where no consensus can be found among member states on council conclusions that aim to report on the eu’s progress on the beijing platform for action. we should all mobilise our forces to counter this very worrying trend and not let this impede our international commitments under the beijing platform for action. every year during the meeting of the commission on the status of women (csw) at the un general assembly, we reaffirm the commitments agreed in the beijing declaration and platform for action and the engagement to embrace the new challenges ahead. the united nations secretary-general called for a pushback against the pushback. power remains a crucial element empowering women and girls, calls for equal rights for girls and women as boys and men. we need to make these equal rights a reality for all. this year’s csw session deals with full and effective participation of women in public and political life. it highlights the importance of women and girls as agents of change and as decision-makers and leaders on all issues and at all levels. this is crucial for adequately translating their needs and skills into policies to achieve gender equality and a more peaceful, secure, just and sustainable world. to reach this we need to help all enablers that allow for women’s and girls’ meaningful participation in public and political life. we also need to eradicate gender-based violence, including online violence, that risks to silence women, and we started our work on eu-wide legislation addressing this. in fact, last monday we launched a public consultation on this topic. this is a key priority for us, including as part of the generation equality forum, where the eu is a co-leader on gender-based violence. in order to tackle gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls, the commission adopted two key policy strategies: the gender equality strategy 2020-2025 for our internal action and the eu gender action plan iii for our external action. the crisis we are experiencing has amplified their relevance and urgency to advance and push further the beijing commitments and make a real change in the life of women and girls. the widening of socio-economic inequalities for women in particular, the spike in domestic violence and the digital gender gap are all at the centre of our areas of engagement, both internally and externally. the eu is committed to lead by example. this is firmly positioned at the core of the eu policy on gender equality and women’s empowerment. we do this by establishing the requirement of a gender-responsive leadership for all eu leaders and managers in the gender action plan iii. gender mainstreaming is one of the core principles of both the gender equality strategy and the gender action plan iii that we presented last year. this is a crucial principle to advance on gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls. it is central to the philosophy of the 12 beijing critical areas of concern. it underlines the importance of mainstreaming agenda perspective throughout all the sustainable development goals of agenda 2030 and its key principle not to leave anyone behind. it is also a crucial principle in the implementation of the women peace and security agenda, aiming for gender equality, peace and security. we need to advance not in words but in deeds, including through our financial commitments. the eu long-term budget 2021-2027, together with next generation eu, with a stimulus package of eur 1.8 trillion, will ensure financing for the promotion of gender equality. eighty-five per cent of all our external actions will be gender-responsive. they will promote gender equality and women’s empowerment across sectors of intervention. i particularly want to congratulate the european parliament on its successful role in ensuring a gender-sensitive perspective in the eu budget and recovery. together with the agenda 2030, the beijing declaration and platform for action, remains a crucial reference document to advance gender equality and to empower women and girls. the covid-19 pandemic and the growing backlash against women’s rights highlight the importance of our continued vigilance on gender equality. it is important now more than ever to keep working together to achieve the objectives that we have set.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, after 25 years of the beijing declaration, one would have assumed that we would have met already those 12 targets that we set there for women’s equality and for gender equality. but even though there has been some substantial progress, especially in some areas like in the eu, globally we have seen little progress, on one hand. and on the other hand, we have seen some backlashes, as we have seen in sexual and reproductive rights, even in some of the eu member states. and this is something that needs to come to end. what the parliament says is: looking through the targets of the beijing +25 evaluation, highlighting the points that need to be developed further acutely, and then underlies digitalisation, violence against women, especially in social media, the pay gap that still exists, the pension gap that comes out of that. full parity in representation is still lacking, and social rights, as has been said, very unequally treating male and female people. so, i encourage the commission and congratulate their activity in their gender equality programme, it’s a good one. and i encourage you to read very carefully the parliament’s resolution and other reports that we have made in this area, and we ensure the full support of whatever activities you are going to take in this field.
on behalf of the s&d group dear prime minister, minister, commissioner, colleagues, the beijing declaration is the most comprehensive global agenda for the promotion of gender equality, and today, a quarter of a century later, it is clear that while progress has been made in relation to girls and women, particularly in europe, overall progress is in fact unacceptably slow and risks taking the hard-won gains in gender equality to the downside. to date, no european union country has implemented the objectives set out in the beijing declaration. although all member states have ratified the convention on the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women (which, incidentally, entered into force more than 40 years ago), the persistence and even greater inequality is, unfortunately, a painful reality. ladies and gentlemen, gender inequality is a multifaceted phenomenon. it not only covers all aspects of the labour market, from lower pay for women in the same job to low job security, which in itself leads to a large gender pension gap, which increases the risk of poverty and exclusion, especially for older women and single mothers. gender inequality is not just an economic phenomenon - it is also violence against women and the sexual exploitation of women and girls. and this year we will mark the tenth anniversary of the istanbul convention. but it is a shame that to this day some member states have not ratified the istanbul convention. i would therefore urge the member states, the council and the commission to do everything possible to ratify the istanbul convention, so that we can send a clear message that protection against violence is a priority for the european union. the covid-19 pandemic has made it very clear what gender inequality really means. the social and economic impact of covid is disproportionately burdensome for women, as this pandemic has had the greatest impact on women-dominated sectors and professions, such as healthcare, social services, education, retail workers, cashiers, cleaners, women running small businesses. and there is a very real risk that this pandemic will only exacerbate already high inequalities and reverse the trend of progress. so i really urge both the commission, the commissioner, the presidency and my colleague to join forces to make real progress.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, 25 years after the beijing declaration as has been said, the most comprehensive global normative framework on women's rights, we have to recognize that no country in the world has achieved the goals of equality; not even the countries of the european union. there are still many challenges to be met: differences in pay, in public representation, in the economy, in large companies - but above all, as you have said, madam commissioner, we are facing enormous challenges today in terms of the setbacks and threats to the rights we have won. so today, when we see that in european union countries such as poland women's right to sexual and reproductive health is threatened and trampled on, we must remember, as was said in beijing 25 years ago, that women's rights are human rights and human rights are women's rights. and in the european union we cannot allow any country to trample on the human rights of its citizens. that's why i want to ask you to focus on the biggest threats. violence against women is a great threat and it is increasing. i ask you, commissioner, to urgently put in place a special protocol for the assistance and care of women victims of violence in emergency situations, such as the pandemic. we must not allow them, in a third wave, a fourth wave, to live alone and with their aggressor without the necessary services this violence.
on behalf of the green group, mr president, we are commemorating the 25th anniversary of the beijing declaration and platform for action. it was a historic moment, a moment of ambition to really build a multilateral framework of dialogue to advance women's rights. and if there's anything we need to learn from this anniversary, it's that we need to put the same ambition we had then back on a global agenda to fight emerging inequalities. because we know that the pandemic is reproducing some patterns that we thought we'd forgotten, either by escalating violence or forcing women to return to more reproductive tasks because of the pandemic. and that forces us to be more ambitious in our policy. in domestic policy, commissioner, we already know that we have an ambitious gender equality agenda and strategy to develop, but we cannot allow, as other members have said, that some member states still trample women's rights within the european union. we must be forceful about this and also be a powerful voice abroad, with that gender equality action plan that we have in the european union, because the european union has to intervene in any scenario where women's rights are being trampled on.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i'm not going to let you down. ladies and gentlemen! the 25th anniversary of the beijing declaration is an opportunity to take stock of progress towards gender equality and the protection of women's rights. so let us examine what it was really dedicated to. it was to draw attention to the problems of poverty, education, the situation of women in armed conflict, the issue of violence against women. twenty-five years later, let us look at how it works and how it has affected the situation of women in the european union. well, more than half a million women in the union have been subjected to female genital mutilation. we also still have a big pay gap when it comes to women's pay. fortunately, in poland, which is mentioned so often here, this gap is one of the smallest. yes, the wage gap in poland is one of the smallest in the european union. it's about women's rights. in the european union, however, young girls are still forced into marriages with adult men, which is a de facto pedophilia practice. so we have a lot to do in our common home, in europe.
mr president, gender equality is a basic prerequisite for world peace and development. the beijing declaration sets strategic goals for achieving equality in twelve key areas - milestones for women's issues. twenty-six years after the adoption of the beijing platform for action, no country has achieved equality for women and girls. important areas for improvement are women's access to work, reducing the gender pay gap, eliminating violence against women, and effectively safeguarding women's rights. the european union continues to lead the way in ensuring equal opportunities for women, equal access to all areas of social, political and economic life, and adequate representation in decision-making bodies. unfortunately, the economic crisis of the past decade and the current health adventure have exposed women to alarmingly high rates of violence, poverty and deprivation. the phenomena are sounding the alarm to governments and citizens not to undo the gains made in the last decades in the protection of women's rights. ladies and gentlemen, with an eye to the future generations, we have an obligation to make a decisive contribution by collective organized action to reducing inequalities and justifying the struggles of millions of women for the recognition of their rights in practice.
mr president, commissioner, what has been achieved in all these years of fighting for equality? well, the natural characteristics of women have been completely devalued. today fertility, motherhood, marital love, and family are viewed as negative. they sell us the freedom to be able to decide about our own bodies. we are promised a false autonomy that isolates us, a selfishness that turns against us. because we're not just one body. we are not a commodity, and neither is the baby we're keeping. the human being has a spiritual and transcendent dimension that you are not appreciating and that, in fact, is everything. this transcendence gives meaning to our lives and allows us to create bonds, relationships based on selfless love that open us to the generosity and solidarity of welcoming a new life: the wonderful gift of being parents of a unique, unrepeatable and unique human being from the very moment of conception. there's nothing more cruel than taking his life. each and every one of us is the fruit of the generosity of parents who have wanted to welcome us. how can we not give what we've received? we all long to have a family.
mr president, commissioner, this year marks the 25th anniversary of the beijing declaration and platform for action, to which the three previous un conferences also contributed, in particular the first world conference on women under the theme of equality, development and peace in 1975. we find that despite the gains made, there are still serious problems in the lives of women. they continue to be the main victims of precarious work, unemployment, rising inequalities, and violence in society, at work and in the family. we underline that sexual and reproductive health and rights are an integral part of the women's rights agenda and that it is essential to increase efforts to improve women's reproductive health and rights at european and global level. we reaffirm that the centrality of the impacts that the covid-19 pandemic is having on the downward spiral in women's lives should be a concern to be taken into account in the short-term challenges.
mr president, when i studied law at the university of coimbra in the 1970s, there was not a single woman on the faculty, today there are 32, but there is still only one woman at the top. when this parliament was first directly elected in 1979, the percentage of women was 15%, 15.2%, today it is 40.4% and now i have a commissioner on my right and a minister on my left. much progress has been made since 189 countries unanimously committed themselves to fighting for gender equality in beijing 25 years ago, but to date none of them has fully met their commitments, and there are many inequalities that need to be tackled. in europe, for example, 7 million women remain excluded from the labour market compared to 450 000 men because of unpaid care work, a situation aggravated by the crisis. as mrs clinton has already said, it is not enough to put rights on paper. women need to be empowered to fight for them in the public and private sectors. for example, here in the union, there is a directive to have more women on the boards of directors in non-executive positions, which is not a big deal. madam president, europe needs to continue to lead the climate agenda as it has done. but we must at the same time be able to ensure a level playing field for our companies against competitors from outside our economic bloc. and i ask you: can we accept that our businesses are being hurt by our climate ambition? because we want more? while others live with impunity in a climate dumping situation? our rules and our messages must be clear: protect the planet and promote a fair market for all. and for that, our companies cannot be put at a disadvantage for meeting our high environmental standards. the free market we believe in, the private enterprise we value and the environment we respect do not tolerate unfair competition. we must therefore also avoid the trick of moving companies to another country just to escape our ambitions and take advantage of the fact that they do not have to comply with stringent environmental standards. respect for the planet, promotion of industry and protection of jobs are objectives which are not contradictory! for our part, europe will comply with all of them!
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, administrative cooperation in the field of taxation is a dimension of european integration which can and should be more ambitious. each member state determines what taxes it collects, how much it collects and how it collects them. this fiscal sovereignty makes sense, but it is only fully achieved if we have the necessary instruments for fair, balanced and effective taxation. in a world increasingly globalised by digitalisation, and in a europe with an increasingly open single market, we need taxation adapted to today. challenges that are not of one country or another, but common, especially when we share a space like the european union. the commission's legislative package for fairer and simpler taxation, which this directive is the result of, is an important step, but it needs to be consistent. it is for the council of the union, which is chaired this semester by portugal, to decide and apparently it is preparing to ignore the contribution of this parliament. if the position was expected, it is still reprehensible. there is still time to reconsider, and that is the first appeal i leave here. i leave the commission, which i asked about this last week, with a second appeal: to work on a more thorough review of the directive. we decided this week on dac 7 and an 8th dac has already been announced. it's time to do some more detailed, steady work. successive legislative changes disrupt the work of tax administrations and create uncertainty for businesses. we have a lot to do: improve the system for exchanging information between tax authorities, increase resources for tax administrations, protect taxpayers' rights, avoid red tape for businesses and ensure data protection for all. the proposals we have adopted this week are in that direction, but we do not decide alone on everything. we hope to have that opportunity in the near future.
madam president, the internal market of the european union was built on the free movement of capital, but without the mechanisms necessary for this free movement of capital to be used to avoid taxation of large multinational companies in the digital sector and not only in the digital sector, generating a race to the bottom that is bad for citizens, but not bad for everyone, as is the fashion to say in these debates. it is good for these multinational companies and it is good for all territories, including member states which base their economic and public accounting models on the predation of tax revenues from others. citizens are fed up with this reality, which weakens public services and increases taxation of labour income. tax administrative cooperation and tax transparency must be a step towards creating minimum capital gains taxation rules within the european union, making transparent the race to the bottom we have seen in the european union and the world.

madam president, allow me to be completely clear. the european semester is old. really old. in spite of the objectives of the stability and growth pact, our experience from a decade ago shows that its rules are not fit for purpose. the extension of the general escape clause for one more year is, of course, good news, but it’s only a first step. the escape clause must not be withdrawn before the economy returns to its pre—crisis level. but before the stability and growth pact is in place again, it must be deeply revised. the economic landscape has changed and europe’s priorities have evolved. the european semester must do the same. the pandemic has resulted in a dramatic increase in public debt for every country. however, as interest rates are estimated to continue to be low for several years, its impact will be watered down. as such, the pace of reduction of public debt can, and must, be reduced, not forcing member states into a decade of austerity and economic depression. furthermore, we must also implement the so long—discussed golden rule for sustainable investment, and we need a more inclusive semester and macroeconomic rules, not just about numbers, but also about the people and the environment. we know what has to be done. let’s just do it together.

madam president, if the european response to the crisis is to reach citizens, businesses and states, then the partnerships between the european and national institutions must work well. right now, in the approval of the national recovery and resilience plans. the key word is urgency. the european parliament, in an interinstitutional framework, has set the objectives for the union, for green, digital, inclusive growth, for the empowerment of young people, but also for the implementation of reforms that can make public systems, in particular education, health and justice, better equipped to deal with global challenges. it is up to the member states, in open dialogue with the local authorities, the social partners and civil society, as my government has done, to decide on their policies and projects to make the best use of available european funding, on a basis of complementarity between next generation eu and the mff, for the benefit of their economies, the european economy and europeans.
madam president, national recovery and resilience plans are a pillar of the european economic recovery. the economic recession of 2020 and this first quarter of 2021, marked by lockdowns, call for urgent responses. the plans must be presented, discussed and approved quickly so that the funds can reach the real economy as soon as possible. to the companies that generate wealth, create value and multiply employment and to the families that find themselves unprotected and without income. for this reason, and in order to truly implement the principle of partnership with states and civil society, we need transparency. transparency in the decision-making process, but also throughout the implementation, because only with full access to information can we ensure that companies have access to opportunities and that citizens can assess the results. and the result we want is economic growth and job creation. if, on the one hand, we cannot waste resources or indulge in fraud or corruption, on the other hand, we cannot accept the concentration in the state of funds which belong to the europeans, to the people. it is essential that the money is invested in the economy and not used to finance the expenditure of public administrations or to feed the propaganda instruments of governments.
in writing. the european union has joined forces to ensure a timely response - a robust financial recovery tool for member states - to an unprecedented crisis. due to covid-19, businesses, workers, families, education, tourism and many other sectors have been affected and are in urgent need of support. national recovery and resilience plans should focus on social policies such as employment, education, skills and health, as well as structural and investment reforms that promote a green, socially inclusive transition to ensure added value. they should also improve the long-term competitiveness and sustainable growth prospects of member states, address the challenges and reap the benefits of the green and digital transitions and the sustainable development goals (sdgs). the level of ambition must be high, but never self-centred and uncoordinated from those that are the objectives of this plan: recovery and resilience are the key words. we will only achieve better indicators of social progress and we will only be stronger if we tackle this crisis with strategic investments based on social policy, cohesion and the specific needs of each country and region.
the european union is also committed to the development of a comprehensive and effective policy on the prevention of and control of the spread of the virus. unfortunately, these repressive measures are not new. over the past four years we have seen an escalation in repressive actions by the cambodian authorities, not only against political opponents but also against trade unions, human rights defenders, civil society organisations and media outlets. the country is moving in broad strokes towards a one-party regime, with laws and actions that limit or make it impossible to have political opposition and restrict individual freedoms in repeated violation of its international human rights obligations. this situation cannot be tolerated. the cambodian authorities must put an end to all forms of repression and politically motivated trials and release all political prisoners. but the european union must also send clear signals to the cambodian authorities that change is needed and that it must use all its resources to strengthen civil society.
mr president, i would like to thank you for this debate here today and express my satisfaction with the commission's communication on rural areas: we need a real programme for rural areas in europe, with a comprehensive approach. we must not look at agriculture in a one-sided way, or at tourism, or even at the environment: we must look at the whole of europe and look across the board to meet the needs of rural areas and the people of europe. if we don't, we're going to be wrong. this cannot be done without local actors, without the town halls and without the people who live in these rural areas. and let us not forget rural women: they must be involved and we must give them visibility, also in the debate on communication. we have a magnificent perch and an instrument such as the conference on the future of europe: let us use it, commissioner, to develop this debate.
mr president, commissioner, i thank my colleague, mr norbert lins, for the pertinent questions, but i am sure that we differ on the position on what role and what investments should be made in the rural world to fix people, to strengthen cohesion, to boost its economy and to regenerate biodiversity. and the common agricultural policy, as it is designed, continues to subsidize highly polluting and over-intensive industries, notably the livestock industry. as well as reinforcing the concentration of investments in large landlords. the cap should be redesigned to adapt to the social, economic and climate challenges of the 21st century, with a focus on more extensive, organic, local and plant-based production. and finally, speaking of the rural world, i cannot forget the bulls that are brutally slaughtered in the arenas of southern europe with the support of the common agricultural policy funds. it is time to put an end to the cruel industry of bullfighting and it is time to put an end to the common agricultural policy funds for this cruel industry.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the european union urgently needs a real rural agenda, an ambitious plan with very concrete objectives and the implementation of which can be monitored. i would say a real solidarity pact for rural areas. its timeliness and urgency are widely recognised, especially now, in the context of the formulation and implementation of the green deal, which will require so much of these rural regions and their people and economies. if we do not act quickly and with strong involvement of the municipalities and regions, we risk having large parts of europe irreversibly abandoned, demographically depressed and economically deficient, especially in local services and in the capacity to create jobs. mr president, let us not miss this opportunity to set an aggregate agenda for the many policies and, i stress, the new reform of the common agricultural policy, the great digital transformation, which cannot ignore these regional disparities between rural and urban areas, a strategy with a decisive direct impact on the vitality of these areas and which will allow us to align our macroeconomic objectives with their specificities. i sincerely hope that this vision will develop with realism and solidarity, especially because in our territorial future, in this union of peoples and states, there can be no europeans of the first rank and europeans of the second.
in writing. regarding the eu initiative 'long-term vision for rural areas', it should be stressed that the rural fabric of many member states, such as portugal, has suffered continuously from eu policies, especially the common agricultural policy, and the result is a depopulated hinterland, with no infrastructure, with weakened public services and far from people and with weakened economic activities. we have seen for decades the abandonment of the countryside and the rural world, the victim of the constant and deliberate divestment and alienation of successive governments, with complete disregard for the resident populations and disregard for territorial cohesion. if this long-term vision means that once again agribusiness is favoured over small farmers and their organic farming practices, or that public services are disinvested, closed or privatised, then we are dealing with another act of propaganda. and the rural areas don't need that! defending rural areas requires jobs, public services, public investment. it calls for a different agroforestry policy, a cap compatible with family farming and the rural world of the mini-fund and it calls for fair production prices! it requires effective regional development which takes account of existing inequalities and which cannot be exacerbated.
in writing. the digital green certificate is an important instrument for the recovery of the economy and tourism, with the aim of facilitating the free movement of citizens, while ensuring all safety conditions. there is no doubt about the urgency of common approaches to enable the long-awaited recovery, but beware that haste is the enemy of perfection. there are fundamental premises which must be respected with this instrument. firstly, the instrument must facilitate mobility. secondly, this instrument cannot replace any of the other measures in place to combat, prevent and contain the pandemic, but rather complement them. thirdly, the instrument cannot be discriminatory and create first-class and second-class citizens, because vaccination is not yet optional and should not be a precondition for the exercise of free movement. finally, it cannot create more stress for those working in the pandemic and who have been on the frontline from the beginning, namely healthcare professionals and border workers. at the next european council i call for a common and coordinated approach, because the lessons of the past tell us that this is the way forward.

the progress of the european project is accompanied by new financing needs, which must not fall on the finances of the member states in such a way as to burden them beyond what is reasonable. the scale of the covid-19 pandemic recovery financial package, the next generation eu, has precipitated the historic option for joint debt issuance. but if the payment of this debt is not to weigh heavily on future generations, new sources of revenue and new resources must be created. the proposals on the table for three new rates are bold but essential. i find it very serious that several member states have not yet ratified the text creating these new and much needed resources. the commission shall, by means of implementing acts, lay down the rules on the application of this regulation and the rules on the application of the delegated and implementing acts referred to in article 5 of regulation (eu) no 1290/2013, including the rules on the application of the delegated and implementing acts referred to in article 5 of regulation (eu) no 1290/2013, and the rules on the application of the delegated and implementing acts referred to in article 5 of regulation (eu) no 1290/2013.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, economic and monetary union is one of the main achievements of european integration, but it is still an incomplete reality. on the banking union side, we still have to establish the european deposit insurance system, and on the capital market union side, we still have more to do, in areas ranging from taxation to supervision to insolvency. if the first case lacks realism, the second lacks pragmatism. capital markets can and should play an important role in europe's economic recovery. the investment deficit we face will not be solved by european funds or expansionary fiscal policies alone. we need private investment to create value, create jobs, innovate in the market, increase the competitiveness of our companies and increase household incomes. the measures we are taking in response to the pandemic crisis are circumstantial, but we will have to make structural decisions. we need to increase access to capital markets for smes, protect individual investors, improve financial literacy for citizens, fight fraud with better supervision and protect competition with smarter taxation. europe needs more investment and we are in a position to boost that growth. let's have the courage to do it.
in writing. strengthening the eu-africa partnership is essential, especially in the context of the global covid-19 pandemic. it has widened inequality and introduced huge setbacks to the commitments made in the attempt to create a new partnership anchored in mutual responsibility for problem solving as well as sustainable and inclusive development of the two continents. europe has prioritised multilateral and partnership relations with africa and successive portuguese presidencies of the council of the eu have contributed greatly to this objective. this partnership has all the conditions to pursue these objectives at political, economic, social and environmental level. in implementing the eu-africa strategy, we now have a solution-oriented approach that puts people at the centre, notably in the provision of universal quality health care and education, the creation of decent jobs, the fight against poverty, the fight against inequality, addressing infrastructure deficits and other issues such as the gaps in international organisations that push away the opportunities needed.
madam president! data is the foundation of digital transformation and innovation. they are the basis for the creation of new products and services and the development of artificial intelligence. their availability and open and secure international exchange can be a key added value and a breakthrough in building an autonomous european digital economy and contribute to improving health, living conditions and the environment. however, it should be borne in mind that ensuring better access to and exchange of data should be done while guaranteeing high standards of personal data protection and cybersecurity. i would like to pay particular attention to the cybersecurity aspect, because this is a serious challenge that we will have to face.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) on the common organization of the market in bananas (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). the regulation has proved its worth. it ensures better protection of personal data and a wider awareness among the public and sets the standards worldwide. thousands of companies have so far done their best to fully comply with the gdpr. they've also made the necessary investments. i would like to make three comments today on our data policy. firstly, we must not be blind to the shortcomings and problems which have become apparent in recent years. we must recognise that the gdpr is time-consuming and expensive for smes and everyday associations. in addition, the application and enforcement of the rules are not uniform in all member states. that is a disruption of the proper functioning of the internal market. secondly, we must not impede the exchange of data with third countries. adequacy decisions with third countries are indeed crucial to enable companies to exchange data with each other in a smooth and gdpr-compliant manner. third and lastly, there are the new technologies, which are often based on artificial intelligence and require large amounts of data to work properly. the gdpr must not be an obstacle to the european union in the global race for innovative strategic technologies. i therefore join my group in calling on the commission to fully assess the impact of the gdpr on these new technologies in the coming months and years and to draw up a plan to enable them to continue to flourish and develop in the future.

in writing. – the data strategy for europe is extremely important. we cannot compete with china and the united states if we are not able to create a common european data pool. if we continue with relatively small data sets, we will not be able to create new innovative products as quickly as our competitors. ensuring access to data is crucial for our businesses’ innovation and growth. we need to boost data sharing and the free flow of data within the european union, but this cannot come about as a protectionist measure. we need to extend the data flows also between the union and third countries, provided that privacy and security requirements are met. further, i would like to highlight the rise of edge computing. as said in the commission’s communication today 80% of the processing of data takes place in the cloud and 20% in smart connected objects. the share of edge computing will increase rapidly and it will be 80% by 2025. therefore, we need to make sure that there are technical and legal solutions in place for providing effective access to data on the edge. we also need to address the emerging cybersecurity and data interoperability challenges the distributed nature of edge computing brings along.
in writing. data is crucial for economic growth, sustainable development and innovation in europe. the covid-19 pandemic has further highlighted the role and need for high-quality data sharing. our task is therefore to provide european standards that keep pace with technological developments and help companies, especially smes, to thrive in the digital age. the creation of a single european data space therefore appears to be crucial. we should be focusing on consumer safety while giving businesses access to important data. interoperability of data and cooperation between member states should also be priorities. i hope that the new strategy will ensure effective, responsible and secure access to data that creates real added value for citizens and businesses across the european union. so we have a huge opportunity and potential in europe and it is time to seize it.
in writing. the advance of digitalisation is transforming the economy and people's daily lives at an ever-increasing and accelerating pace. one of the main impacts of this is the rapid growth of data traffic, which is doubling globally on average every year and a half. in order to control the process and avoid vulnerability to technology companies, appropriate policy objectives and legislative tools are needed, which should take into account three aspects. the new digital data-driven economy should aim to improve the quality of life of european citizens. the digital shift should benefit all workers, entrepreneurs and local communities. the establishment of common european data spaces could be an appropriate tool for this. it is also of paramount importance that micro, small and medium-sized enterprises receive appropriate legal and technical support in relation to digitalisation. smes account for around 100 million jobs and more than 50% of gdp in the european union. maintaining their competitiveness is an essential condition for the success of the digital transition. finally, data-driven technologies should also contribute to the achievement of climate objectives. the it sector has significant potential in this area. the digital transition, if implemented with the right speed and ambition, can greatly improve the chances of achieving climate neutrality by 2050.
the european data strategy does not address the fundamental question of the ownership of 'data', which should always be that of those who originated it in the first place (usually citizens in their interactions with the machine) and not of the actors who collected and classified it. this is the fundamental question for ai, in its transformation of workforce experience into fixed capital, and one that the eu insists on not addressing. in addition, the issue of cloud data federation (interconnection), the creation of so-called common european data spaces, interoperability and data sharing in the eu can contribute to the even greater concentration of data collection, storage and processing, for the benefit not only of peoples and states, but also of large multinationals in various sectors, not only in the digital domain. these aspects should be integrated into a vision of sovereign development, aiming at the preservation and safeguarding of data at the national level, and in a data management that includes both a public dimension and a national interest. the example of a common european health data area will obviously serve private entities operating in the sector, in particular in the insurance sector.

on behalf of the left group. mr president, although there is talk of a new programme, horizon europe still has its old problems. it is still based on competitiveness, glorifying excellence and therefore maintaining the logic of promoting those who are already able to carry out research and development activities, exacerbating existing inequalities and asymmetries. we advocate a shared management system, defining and reserving national envelopes whose total amount should not be less than 50% of the overall allocations, thus providing for a balanced distribution of resources between member states with a view to reducing inequalities, capacities and r & d activities. the programme continues to fail to address the precariousness of employment felt by thousands upon thousands of researchers, some for decades. we need to enshrine their labour rights as the value of careers, guaranteeing stability and protection in sickness, in maternity, in unemployment, which is always on the horizon with the end of a project, a contract or a scholarship. these rights, although contained in the european charter for researchers and the code of conduct for the recruitment of researchers, exist only in the documents of the european union and without scientists there is no science.
mr president, i have often heard scientists complain that they waste time preparing applications that are too bureaucratic. worse still, they have shown me that applications for european funds, such as horizon, are much simpler than those for regional funds. moreover, synergies between the various funds are being wasted. a european fund application that is well assessed but not approved should have access to a green path to obtain regional funds. these are small, big projects that can be solved with a little technology and a lot of political determination, avoiding duplication of work and wasting precious time on innovation and knowledge production. commissioner, this crisis has shown the importance of science in meeting the challenges of the present and the future. we need to give back to scientists, valuing their careers and their time working for research. not only will they, but we all will.
mr president, the protection of citizens and the environment is a matter for the member states. however, in the event of a major event, the european union is able to assist the affected populations and regions through this mechanism in its spirit of solidarity and cooperation. the pandemic has revealed that, while crisis-prepared, the european union needs to strengthen its capacity to respond to any kind of adversity that threatens our health and our environment. a strengthening of this budget and this proposal will allow for more planning in prevention and preparedness at eu level with cross-sectoral and cross-border responses, for better disaster risk management combined with a strengthening of the strategic reserves of resources and better networking with civil protection authorities, regional and national. the eu is thus more prepared and more united. a special thanks to the rapporteur and to all the work here in the european parliament.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to express my full support for the proposal put forward by mr giuseppe ferrandino. i am grateful for the work that the negotiating teams of the three institutions have done to reach an agreement which i believe is balanced and fair. i regret that the council has not endorsed it. what is at stake, from the point of view of parliament, is to defend small-scale fishing. it is carried out by vessels that have access to limited resources due to their autonomy and their area of operation. around europe, coastal communities are dependent on the income of these fleets. we must ensure that migratory resources such as bluefin tuna are accessible to small-scale vessels and for that it is necessary to make it compulsory to allocate a part of the available fishing opportunities to this fleet. this is what parliament wants to achieve, recognising the importance of small-scale fishing and the importance it has had in restoring the conservation status of this resource.
mr president, commissioner, madam secretary of state, we support this agreement. the situation would be much worse if he didn't exist. but its implementation will have to be followed very closely and firmly by the european union. in these four months, difficulties have already been felt. fisheries was at the heart of many of the brexit falsehoods and was one of the hardest points to negotiate. the agreement contains a set of rules that will apply progressively until june 2026. it is imperative to ensure that the agreed rules are respected. there are signs that this is being made more difficult by the united kingdom, particularly as regards the allocation of fishing opportunities. fisheries companies need predictability in their operations. we cannot be entering may without knowing how much we can catch by the end of the year. the price of fish is not constant throughout the year. if there is no predictability, there can be no guarantee of economic stability for companies. the fishing industry is facing the perfect storm between the uncertainty of brexit and the consequences of the covid-19 pandemic. we therefore also have an obligation to ensure that at least what we have agreed with the united kingdom is scrupulously respected by both sides and i urge the commission to ensure that this happens at all times.
