madam president, the egf has financed active measures such as training, job search and the creation of businesses in the event of unexpected redundancies, which have been promoted in the context of the globalisation process or the economic and financial crisis. we support the improvements introduced during the negotiation process, in particular the broadening of the objectives, the reduction of the dismissal threshold to 200 workers and the extension of the reference period when this number is not reached , the co-financing rate or the simplification of the application procedure. these improvements, however, lack the appropriate budgetary allocation. and despite the improvements, this fund is still little more than a palliative, dissociated from any action on the structural causes of business relocations and capital flight that cause unemployment and social exclusion. workers can count on our firm opposition to policies which encourage the relocation of companies and on our solidarity in the fight to preserve their jobs, in the regulation and promotion of work.
in writing. in 2019, tourism and its relationship with other sectors of the economy accounted for around 10% of wealth generated in the european union (eu). the regions that rely most on this sector, such as the outermost regions, have been particularly affected by the economic and social crisis generated by the covid-19 pandemic. with the approach of the summer season we must ensure all the conditions for the safety of travellers and the operation of the sector in the short term. the european covid-19 certificate will be an essential tool in the process of standardising and facilitating measures to allow the free movement of citizens. member states will have a key role in implementing the measures and providing reliable and up-to-date information on the standards in force. we cannot yet forget that this crisis will go far beyond this summer. to provide a medium- and long-term response to our citizens, the eu needs to undertake a european policy for tourism, with a specific heading in the community budget. only in this way will it be possible to effectively support enterprises, in particular micro, small and medium-sized enterprises, in helping to economic recovery and the green and digital transition.
mr president, commissioner, today commerce is increasingly online, advertising is more personalised, products are more connected, from a simple doll to a smartwatch. they change throughout their life cycle as a function of software updates. the rules of supervision to ensure consumer safety must therefore be different. but good rules in the official newspapers are not enough. without properly trained authorities to apply them, they will be of little value. and for regulators to respond appropriately to what's changed in trade and in producers, we have to invest in the skills of the people who work there and bet on using new technologies like blockchain to track products, artificial intelligence to determine risk zones. so, among other aspects of this excellent programme, i welcome the financial support of eur 105 million for the supervisory authorities of the member states, and hope that they will make good use of it in the name of strengthening the internal market and the security of all of us.
madam president, commissioner, secretary of state, ladies and gentlemen, i come from madeira, an outermost region, and currently when i go home i have to go through three different types of procedures here in the eu. this certificate is a measure that should facilitate the free movement of citizens through the recognition of the vaccine, negative test or recovery of infection and thus complement the infection control measures already implemented. this is a necessary bet to accelerate economic recovery, freedom of movement, combined with pandemic control. the eu must take this step, but with caution. we cannot have a certificate which creates inequalities or discrimination for citizens or exposes their data or increases bureaucracy. on the contrary, i did. we have here an opportunity for the european union to show that it can have a coordinated and adequate response, which will facilitate and protect the lives of its citizens.
madam president, as we move forward in the vaccination process, we must recover freedom of movement, an indispensable step for post-pandemic economic recovery and restoring citizens' confidence. the proposal for a regulation on the digital green certificate, as amended by the european parliament, is designed to facilitate free movement, make travel procedures more predictable, ensure effective coordination between states, ensure the protection of personal data and respect for privacy, prevent discrimination and ensure that this instrument is not used indefinitely or for purposes other than those specifically defined. these are some of our concerns and these are the proposals we have put forward. yes, it is time to restore free movement with a reliable instrument capable of keeping pace with the evolution of knowledge and the solutions that science puts at our disposal. and, yes, in this matter too, we will leave no one behind. we have done our part, the council must do its part.
madam president, the digitisation of the economy is an inevitable and irreversible process, and that is a good thing. opportunities are manifold, but they do not come without challenges. digitalization brings the dematerialization of many activities. digital companies operate in our internal market, compete with european companies and generate their profits here. and if they do so in europe, they must also pay their taxes in europe. we have to balance the competition of the digital supply with the physical supply. to do this, we need to determine what taxation we want for digital companies, where they pay their taxes and how much they should be taxed. in a globalised market, multilateral solutions are required. trade wars cannot be new tax wars, and that is why we need a global agreement on the tax relationship of a given company with particular countries, and we need an effective minimum rate of corporate tax that ensures fair and honest competition, but is also a rate that safeguards the competitiveness of our companies, that stimulates innovation in smes and that ensures the rights of taxpayers. i have said it in the past and i will say it again: if an agreement cannot be reached, the union must be prepared to come up with its own solutions. a good agreement is the first step towards freeing european businesses from the tax burdens that are suffocating our economies, and it is with european businesses that we must build a decade of recovery and growth.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, today there is growing consensus that we need to ensure fair taxation where the digital giants are also held accountable. we need fair taxes. there's a recovery to fund, and we're aware that the digital giants have won, and they've won a lot, in the last year. we cannot ignore the biden administration's contribution to this issue, in particular the proposed 21% minimum corporate tax rate. today, even the unsuspecting imf accepts and recommends measures like this to finance the crisis. today, this parliament is in a position to adopt a text that is on the right track, telling the european states that there are no excuses for not doing what is necessary, a fair taxation of the digital giants and the big multinationals. citizens expect tax justice. the time has come to live up to that expectation.
madam president, in the face of mr putin's numerous provocative acts, we cannot continue to limit ourselves to expressing our deep concern. it must be made clear once and for all that if anything happens to navalny, putin will be held responsible, that actions to destabilise the euro-atlantic region will not go unanswered, especially if they harm the territory of the union, as happened with the czech republic. military exercises which do not respect the principles laid down in the osce framework, which do not respect sovereignty, territorial integrity and transparency, as was the case with the manoeuvres in the black sea or, more recently, with the military exercises on the border with ukraine, will not be tolerated. the commitments in the helsinki final act and the minsk agreements are to be fulfilled. i agree with the high representative when he says that we must speak with one voice once and for all. and i add: we must speak with one voice, we must pursue the interests of the russian oligarchs and we must rethink nord stream 2.
madam president, beijing will not silence us. it will not silence us in denouncing arbitrary detentions, forced labour, the death penalty, the denial of freedom of expression or association or forced abortion in xinjiang and tibet. it will not silence us in denouncing the imposition of the national security law and the violation of rights in hong kong, and the imposition of the directive of patriotism to china in macau, undermining the freedom of the press enshrined in the basic law agreed with portugal and in force in the territory until 2049. human rights are universal, indivisible, and not a matter of domestic policy alone. beijing has to understand this! and we will not be silent in the face of provocative actions in the south china sea and off taiwan. the sanctions imposed on citizens, institutions, members of this parliament and on the european parliament's subcommittee on human rights itself are a flagrant attack on democracy. we will not bow, we will not capitulate! the investment agreement with china will have our full opposition until sanctions are lifted. china is an important partner, but our values are not for sale. to talk to us about trade, you'll have to talk about human rights. we will not let a dictatorship dictate the rules!
madam president, the chinese counter—sanctions are an overreaction that this house cannot accept. it is by no means comparable to put sanctions against public officials involved in human rights violations, and to put sanctions against elected representatives for our opinions and our defence of human rights, which are universal. this is a complete disregard for freedom of opinion and expression, and a lack of respect for the task of us lawmakers, and even for our voters. we are friends of the chinese people; we wish them well, we praise their culture and heritage, and we commend their remarkable achievements over the last years in terms of economic and social progress. but by being vocal against human rights violations in china, and indeed everywhere they happen, we are simply upholding human dignity, which no government is allowed to degrade. we will keep doing so regardless of any sanctions. finally, a word on the agreement on investment. the eu and china need to cooperate on economic and other kinds of matters – sure, of course. but tell me, how are we supposed to give our consent to this agreement, while fellow members are being sanctioned? first, drop the sanctions on us, and then we talk. only then can we work on a better agreement: fairer for our businesses and fairer for the rights of workers.
madam president, we should use the debate today to send a clear political message that would have economic implications. the common argument that we should look separately at trade and human rights cannot be used anymore after the latest events. chinese assertive actions targeted our institutions in the most direct way. we as the european union should be leading the fight when it comes to human rights protection and democracy, but also when it comes to defending european values. therefore, i do support the position that we should halt all the talks on the cai as long as there are sanctions imposed against our colleagues. we should be proactive rule setters, not a mere reactor. this is also a reminder to member states that still haven’t created investment screening mechanisms. while we negotiated with china on the rules on how we trade with goods, we should never trade with our values.
madam president, i cannot help but begin by expressing my solidarity with my colleagues in the european parliament who are being politically targeted by the chinese authorities for defending democratic values. if there's one thing that unites us in this house, it's freedom and democracy. and speaking of freedom and democracy, we cannot ignore that the red carpet that the european commission and the portuguese presidency of the council of the european union are giving to the autocratic communist regime by forcing the conclusion of the investment agreement with china validates the occupation of tibet, the siege of hong kong and taiwan, the persecution of journalists and the constant violation of human rights in the country. moreover, to think that a trade agreement with xi jinping will guarantee more freedoms and openness to democratic values is to fail to understand that china's entry into the world trade organization in 2001 did the exact opposite: it validated, in the eyes of the international community, the dictatorial regime and opened the doors of the markets to the expansionist and communist strategy of the chinese communist regime. i end with a message of hope and support for all chinese democrats fighting for a free country: your day will come!
mr president, 1964 is the year of the creation of the farc and the beginning of a long conflict, the longest of the armed conflicts in latin america, which has damaged the image of colombia, its people and its economy. let us not forget what we are discussing here: the fifth anniversary of a peace agreement which is a historic achievement, a blessing for the colombians as well as for the entire region. it would be unreasonable to expect that after decades of conflict dividing colombians, everything will run smoothly. the truth is that the agreement has withstood the natural difficulties of the process, and even serious crises such as the one we are experiencing and the venezuelan refugee crisis. colombia has responded to all this, and we should leave our praise and thanks here. nevertheless, it is important to draw attention to the need for greater efforts to better integrate communities that have lived for many years on the margins of colombian society, to respect human rights, to invest in people and infrastructure, including digital, so that inequalities are not the final blow in a peace process that has been so resistant so far.
mr president, in 2016, with the peace agreement between the colombian government and the farc, we all wanted to start a phase of progress and reconciliation. unfortunately, despite some progress, we are still far from the targets set. in these five years, more than 440 human rights defenders have been murdered. in 2020 alone, more than 240 people were killed in 61 massacres under the cover of unacceptable impunity. seven million colombians still suffer from food insecurity. the european union has made a strong contribution to humanitarian aid, but all that support will always be inadequate if we do not get to the root of the problems, if we do not tackle inequalities and accelerate the implementation of the peace agreement in terms of transitional justice, rural reform, the reintegration of ex-combatants, the fight against crime, and if we do not guarantee the safety of human rights activists, community leaders, ex-combatants and environmentalists. we have to go further!
mr president, today’s discussion on india comes at a very dark time for the country facing the biggest numbers of covid infections: more than 300 000 per day over the last few days. i’m very glad that the european union has reacted quickly and, via the eu civil protection mechanism, has made shipments of urgently needed oxygen, medicine, and equipment available. i would like to thank those member states who have worked within this mechanism, and there are others who have helped bilaterally. today’s debate was designed to be ahead of the planned leaders’ meeting between the eu and india in porto. it has been cancelled due to the surge of infections in india, however, topics on the bilateral agenda remain valid. the epp group believes that the bilateral relationship requires a constant improvement and deepening. we have by no means achieved our full potential. india will be, in a couple of years, the most populous country in the world. its rise since the start of economic reform at the beginning of the 1990s has been spectacular. our trade in the last decade has increased by 70%. the eu is ready to launch negotiations on the stand—alone investment protection agreement, which would increase legal certainty for investors on both sides. as a friend, i say we can also address some issues of human rights where we see negative tendencies. india prides itself on being a country that treats human rights with respect and care, but i think one aspect is important: the citizenship amendment act. i have studied it. i think it is about temporary privilege for some, but not for discrimination for others, so we should not criticise from this angle but look at other issues that are at stake.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. of course we are supporting india in this current crisis, but i think we need to do more than we have done so far. so, thank you to all who have helped so far. but i think we need to increase the supply of medical supplies and vaccines to india as well. but apart from the present situation, we must also look at how we are going to develop our economic relations in the long term. we've been negotiating a trade agreement for years, with no significant success. and that is why, commissioner, we may have to consider whether the mandate of these trade negotiations is still valid. because it's now clear that we have some areas of disagreement with our partner india. so i think we really need to change the mandate - where we want to focus - really. and one area is certainly investment protection. india has, as you know, repealed the old isds rules, which i very much welcome. they have the right to do so, they were faster and better than the european union. but now we have also moved to throw these private law disputes into the dustbin of history and want, if anything, a judicial situation. and i think we can negotiate very carefully about investment protection. in addition to the question of tariffs, in addition to the question of intellectual property, there is of course also the question of sustainability. i believe that we cannot conclude an agreement - an investment agreement or a trade agreement - if we do not guarantee that workers' rights are respected, that environmental standards are respected, that the paris agreement on climate change is implemented and, above all, that there are enforcement possibilities. and that, i think, is the lesson of our trade agreements so far: we need better and stronger enforcement capabilities, and we need to agree that with india.
mr. speaker, first of all, i would like to express my full support to the people of india in the ordeal they are going through in this covid-19 crisis. i, in turn, welcome the european union's provision of medicines through the civil protection mechanism. this shows that the eu is a trusted partner for india and a friend in times of trouble. the choice of prime minister antonio cósta to organise this euro-indian summit, even virtual, on 8 may is therefore excellent. we must enter a new phase in relations with delhi, relaunch strategic partnerships with a strong parliamentary dimension, promote an open and demanding dialogue on respect for human rights and the role of civil society, work towards an investment protection agreement, act together against climate change. the geopolitical balances in the world and in asia are shifting and a strong alliance between europe and india is fundamental. we don't have to agree on everything to agree on the essentials.
mr president, as a portuguese and a communist, i cannot help mentioning that this year marks the 60th anniversary of the liberation of goa, daman and diu from portuguese colonialism. it is an event that reminds us that international relations must be guided by principles such as respect for sovereignty and independence, the development of mutually beneficial economic, scientific, social and cultural relations, principles which must guide the relations between each of the different countries that make up the european union and india. this means rejecting policies aimed at imposing relations of domination and dependence, the primacy of the interests of financial and economic groups over the rights of workers and peoples, and confrontation at the international level. i would like to take this opportunity to greet all those who, whether in the countries of the european union or in india, are fighting for their legitimate rights to work with rights, to produce, to a fair and decent income, to health, to education, to social progress.
on behalf of the left group. madam president, the covid-19 containment measures have exposed the precarious situation in which millions of children live and shown the importance of universal and free access to care and services. the child guarantee may be a response to the problems of children in poverty or social exclusion, but we cannot forget the structural causes of this scourge. we cannot erase the enormous responsibility of the european institutions and their policies which have thrown thousands of workers into poverty, dragging their families and children into poverty, cuts in wages, pensions and social benefits, closure of essential public services, especially in rural areas, underfunding of national health and education systems or the role of country-specific recommendations which, in the name of the holy grail of the debt and market deficit, have sought to deepen economic, social and territorial inequalities. the best thing in the world is children. children's rights are defended through public policies that address their needs in a multidimensional way. they include the right to universal, free and quality education from early childhood, the right to universal, free and quality health services, the right to housing, the right to play, access to sports, culture, civic participation, to live in an environment of peace and solidarity, and the right to have parents and carers with work and decent pay and time to devote to their children, our future. child support is one answer, but it's not the answer, so let's not get our hopes up.
madam president, commissioner, madam secretary of state, the harsh reality of 18 million children at risk of poverty is eroding the very foundations of the union. i therefore support the proposal for a child guarantee which is due to the persistence of the s&d. it contains positive aspects, but it is at the same time disappointing because it is not binding, unlike, for example, the 5% of the european social fund which will have to be channelled to programmes to combat child poverty in the most affected member states. i would also like to highlight a structural problem that could jeopardise the effectiveness of the guarantee: the lack of accurate data on child poverty figures in most member states. without quality data it will be difficult to monitor and evaluate the implementation of the child guarantee. i therefore call on the commission to mobilise resources to support eurostat and the member states in collecting and processing data of a multinational nature. without good information there will be no good implementation and that is what the children of europe are demanding of us, a paradigm shift.
author. mr president, all of us around the world have seen our economies affected by the pandemic crisis we are experiencing, our routines disrupted, our public services challenged, our fears accelerated, the fear of illness, the fear of losing our jobs or our businesses. but the crisis was not the same for everyone. where inequality was greater, where jobs were more precarious, where public services were more fragile, where governments preferred to deny scientific evidence rather than do what was within their power, the pandemic was more punishing, took more lives and pushed some who had just emerged from it back into poverty. unfortunately, this is the case in many latin american countries, which are home to 8.4% of the world's population, but which have already recorded 25% of all deaths. in this resolution we call for greater help and attention from the european union to this region with which we have so many historical ties. this aid is needed now to support the purchase, production and distribution of vaccines, to combat disinformation. we call for a closer look also at the rights of women, who were in more precarious jobs, who saw domestic violence increase, who were left without access to the already deficient sexual and reproductive health, and yet that we look now at recovery and resilience in debt relief, in deepening science and innovation partnerships, for example in horizon europe research projects or in the creation of a new transnational institute for infectious diseases. we ask for latin america what we know is needed in the european union, knowing that the lack of aid to this region will prevent it from making progress in its peace processes, reducing inequalities and keeping up with the challenges of the future, the digital and climate transitions.
mr president, these debates on vast regions of the world always run the risk of mixing very different realities, and when we look at the covid-19 pandemic in latin america, although the situation is generally dramatic, we must distinguish cases. the worst of these cases is brazil, where more than 395,000 people have died. it is the worst case and the most difficult to answer because it is rooted in bolsonaro's most irrational denialism and the fact that he did everything to prevent the population from being vaccinated. it's not a mistake or bad luck, it's deliberate irresponsibility. time and the people will judge him. this is the time to call for the support of the european union and its member states to brazil and the whole region, through our civil protection mechanism, vaccination campaigns, combating disinformation, and ensuring that the poorest countries are better able to manage their debts. we cannot allow the irresponsibility of a few to jeopardize the future of all.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, covid-19 has not left any country indifferent to the force of a virus destroying health systems, and, as has already been said, the countries of latin america have been among the most affected. in this region, the pandemic has further exacerbated social and economic inequalities and exposed the fragility of health systems. this parliament shows its full solidarity with the region, but more must be done and the european union can do more. it is necessary to continue cooperating with latin american countries to face covid-19 with an active and present posture. we need to bet on a massive vaccination campaign. the need to increase production and distribution capacity under the covax initiative is all too evident. vaccines should be considered a global public good because no one is safe until everyone is safe, and that means until we are all vaccinated.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, contrary to what some may imply, it is not easy to resolve a situation that begins with a turbulent electoral process and opaque transfers of power, but which, fortunately, bolivia seems to have overcome, as the 2020 presidential elections and the recent regional elections have shown us. it is not enough, however, that the electoral process should proceed normally, all political forces must respect democratic rules and the bolivian government must ensure that the rule of law is respected, as it is its responsibility, by guaranteeing access to justice and the opportunity for defence, particularly when the accusations in question are of a political nature. political peace and stability of the bolivian regime are values to which we must commit ourselves. i therefore call on the external action service and all member states to redouble their efforts to support the dialogue between the parties in bolivia to ensure that this happens.
mr president, bolivia is still experiencing a delicate political and social situation, but it is essential that, after last year's elections, all national and international actors can contribute to a rapid return to democratic normality. the acts of violence that occurred in 2019 should be analyzed and judged within the parameters of an independent judicial system and without any pressure from the political power. the complete independence of the authorities is a guarantee of the rule of law which must be respected in order to be able to lead bolivia back to the necessary reconciliation of its society. political and social polarization is a dangerous spark that will inevitably lead to serious divisions and discredit the institutions. i therefore call on all bolivians to respect the powers that have been established and on the european union and its member states to support this country in a process of constructive and reconciliatory internal dialogue.
the so-called blasphemy laws carry with them a shocking level of intolerance and disregard for freedom of expression, belief and thought. the case of the arrest and sentencing to death of the couple shagufta kausar and shafqat emmanuel is an extreme example of what has been going on in pakistan for years. other cases confirm the violation of freedom of thought, the persecution of religious minorities and other forms of discrimination in this country. i express my disapproval of all this and call for the swift abolition of the death penalty, the immediate elimination of the blasphemy law and the release of all those still imprisoned because of its application. it is essential that the european union, through its delegation in pakistan, continue to support non-governmental organisations in pakistan that provide assistance to victims of religious violence and human rights activists, and that trade relations with pakistan be accompanied by an effective human rights dialogue.
on behalf of the ecr group. madam president, energy is a basic commodity which must be affordable for everyone. we in our group have worked to make that happen. however, this strategy will increase the price of electricity, making it difficult for our industry to be viable and leading households to suffer from dramatic energy poverty. all for imposing capricious goals to advance their green ideological agenda. the union's mission should be to ensure an energy infrastructure with sufficient capacity to enable member states to choose an energy mix that ensures their stable supply, without depending on third countries. this strategy of alleged integration, however, excludes sources such as nuclear energy, an autonomous source, with on-demand and affordable production, which has reduced co2 emissions by more than sixty gigatons. if this document is adopted tomorrow, be honest and, when the price of electricity in your countries doubles, confess to your voters that it was thanks to your vote in favour. believe me, i'll remember it in my country.
madam president, it is essential that the european commission puts greater emphasis and financial support to hydrogen as an excellent zero-emissions alternative to traditional fuels. i think it’s very unfortunate that when it comes to infrastructure and filling stations, the eu is way behind in comparison to other developed parts of the world. the joint undertaking for fuel cells and hydrogen and its further support is crucial but they can’t do it alone. despite the fact that technical neutrality is the official policy, if you look back, the commission was way more open and supportive when it comes to battery technology and electric vehicles. i think the time has come that hydrogen gets a similar boost from the commission in order to create a solid basis for the future european hydrogen market. electric vehicles have already planted their foot firmly, now it’s time that hydrogen and fuel cells do the same. i cannot imagine that the green transition can be achieved, especially in freight and long-haul transport, without hydrogen and fuel cells.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the strategy for the integration of energy systems is a strategy which the european institutions have decided to adopt in order to achieve the objectives we have set ourselves with the so-called green deal. challenging objectives, but objectives that are within the reach of a europe which is the area of the world that has accepted these challenges the most: sustainability challenges balanced with economic development challenges, accessible to all; so don't challenge them to contradict each other. we sometimes take that risk. how can you avoid this risk? how can we prevent the green deal from being the grave of development but the cradle of development? the principle of reality must prevail, and it is right to take energy efficiency as a major pillar, but energy efficiency cannot be separated from cost efficiency: energy costs must be affordable for citizens and businesses. the hydrogen challenge must be a challenge in which the great goal of green hydrogen is reached through intermediate stages. this realism is our task. this is why the european people's party talks about "technological neutrality": we can't fall in love with individual technological solutions. the enterprise system builds the integration paradigm through a balanced relationship between the various solutions: we can't impose one!
madam president, commissioner, combating climate change is not just a challenge of civilisation and sustainability. it is an opportunity to equip the energy union with a renewable energy-based system that makes a decisive contribution to carbon neutrality, supports the competitiveness of the european economy and is fair and effective in eradicating energy poverty. the reports we are discussing here are a strong contribution by the european parliament to the successful implementation of the climate law and its objectives. delivering on energy system integration and hydrogen strategies requires a clear and long-term vision and reporting framework, a just transition and an ambitious focus on scientific development and technological innovation, and on the production, storage, transmission and use of renewable energy and, in particular, green hydrogen. it's not just the energy system that we're transforming. it is a new model of economic and social organisation that we are building, a new cycle of opportunities to create wealth, jobs and a better quality of life, taking european leadership on the way to a less unequal, more sustainable and better quality of life society.
i have to say that i am very pleased. first of all, congratulations to our two rapporteurs on these two important files. the fight against climate change is urgent and the eu must take the lead. it is precisely cross-sector approaches, cross-sector linkages, that are crucial in the next step in the green transition, because only by going cross-sector can we really fight climate change. the development of green hydrogen is the future for many of the sectors that cannot be electrified immediately, such as heavy transport and industry. but hydrogen requires large amounts of green renewable energy. as the european parliament's rapporteur on the eu's offshore strategy, i am working to harness the potential of the seas by creating an ambitious intersectoral link so that green hydrogen can be produced on the basis of green power from offshore wind, and by integrating wind energy, through power-to-x and also in district heating, so that green renewable energy can fill much more of our energy supply than it does today. we can take inspiration from what is happening in skive in denmark, where the so-called greenlab has been set up, which is just now being used across sectors.
madam president, the long process of privatisation in the energy sector, promoted and encouraged by the european union, is contrary to national interests and has contributed to the destruction of the productive apparatus and skilled employment, thus increasing the external dependence of countries such as portugal. under the pretext of a green transition, radical energy closures and industrial restructuring are already in sight with a view to the early decommissioning of production units based on conventional energy sources with the harmful consequences for the productive and industrial capacity of states and without any environmental benefit. this is the case in portugal with the recent closure of the matosinhos refinery, whose workers here are to be congratulated for their struggle in defence of their jobs. the commission has already made a number of proposals. will they play only a notarial role, facilitating private investment, once again placing the question of energy sovereignty at the mercy of the sacred market and leaving states and peoples hostage to the interests of the large economic and multinational groups in the sector?
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, i wish to start by thanking the chair of our committee, the committee on regional development (regi), younous omarjee, the german presidency, and also our rapporteurs and shadow rapporteurs, particularly our rapporteur, manolis kefalogiannis, and commissioner ferreira. this is indeed a great day. with the just transition fund, the european union starts to put into concrete action those words that we have said, ‘leave no one behind’. from now on, this should only be the first day because, in all the implementation of the green deal, this should be the principle. we will not have a green transition if the transition is not just, if we leave anybody behind. that is our goal is and that’s what we have expressed with the just transition fund. we want more funds, but we want more policies going in this direction. the european parliament secured its priorities in the negotiation. we focused the just transition fund on the social consequences of the transition, not just – although it’s important – the reskilling of workers but, far beyond that, investment in job creation at a local level in the most affected regions through economic diversification and smes, through the social infrastructures and services in the local communities, and by also tackling energy poverty, which is critical in many of our regions and in many of our countries. we also put climate conditionality into the fund. half of this fund will only be available to those who commit to the climate targets and, yes, we put the green reward mechanism in the instrument. we did well. this is the beginning of a great transition supported by european funds.
madam president, commissioner, when we voted for a just transition fund, we assumed that the objectives of the european green deal could only be achieved if we preserve social cohesion and protect those who can be reached. the portuguese oil company galp recently announced the closure of its refinery in matosinhos, near porto. the closure is justified for environmental reasons, but it will greatly increase the company's profits and cause the layoffs of hundreds of workers. it is urgent to develop an innovative and environmentally sustainable economic project throughout this vast area and to support the retraining of workers. that work can and should be supported by this fund. but be careful. at the same time, it is imperative to avoid abuses, so that the company does not evade its responsibilities towards the workers and the decontamination of the land. the union is given a new instrument to promote social justice. let's get it to the people and communities that need it.
the current demographic trends in the european union are a problem at different levels. this should be seen globally, but actions should be implemented at local level, with particular attention to areas where the climate is more adverse and where access to services is limited or non-existent, without forgetting the specificities of territories with low population density, as well as the outermost regions. the latter suffer doubly from this phenomenon, either through internal migration flows or through the continental shelf. it is urgent to create the conditions to combat this phenomenon. in this sense, and with a view to fixing populations in regions with low population density, solutions must be developed to attract and retain populations, especially young people. we need an appropriate strategy for the development of these regions, which provide opportunities, good accessibility, access to services, employability and training and education. cohesion policy instruments are key to leveraging projects at local and regional level, in conjunction with the union's climate and digital objectives, which aim to ensure equal opportunities in all regions of the union.
mr president, commissioner, we are discussing a global health problem tonight that has a solution, but we are not doing the right thing in eliminating hiv/aids. one of the missions of the covid-19 pandemic is that we can respond better if we work together and coordinate with a global vision, and these good examples should be followed for the fight against hiv-aids. to do that, we need to increase health literacy, improve access to testing and screening services, improve access to medicines for treatment as well as prevention, while also empowering health professionals to better advise on these issues. we need to improve accessibility globally, at a fair price and in a timely manner, so that we leave no one behind.
madam president, the humanitarian crisis started too many years ago. europe is still trying to find and implement the proper answers, unfortunately. more than 600 people have already died this year – human beings. a solidarity response from member states, minister, is needed when scores of refugees seek protection in our countries. basic values are at stake when we talk about saving lives, about rescues at sea. protect and not criminalise those that are protecting our basic values like the ngos at sea. criminalise and never protect the smugglers that profit from desperation. on the external dimension of our policy, if we want to save lives, what we have to do is to properly handle through the partnerships with countries, in africa in particular, the situation of these migrants and refugees. promote economic and social development. commissioner, we need it for now, not for tomorrow: the full implementation of the global europe initiative. the strength for the southern countries can provide up to 30 billion in investment in these countries. certainly, the complex situation of ceuta, as you have mentioned, is now in our eyes, but also the situation of countries of origin like libya or of sub-saharan africa from which these people in despair come to europe. we need to act now to create hope where they only see despair at this moment.
madam president, human rights are amongst the founding values of the european union. we stand for them for all european citizens, therefore we must defend them for any human being. that is why there cannot be any conditionality to humanitarian or emergency aid. in the framework of the external migration and asylum policy, human rights must have a crucial role. as such, the eu’s cooperation with third countries on migration must, above all, respect and promote the human rights of those migrants and refugees across the migration routes. all the agreements, and particularly the readmission and border management agreements, can only be concluded with third countries that commit to respecting human rights, notably the principle of non—refoulement, and the un convention on the status of refugees. defending human rights is also creating the partnerships with countries of origin so that despair gives room to hope, so that human beings don’t feel the need to evade their countries to risk their lives on the way to europe.
the european union's external migration policy has left much to be desired. i regret that in this report it has been necessary to recall that this policy must respect, in all its dimensions, the principles enshrined in the treaty on european union and the charter of human rights. agreements which make development aid and humanitarian aid conditional on the management of migration, by outsourcing our responsibilities, are unacceptable. the money of european taxpayers cannot finance human rights violations, as we have seen under the eu-turkey declaration and the agreement with libya. the use of eu-trans-sam cannot continue to be shrouded in a lack of transparency, without parliamentary democratic control. agreements must be subject to a thorough assessment of their human rights implications. the atrocities committed on our borders must be stopped once and for all.

in writing. the ambition shown by the european commission deserves my sincere congratulations. reviving the european economy requires a vision of the future that is able to combine sustainability, competitiveness and social justice. putting into practice the ideas shared by commissioner paolo gentiloni allows us to build this path. in particular, the befit initiative should be a pillar of economic and monetary union: 1. addresses fundamental gaps in the fabric of tax systems that have allowed for aggressive tax planning schemes and, as a result, billions in lost revenue; 2. put an end to the unfair competition and 'race to the bottom' where those who are most willing to lower standards, i.e. tax havens, win; 3. creates a fairer common market, not only by facilitating cross-border economic activity but also by promoting opportunities for smes. the commission should also be able to provide information on the progress of the negotiations on the future of the union. this spirit of cooperation is the basis for building fairer tax systems and thus achieving an inclusive european economy that works for everyone.

mr president, mr minister, mr commissioner, it is clear to everyone in this house that you cannot be a political decision-maker on the budget of the european union and a direct or indirect beneficiary of that same budget. that would lead to a clear and blatant conflict of interest. it would mean a lack of transparency in the recipients of community funds. we demand more transparency in the management of the community budget. it is the money of european citizens that is at stake. in the negotiations on the multiannual budget, parliament therefore asked the commission to create mechanisms to prevent such conflicts. commissioner hahn has told us here about these initiatives by the commission, rules which oblige us to publish the final beneficiaries of european union funding, rules which assure us that we are only benefiting from the political objectives of the union and these mechanisms are binding on all member states. member states must comply with them in a manner that respects the citizens.

author. madam president, the conflict between armenia and azerbaijan has transformed the relationship between these two countries since 1988 and continues to cause suffering to the people. in the wake of the escalation of the conflict at the end of last year, which caused the death of more than five thousand soldiers in just 44 days and countless people who were injured or forced to leave their homes, many others were taken as prisoners of war and this is the issue on which this urgent resolution is based. in the tripartite declaration of ceasefire between the countries, they agreed to exchange prisoners of war, hostages and other detainees, as well as the remains of the victims, according to a principle of all for all. it is therefore unacceptable that to this day some armenian prisoners are still being held in azerbaijan and that we continue to receive reports of torture and degrading treatment of detainees in clear violation of the geneva convention. we therefore call for the immediate and unconditional release of all prisoners and for the full implementation of the ceasefire agreement. it is essential that both sides abandon hostile rhetoric and swiftly resume high-level political dialogue under the leadership of the minsk group co-chairs. only a negotiated political solution, respecting the basic principles of the osce, will be able to determine the future of the region in a peaceful, lasting and sustainable way.
mr president, commissioner, the frost that has just hit several member states is a real disaster for agriculture, especially for wine growers and for tree growers, as has been said at length. in france, the losses are estimated at €5 billion. this is another blow to a sector which has made it possible - need i remind you? - by its commitment to prevent the health crisis from becoming a food crisis. the seriousness of the situation in several european regions requires immediate support for producers through the various mechanisms for burden-relief and loans guaranteed by the state. the european union must also agree to lift all requirements on de minimis rules to allow member states to pay the necessary compensation in the name of national solidarity. there is an absolute need for exceptional emergency aid measures - and you have said so, commissioner - and the situation is very critical, as you know, for many farmers. this new crisis reminds us that climate change is no longer a matter of dispute and calls for a fundamental change in the way we manage agricultural disasters. agricultural risk management can no longer be seen as just an emerging market for insurance companies and the financial sector, as it has been for over 30 years. of course, insurance should be used for manageable risks; but when it comes to natural disasters, national and european solidarity must take over. this requires being pragmatic and looking at what else works. look at the united states for example: when losses are more than 50% of the harvest, it is the federal state that pays for these losses. spain has developed an exemplary system based on a robust public-private partnership over the last 40 years. why not take a look at it? it is high time in europe that public authorities take up their role as guarantors of the public interest and unwavering support in the event of a major crisis to secure our food system. yes, we need trade and commerce, but we also need food stocks. what is more essential and more strategic than food? today, the issue of strategic food stocks has become a major issue. this issue must no longer remain a taboo for the european union.
mr president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your kind words. today, 20 may, we celebrate world bee day for the fourth year running at the initiative of slovenia, in memory and honour of the great beekeeper anton janša. and when we talk about the slaughter, we also have to talk about the beekeeping and the damage that has been done to the bees, who, in cooperation with the farmers, produce food and provide us with food security. i can tell you very firsthand about the damage in the orchard. i'm a politician and a fruit-gatherer. i have three hectares of apples at home, and if last year we had 200 tons of apples in this apple orchard, and we had to burn the orchard to protect them from the overnight frost, this year the crop has been completely ruined, because 15 centimeters of snow, minus eight degrees, was too much for any active protection. i think it is right that we should be talking about this case in this parliament, because these injuries are becoming more and more frequent. in slovenia, we fruit growers have suffered the fourth damage, the fourth foreclosure in the last six years. it is therefore right that we should also speak out in the light of the adoption of the common agricultural policy for the coming years, to first seek out and put in the rural development programme the possibility of active protection, which is known to have certain conditions. i also agree with what has been said today about research into new varieties. but we also need active protection and a safety net, a safety net for flights like this one. this covid crisis, farmers have survived and made food security possible. i fear that these climate changes are causing these cases to become more frequent, so we certainly need to be able to jump in with a crisis reserve and help farmers, as we know how to help after covid with a recovery plan, otherwise farmers will fail and the next crises will simply not survive such cases.
mr president, despite all the technological modernisation, farmers' lives are still to a large extent marked by the constant shock of the increasingly extreme weather phenomena that dilute the very identity of the seasons. the unusual frosts which have recently severely affected thousands of hectares of vineyards and orchards are an example of this. agricultural activity is in fact different in character and is already closely linked to the characteristics of natural resources. this is coupled with their vulnerability to increasing climate change, which poses significant additional risks not only to agricultural activity but to food systems as a whole. of course, redoubled efforts are needed to effectively deliver on our shared commitment to achieve climate neutrality by 2050, but also to adopt concrete adaptation measures that mitigate threats, protect farmers' incomes and prevent desertification of our rural areas. we are talking about having effective risk management instruments which allow in extreme situations, such as the one we are discussing here today, to give a rapid response to farmers who, overnight, lose the produce of a whole year's work. but we also talk, for example, about supporting investments that increase fair access to water and its efficient and sustainable use, particularly in the most vulnerable regions such as the mediterranean regions, let me remind you here, where higher temperatures and reduced rainfall are threatening ever larger rural areas, condemning them to desertification.
mr president, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the context of today's world bee day, i would like to remind you of their enormous importance, but also of the increasingly significant impact of climate change. the spring frost, the worst since 1991, has caused great damage to our farmers who are now reaping the losses in this already difficult period. in croatia, the damage caused by natural disasters in recent years has left its mark on both producers and consumers. frost damage on some crops is as high as 90 or even 100 percent. this is unfortunately very much in evidence in the case of the native cherry and apricot. the apricot harvest at eu level is expected to be the lowest in 30 years, and the domestic cherry plantations have been particularly badly hit in dalmatia. i would also remind you that during the current pandemic, farmers have already suffered huge losses. european agricultural production does not exist without domestic agriculture. it is therefore necessary to support farmers not only with greater investment but also with modern technological solutions. and speaking of negative trends, recently our winemakers have been alarmed by the signs about the commission's plans to favour dealcoholised wine. such alternatives must not be on an equal footing with our traditional products and must not jeopardise their access to the market. we in croatia are proud of our varieties and marks of origin and want to preserve them. in general, we expect the commission to be flexible and to propose measures to effectively help farmers, fruit growers and vine growers to cover frost losses.
member of the commission. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i'm impressed with the discussion and the speech. the members recalled the dramatic scenes of farmers fighting to save their plantations, and we all saw the fires burning in europe. it was a dramatic fight, hard to win, of course, and the losses are huge. we all know that. i have great respect for the farmers for this dramatic struggle and i am sorry for those who have lost their livelihood. it is a dramatic situation indeed, and today we are faced with one. this crisis shows that farmers are indeed the first victims of climate change. the first victims, because it's the farmers that are most affected right now. we have a whole series of events like this that dramatically affect agriculture: disasters (these are climate-related), droughts, floods, hurricanes, frosts, fires that increasingly affect european agriculture. we have disease crises -- the pandemic has had a huge negative impact on the economy and agriculture -- african swine fever, bird flu. we also have political crises, like the russian embargo or the american tariffs. these are examples of events that suddenly hit farmers and cause huge losses. i think that the great risks of farming today are the main reason why a lot of farms are disappearing and why it's becoming increasingly difficult to replace generations in agriculture. farming is a very risky profession, and many people are afraid that in this profession you can do everything with the best of knowledge, will, and care and because of these various events, you can lose everything. so it's really important that we have a system of support for farmers in these situations? the farmers can't do it alone. as a commissioner, i would very much like to have such an emergency fund which could be set up in such situations to help farmers effectively. we have the budget we have. the contingency reserve provided for in this budget is small. we're still debating its size and how it works. eventually, it will be revealed in the trilateral talks how this debate will end. i am in favour of, and the commission is in favour of, strengthening the crisis management system in agriculture in a decisive way, so that these are more effective elements. it is also budgetary, because the crisis reserve has practically never been used in the past, because unfortunately only a minority of countries are affected by crises, and most of them are generally in favour of not breaching the direct subsidies or reducing them in their countries. and this reserve is dissolved year after year and not used. we're discussing this in the trilateral talks. as regards insurance, there is evidence and reports (such as the european court of auditors report) that agricultural insurance schemes are not effective. although it's different in different countries. the court of auditors' report includes a map of europe showing insurance systems. we have a group of countries that benefit from insurance subsidies from the european union budget (portugal, france, belgium, the netherlands, italy, croatia, hungary, romania and the baltic states). there are countries that contribute from their own budget and pay for insurance (spain, slovenia, austria, poland, greece, bulgaria, etc.). and there are countries that have no subsidies but only commercial insurance (this system is used in germany, slovakia, ireland, sweden and finland, among others). we really need to have a serious debate about how to strengthen the crisis management system in agriculture. part of this debate is taking place in the context of the reform of the common agricultural policy, but i think that is perhaps a little too little. we need to think about a long-term crisis management system in agriculture. there must be different instruments, including instruments that will increase the resilience of agriculture within the framework of strategic plans. we will be looking at this and we want member states to propose actions that will reduce the risk of farmers being hit by disasters and that will strengthen the resilience of agriculture. we have reconstruction funds that are being debated and member states are making proposals. agriculture is not excluded from the recovery funds and various measures to strengthen the resilience of agriculture to such crisis situations can also be supported. it has to be a synergy of different policies, it has to be a system that will strengthen agriculture, because i am absolutely convinced that we have to do more. farmers provide us with food security, and we should work together to provide farmers with economic security and help in difficult crisis situations.
the debate is closed. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. farmers are increasingly suffering from the effects of various weather events. for example, frost and hail are becoming more frequent in spring, which in many parts of europe seriously affects fruit and wine growers and their production, with considerable economic losses. similarly, droughts, heavy rains and fires cause enormous damage to agricultural activity. in the context of the discussion on the common agricultural policy (cap), we have proposed a public agricultural insurance, financed from the union budget, which would provide a minimum level of protection for all farmers in all member states. we believe that this would be a way of guaranteeing a minimum income to farmers affected by extreme weather events and other natural or man-made disasters, including forest fires, diseases and pests. the existence of such insurance would not prejudice the existence of other insurance schemes, specific to certain sectors, and would ensure a minimum level of protection for farmers. in the current situation, where mitigation and adaptation measures are still lacking, the creation of this insurance would be a more than fair measure. unfortunately, this proposal of ours has been rejected by the majority in this parliament.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, the landscape of small and mediumsized enterprises in the european union is heterogeneous. many of them struggle daily against the crushing of large economic groups and the unfair and unequal competition of large companies and multinationals which contribute to social dumping, deregulation of labour relations and insolvency. with covid-19, the contrast between member states in the capacity to intervene in support of smes has exacerbated inequalities between these companies and, at the same time, has drawn attention to the different national specificities. it is therefore important that the member states should be able to intervene to support the solvency of smes in difficulty, and such support should presuppose the preservation of jobs, the defence of wages and full respect for workers' rights. in order for member states to be able to support small and medium-sized enterprises in the transition processes in the areas of sustainability and digitalisation, it is essential that the european union's impositions and constraints be eliminated through so-called economic governance and that member states have the means, including financial means, to promote these transitions within the framework of cohesion policy.
on behalf of the left group. madam president, this crisis has shown that no set of european rules can withstand a crisis. budgetary rules, financial system rules, competition rules, whenever the european union and the euro zone are faced with a crisis, they have to suspend all their fundamental rules. that should give you pause, but apparently it is not in this report. the competition rules were suspended so that germany could, thanks to the suspension of the budgetary rules, inject tens of billions of euros into its public or private companies. the european commission, which has always applied the rules in a highly discretionary and uneven manner between member states, is now also applying the suspension of the rules in an equally discretionary and uneven manner between member states. and, interestingly, it continues to see no competition problems in fiscal dumping, social dumping, labour dumping and environmental dumping, which continue to be the business model of most eu member states. discretion in everything, including criteria.
madam president, the internal market of the european union is one of the greatest achievements of integration. for my generation, which does not know the closed borders of other times, there are three pillars of this single market which will progressively have to be greener and more digital. first, the free movement of persons, goods, services and capital. secondly, the european social model on which our social market economy is based. thirdly, competition policy which, when it is fair, fair and balanced, protects consumers and helps our economy to grow and innovate. some national politicians attack european competition policy when they do not like the decisions. the case of the portuguese government with tap, the national airline, is an example of this. what these politicians do not realise (or pretend not to realise) is that it is the european competition rules that protect the competitiveness of companies in countries like portugal. the pandemic has shown that we have a framework of rules that is flexible enough to deal with emergencies. what we need now is to look at the challenges of the future, which is increasingly digital and online. ensuring that the same rules apply to anyone selling the same product or providing the same service, whether it's on our street or on our screen. it's time to move on.
mr president, the recovery and protection of biodiversity are fundamental for the environment, the economy and society. only with healthy species and habitats will we have a resilient environment ready to respond to imbalances like climate change. fisheries and aquaculture depend on a healthy and diverse environment. so long before the european green deal, they have been adapting to fishing and production techniques with a lower impact on the environment. there are steps being taken in this direction, but the adaptation effort that fisheries and aquaculture have been making cannot be ignored. we must have an ambitious strategy. we must ensure that there is a balance and sustainability in the exploitation of resources and the preservation of the environment. this balance cannot, however, be achieved by excluding all activities. we must instead ensure that they are carried out with appropriate sustainability in all areas.
in writing. the eu digital covid certificate is a milestone for the european union. in just two months after the european commission's proposal was presented, we reached an agreement. today we have a tool which will be implemented in all member states on 1 july, and which will thus facilitate the mobility of people, safeguarding public health. this certificate will contain information about the individual's immune status to covid-19: whether they are vaccinated, whether they have contracted the disease and are recovered, or whether they are not infected with the virus. the latter shall be confirmed by a negative antigen test within 24 hours or a negative pcr test within 72 hours. the certificate will be issued by national authorities in digital or paper format and will be an important instrument to ensure the free movement of citizens within the european union. note the guarantee of privacy of citizens' health data throughout the process. once again, the eu is showing its mettle and responding to a global challenge in a coordinated way. the free movement of citizens will be restored, with an instrument that will complement all the prevention and mitigation measures already implemented against this devastating pandemic.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is fair to say, commissioner, that you have the knife and the cheese in your hand. all the conditions are met for the commission, on behalf of the union, to obtain financing on the capital markets in order to create a recovery facility. one after another, democratically, national parliaments have confirmed this. the commission is committed to a roadmap for the introduction of a new own resources envelope to offset, for the time being, the capital and interest costs of financing the recovery facility. we are aware that it is not enough for the commission to propose. but it is essential, commissioner, that we stick to this road map. perhaps the compromise reached at the g7 a few days ago can help to speed up the timetable for the commission and the member states to approve it. we know that the success of economic and social recovery, now and for generations to come, also depends on this. we know that the future ambition of the european union depends on its financing and that it also passes through here.
mr president, the idea of european parliament scrutiny of the implementation of the rrf is one which my group supports and on whose behalf i would be prepared to make very considerable political concessions, as the negotiations have shown. but the end result is too bad. the demands for scrutiny of the commission are absolutely paltry, especially when compared with the draconian calls for the commission to impose on the member states structural reforms which in many cases have had catastrophic consequences in the past and have been democratically rejected by the peoples of those countries. the specific recommendations even adopt a quasi-colonial discourse which reveals an absolute disregard for the sovereignty and democracy of the member states belonging to this union and also an absolute hypocrisy in the speeches. here we hear the president of the commission, who is no longer here, talk about the digital transition and the green transition. but at the same time as these fine speeches are being made here in the european parliament, the technical teams of the commission are calling on the government of my country to cut health care, to cut pensions and to further deregulate the labour market. if the idea is to hand over some of the biggest countries in the european union to the far right, congratulations, we are on the right track.
mr president, madam secretary of state, ana paula zacarias, ladies and gentlemen, we know very well that what is at stake is the economic and social recovery of the european union. and that requires a huge and concerted effort by the member states and the european institutions to get out of this crisis that has deeply affected our lives. it is in this combination of efforts - national institutions/european institutions - that we will ensure european added value. only then can we be sure that the green deal and the digital transition are effective and that they generate more equitable and less unequal societies. the european parliament is not the executive. we will act politically within our competence. but we will do everything to ensure that the recovery fund does not one day delay its arrival in the member states and, consequently, in the citizens. yes, europeans, who rely on the european response, must be at the centre of the recovery. it is urgent to approve the plans now, but it is also essential to ensure that they are implemented. we need a social scoreboard to show the progress of social policies and that the conclusions of the porto social summit are to be implemented. in line with the recommendations of the european court of auditors, we need to ensure that recovery makes a decisive contribution to gender equality, starting with the reduction of the gender pay gap by half. this time we have political objectives and we have the resources to achieve them.
