madam president, the afghan people have been living in a desperate situation for decades now. political and religious violence, severe discrimination against women and disrespect for basic human rights are far from having been eradicated, and it’s hard to be optimistic about the future given the recent developments on the ground, as we have talked about at length. increased violence has sparked fears that things may get even worse. the united states announced its full withdrawal from afghanistan, followed by nato. there is a high risk of increased violence. therefore, the international community cannot just wait and see what will come after this. the eu and its member states and the usa must remain engaged in the peace efforts, supporting the afghan authorities and promoting regional cooperation through a multilateral framework. the international community cannot turn a blind eye to afghanistan and abandon it to its fate. there are millions of lives at stake.
madam president, mr vice-president, we can say to all cubans that europe is with you, with your rights, against dictatorship and against repression. and that is why i say, and i say it forcefully: mr high representative, what the union's representative has done in cuba is not understood. it has put all human rights issues on the back burner and has devoted itself to defending one of the worst dictatorships in history, accusing our allies, the americans, of interference. this is not a way to contribute to better relations, but to worse ones. that you, mr high representative, do not know russia, we can understand. we cannot accept that he does not know cuba and the suffering of the cuban people.
mr president, mr high representative, i would like to begin by expressing our solidarity with the santo isidro movement and all human rights activists in cuba and urging that their physical integrity and personal dignity be preserved. we also hope that the european union will call on the cuban government to repeal laws that impede freedom of expression and association. however, we cannot ignore or fail to highlight the important steps that have already been taken with the political dialogue and cooperation agreement between the european union and cuba, including the defence of human rights. we also stress the importance of the european investment bank recognising cuba as a country eligible for access to its financing, as it does other countries with much greater problems. in short, we are as concerned about cuba as we are about other latin american countries in this crisis today, to provide them with the economic and health aid they need so that they do not suffer setbacks. the small, now great achievements made on the road to progress, peace and democracy.
madam president, mr high representative, it is true that four years ago we inaugurated the new political dialogue and cooperation agreement, which, incidentally, put an end to years of isolation, a position which also clearly proved ineffective. but we must recognize that, after four years, the situation of human rights and freedoms on the island is deteriorating every day. for this reason, if the human rights clause in this agreement is to have tangible results, we must redirect our action, the action of the european union, and we must implement the agreement differently. we must recognize that cuba is a dictatorship and that there is no other way out of this dictatorship than a democratic transition. we must put an end to this outdated dialectic, this abusive and anachronistic discourse that confuses the identification of the survival of castroism with cuba; that repeats "fatherland or death" while persecuting bloggers, singers, artists who are increasingly shouting in cuba "fatherland and life". that is where the european union has to be.
madam president, once again the european parliament is being used as a hypocritical forum for democracy and human rights. if this parliament were really concerned about human rights, it would be demanding an end to the criminal economic, commercial and financial blockade that the united states of america has imposed on cuba and its people for 62 years. a lockdown that the trump administration has cruelly stepped up, even in the context of the pandemic, and that the biden administration is maintaining. let's get serious about this. in fact, this debate has other purposes. it is intended to call into question the progress made in the normalisation of relations between the european union and cuba with the signing of the dialogue and cooperation agreement and to seek to impede the completion of its ratification and to create the background for further and unspeakable manoeuvres of interference against cuba and interference in decisions which only its people can sovereignly take. we express our solidarity with cuba and its people and praise their example of sovereignty, resistance, perseverance, coherence, courage and dignity.
the fight against climate change, the defence of human rights, the rule of law, peace and security, sustainable development are challenges that require global responses and reinforce the importance of multilateralism when, paradoxically, so many put it in question. in this report of recommendations to the council we stress the importance of strengthening the role of the european union within the united nations. for this it is essential that we are able to speak with one voice and move towards decision by qualified majority. the effectiveness of our action and our ability to influence depends very much on this. it is essential that the union is more active in supporting the reform of the un security council, particularly with regard to the limitation of the veto power which undermines its functioning and its composition by adapting it to the present world. let us be clear, the european union must have a permanent seat on the security council. i would like to end by congratulating the rapporteur on her very constructive attitude throughout this work.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, commissioner urpilainen, the establishment of global europe is, indeed, as you refer, a major achievement for the eu with which europe assumes its role and its responsibility in the world. the programme is dedicating 80 billion euros to promote eu objectives worldwide, including the sustainable development goals and the climate objectives. our political group, our political family, fought for having development assistance detached from any conditionality related to migration management. and i am happy to say that it was a great victory, not only for the progressive forces but mainly for the developing world. development aid is due to promote development, not to be used as blackmail, and human beings cannot be pawns in political negotiations. gender equality, social inclusion, human development are objectives per say. health, education, nutrition, water must be accessible to everyone. they are basic human rights. there is still much to be done, but with global europe, the eu is on the right path. let’s proceed on this road.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur and all those who have worked on the difficult negotiations with the commission and the council on this report. we didn't win everything, but we did get some important guarantees. of course, the 25% guaranteed for social inclusion and the fact that the fund for european aid to the most deprived (fead) was left out of that 25%. we also got the guarantee of minimum values for support for youth employment. they were very difficult battles and it's important that we were able to win them. however, i would like to point out what i consider to be the main defeat in these negotiations with the commission and the council and reveals the enormous hypocrisy of these two institutions, namely that the 5% dedicated to child poverty is only allocated to member states with child poverty levels above 23.4%. at a time when so much hype is being made about the child guarantee, to consider that one in five children being poor is acceptable in the european union was a bad sign given by these institutions. but they did not do so on behalf of this parliament.
mr president, commissioner, more than 60 years have passed since the european social fund was set up. in france, during the last programme, almost four million people were able to be assisted in returning to work, in re-training or in retraining. after these months of pandemic, the €88 billion of this new european social fund will have to be mobilised quickly to support the most vulnerable, the poorest, children at risk of poverty. they will also have to accompany young people, our young people who have been so affected by the crisis, who often lack training or work experience, even though they are the ones we need to create, to build together the future of europe. with this new fund, ladies and gentlemen, europe is at the rendezvous of solidarity. but the ball is now in the court of the member states.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i can only join in the thanks and congratulations that have been addressed to you, mr rapporteur, for the good work that has been done. i also note that there is a very broad consensus and very broad support for this european social fund, which is - and this has just been mentioned - the oldest of the structural funds. so it is also a small reminder to all those who sometimes question or doubt the need for the social dimension of our europe. the founding fathers had thought of that. they understood that there is no strong europe without a strong social europe. i also believe that this fund is a response to the expectations of these europeans - and more than 90% of them have expressed their expectations and their wish for a strong social europe. now, let's get to work. i take up the motto of the portuguese presidency, which i would also like to congratulate on having contributed to this compromise, and whose motto is: 'it's time to deliver'.

in writing. i am very pleased that agreement has been reached on the esf+ fund, which will play a key role in europe's post-pandemic recovery and in strengthening the social pillar. in 2019, there were 18 million children at risk of poverty and social exclusion in the eu. the social and economic crisis caused by the covid-19 outbreak has disproportionately increased child poverty and inequality. child poverty is widespread in all member states and is higher than that of working-age adults. the esf+ is becoming one of the main tools to tackle child poverty. the youths were also deeply affected. the commission shall be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 26 to supplement this regulation by laying down detailed rules for the application of this regulation. youth unemployment stood at 17.8% at the latest and is more than 10 percentage points higher than adult unemployment. the esf+ is becoming one of the main tools to tackle child poverty and the main eu funding instrument for the recently adopted enhanced youth guarantee and other relevant measures under the youth employment initiative. i am very much hopeful that the commission will do its utmost to ensure that the member states allocate adequate esf+ funding to the esf+ programme, thereby contributing to the implementation of the european child guarantee and youth guarantee initiative, and will encourage member states to use other eu funding instruments and available national resources to support appropriate investments in this area.
in writing. the interinstitutional agreement on the european social fund regulation which was adopted today is far from providing a solution to the problems. the esf has played a leading role in public funding of social policies in portugal. we advocated in 2019 a considerable increase in budget allocation, a proposal that the covid-19 accelerated crisis has proven to be fundamental. we cannot but regret the agreement reached, which provides for a lower appropriation at 2018 prices compared to the previous mff. the agreement reached is also affected by the political conditionality imposed by the subordination of esf+ to the european semester and the country specific recommendations, instruments which have contributed so much to the deterioration of the social and labour situation in the member states. in our view, what was needed in this interinstitutional agreement was a substantial increase in the esf+ budget and support for projects that place value on free, universal, quality public health and education services as a key factor in tackling social problems and challenges.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, commissioner, climate change has an impact on infrastructure and the economy, but it also has social effects which cannot be ignored. the loss of jobs, particularly in fisheries, is an important consequence to be taken into account. measures to make fisheries and aquaculture more resilient imply environmental, economic and social sustainability. the measures to mitigate and combat climate change will therefore also have to guarantee employment. fishing faces an added challenge, the increasing average age of fishermen. making fisheries resilient means ensuring decent employment with conditions of safety and stability. this is the only way to contribute to the generational renewal of fishermen. young people are better prepared for issues related to biodiversity, environmental protection and the digital transition. to ensure the continuity of fisheries and to make them more resilient, fisheries need this renewal.
on behalf of the left group. madam president, this will probably be my last speech here in this plenary in strasbourg as a member of the european parliament. i'm grateful that this is a talk about a subject that has occupied me for a long time over the last 12 years. i would like to say that over the years the situation in the fisheries sector has improved. unfortunately, that is not possible. the problems are largely old, the causes have been mentioned here on several occasions, and the solutions are: the development of small-scale, artisanal and coastal fishing - based on local management, which counters the increasing centralization of management far from reality - the improvement of fishermen's incomes - essential to ensure the rejuvenation and continuity of the sector - the improvement of marketing and a fairer distribution of added value along the value chain, more investment in research and knowledge of the state of resources. all this requires a greater role for the states and public policies, contrary to the logic of increasing subordination to markets and other constraints imposed on a supranational level. the fighting continues in the fisheries too.
madam president, commissioner, as a child of a fishing family, i learned very early on what sustainability means and the commitment it entails. life that seemed endless, that filled boxes and boxes of fish, went out. less fish, fewer boats, less quota... we were among the first to pay the social and economic price of assuming that we live on a planet with limits. now, as climate change announces irreversible consequences and new constraints, we who live by the sea want to prioritise the measures we will need to adapt to the new certainties. people in coastal communities live on the shore where they anticipate all the changes that are coming. that is why we are asking for a specific, priority plan that will allow us to be more proactive than reactive. we welcome the importance that the european strategy for adaptation to climate change gives to local communities and we want to take advantage of it by being protagonists in information, knowledge, training and diversification. we are asking for solidarity, empathy and affection. economic activities linked to the sea are more sustainable today thanks to the efforts of coastal communities. we deserve to be recognised and to build on the path we have taken to meet the next challenge: ensuring the resilience of a strategic sector. aquaculture and fisheries are not only key to european food security, but they also sustain communities and cultures that are an essential part of the diversity on which our union is based.
madam president, the issue of climate—resilient fisheries and aquaculture is indeed very important. but we need to address the negative impacts of fisheries on climate change, and we can start to tackle the problem by fully implementing the common fisheries policy and the related legislation that we already have. but i wanted to focus on the fact that the sector itself has an important role to play and must contribute to global co2 reduction efforts. a recent study published by the scientific journal nature showed that industrial fishing – in particular, bottom trawling – generates more co2 emissions than air travel. why? because when trawls are dragged along the sea floor, large amounts of carbon are released, which increases global warming and makes the sea more acidic. it also has an adverse effect on plankton and entire marine ecosystems, which produce more or less 80% of the oxygen on earth. so, on world oceans day, let us take concrete steps to end bottom trawling by eu vessels worldwide. without this ban, we cannot achieve climate neutrality, and this is the only option if we want to be coherent with the european green deal and the eu’s targets to reduce co2 emissions.
in writing. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, months ago i wrote with great satisfaction that joe biden was a good man, who would certainly make a good president of the united states. barring any outright sectarian views, i would say it's almost unanimous that biden has proven to be a great president. now he's come to europe. i shall not go into the details of the virtualities and importance of this visit. i just want to reflect on the absolutely diametrical difference in biden's political intervention when compared to the previous president. even before coming to europe, biden was already, through various channels, revitalizing the transatlantic relationship, he was already in dialogue with the other g7 countries, he had already stressed the importance of nato. the european union has become a partner with a vision of cooperation and joint work. i should like to conclude this speech as follows: mr president joe biden, welcome to europe.
madam president, the european union has the necessary instruments to monitor, assess and respond appropriately to problems relating to the rule of law and the protection of fundamental rights. prevention and promotion tools, such as the annual rule of law report, and response tools, whether article 7 or the article 258 infringement procedure. but the european institutions have shown a tremendous inability to enforce various european policies, while the european parliament has done its job. in matters of the rule of law, it's glaring. i could give other examples, such as refugee policy. it is imperative, it is unavoidable that the commission, as guardian of the treaties, should act against all persistent violations and mobilise all instruments, starting with the regulation on the mechanism of conditionality of the rule of law, which entered into force on 1 january, and which introduces a mechanism with effective consequences to enforce and enforce it.
the next item on the agenda is the debate on the statement by the council and the commission on the follow-up to the social summit in porto on 7 may 2021 (2021/2714 ((rsp)).
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, thank you for this opportunity to speak about the porto summit and, more specifically, about its follow—up. as you know, this is a subject particularly close to the heart of the portuguese presidency and to mine personally. the 7 and 8 may were key dates for portugal and for the union as a whole. as the holder of the presidency and host of the event, the porto social summit was a central feature of the semester for portugal and an integral part of our vision for the future of europe that puts people first. the porto social summit comprised the high—level conference and an informal meeting of eu leaders. it was historic in two respects. first, the conference agreed on the porto social commitment, enacting the intention to put the social pillar principles into action together. it was co—signed by the president of this parliament, the president of the european commission, my prime minister (as the host of the conference), and the european social partners – the first time ever that this has happened. second, the leaders agreed on the porto declaration. this was also the first ever statement devoted exclusively to social policy issues. the social pillar will be a fundamental element of the post—covid recovery. its action plan will guide us in developing the union’s social dimension. together, they will help us navigate towards a sustainable and fair recovery that leaves no one behind. they will strengthen the union’s drive towards a digital, green and fair transition and will contribute to achieving upward social and economic convergence. in porto, we reaffirmed the commitment that the pillar principles will be implemented at eu and national level with due regard for the respective competences, the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality, and different national circumstances. active involvement of the social partners and civil society will be key in this. these are not empty words. progress towards the implementation of the pillar principles will be closely monitored as part of the policy coordination framework in the context of the european semester, including at the highest level. the eu headline targets on jobs, skills and poverty reduction were broadly welcomed. they respond to the repeated call by employment and social ministers for a follow up strategy to europe 2020. the commission’s proposal for a revised social scoreboard also found a positive echo. both the eu—level headline targets and the revised scoreboard will help monitor progress towards the implementation of the pillar principles in the context of the european semester. the employment committee and the social protection committee have, in the meantime, agreed on the headline indicators of the revised social scoreboard, as proposed by the commission, to be used in the joint employment report. they are currently pursuing their work in relation to the possible integration of secondary indicators. finally, the idea of an alternative set of indicators to measure economic, social and environmental progress was also welcome, supplementing gdp as a welfare measure for inclusive and sustainable growth. building a social europe will always be a work in progress, not least because challenges evolve over time. it will always rely on a diverse toolbox and will always comprise different architectures. i am deeply grateful that, over the years, the european parliament has been one of the staunchest defenders of the european social model. your own—initiative report on a strong social europe for just transitions of december 2020 proves that social europe remains high on the list of priorities of this parliament. the council is also playing its part in this common pursuit of a fairer, more inclusive and resilient europe. important advances are being achieved under the portuguese presidency. on 14 june, ministers of employment and social affairs will adopt by unanimity the long—awaited council recommendation on a european child guarantee, inspired by this house. on other commission initiatives, for instance on the minimum wage and pay transparency, work will continue in the council. our presidency will not rest in its efforts to achieve as much progress as possible by the end of this term.
member of the commission. – mr president, madam secretary of state, honourable members, the adoption of the social pillar action plan represents a milestone in our common work to build a strong social europe, and it has paved the way to the porto social summit. i would like once again to praise the portuguese presidency and particularly prime minister costa and yourself, secretary of state, for putting the strengthening of the social dimension high in the european agenda and providing steer, vision and determination. it is safe to say that the porto social summit is a major achievement. eu institutions, member states, social partners and civil society have rallied around the common aim of further deepening the implementation of the european pillar of social rights as a fundamental element of the recovery, contributing to fair transitions and upward social and economic convergence. i came before parliament in march to present the social pillar action plan and the parliament’s contribution has played in this respect a very decisive role. based on your input and concrete proposals for the implementation of the social pillar, including on key targets, we have designed a long-term vision and a coherent and ambitious roadmap to shape our post-pandemic economies and societies in a fairer, more inclusive and sustainable manner so as to improve the well-being of our citizens. it was an urgent step to take in the face of the digital and green transitions. those are necessary transformations, just as it is necessary to make them work for everyone, especially for the most vulnerable or those exposed to the fundamental changes we are heading for in terms of their jobs and their income. as stated in the porto declaration, the pillar of social rights is a fundamental element of the recovery, but also of the union’s drive towards a digital, green and fair transition. it was also an urgent step to take because this pandemic risks leaving permanent scars and throwing millions of europeans into poverty, and among them a lot of young people. it was the right step to take after a decade of growing inequalities, low wages, low pensions, which also led to political distrust. since march we have taken a major leap forward, from the leap of faith of gothenburg, porto marked the moment to turn words into action and rally around a different message and a different project. the porto social summit has resulted in the commitment that our citizens expect, a commitment to a fairer and better future for all. porto is a collective pledge from member states, social partners, civil society and the eu institutions, that people can count on us to deliver concrete solutions for the challenges they face in their daily lives. having all these stakeholders committing to implement the pillar is an important political novelty brought by this summit. it reinforces the notion enshrined in the social market economy: all actors have a role to play in keeping with their sphere of competence. altogether, porto sent a strong signal and marked a shift with respect to our european social model. this is not simply seen as a feature that we need to protect, this is considered as the engine of our future prosperity and well—being. to make sure that this time is different, to make sure that we indeed reinforce the european social model, member states have agreed to three new social targets set by the european commission. by 2030, we will need to reach an employment rate of 78%. we need to target women and young people. this also means we have to remain ambitious with our proposals on minimum wage and pay transparency, but also for people with disability. by 2030, we will also have developed adult training, aiming to achieve the objective of at least 60% of adults participating in training every year, to ensure everyone remains relevant and seizes the opportunities that tomorrow’s world of work will offer. and by 2030, we will have to double down on our efforts to lift at least 15 million europeans out of poverty. it is the focus of our child guarantee initiative, which aims at supporting children at risk of poverty and social exclusion and providing equal opportunities, as well as of our proposal on minimum wage to combat in-work poverty. it will remain the focus of our upcoming initiative on homelessness and our proposal on minimum income. what are the next steps? implementing the action plan and achieving its new targets by 2030. member states have committed to this. the governance of the implementation of the pillar action plan is the key to success. first, we will have to follow up with member states and stimulate the adoption of national targets. we will miss our objective if some make considerable progress, while others stagnate. we have to improve life opportunities, working conditions and social protection for all in all member states. second, we will have to report on our progress towards reaching the targets, firmly anchoring the social pillar in the european semester process. we have proposed a revised social scoreboard that is currently being discussed with member states. this is a tool that helps measure social progress in this regard. i also welcome the initiative taken by social partners on the ongoing reflection on alternative measures to gdp. as stated in the porto declaration, we will also have to regularly take stock of our progress at the highest level. we will have to be ambitious and i would call on the european parliament to support, and i would say to stimulate, this ambition. this means that we have to strengthen our social economic governance. finally, we want to track the impact of future social investments under the recovery and resilience facility so we make sure this recovery is job-rich and fair. we have to look into the distributional effect of investments and reforms. we cannot claim to help the most vulnerable and then only look at the big picture. in this context we have to ensure a just transition. we have to measure the impact of our policies on people’s lives. together with member states, we have to set up adequate coordination mechanisms to deliver concrete progress for the european people. i am looking forward to having the parliament’s views, not only on the result of porto, but also precisely on the follow-up. and you have been a driving force since gothenburg to the summit of porto, to build this social europe which is so important for our union.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i think there are two questions that people have been asking themselves on a regular basis. the first question is, which came first, the chicken or the egg? and the second question is, is the glass half full or is the glass half empty? and although there were some at the social summit in portugal who are known for their frequent cackling but rarely laying an egg, i want to focus on the second question, which is: is the glass half full or half empty when looking at porto? i think the glass is half full. although we were much more ambitious as a parliament in december when we were preparing porto in our own-initiative report than the commission was and much more ambitious than the heads of state or government have now shown in the final result, i think we have achieved a great deal. and nicolas has just described this, i think, well again, the way from gothenburg to now the summit of porto. and the secretary of state is right when she says that the issue of 'social europe' is not a matter that can be dealt with in one step, but that it is an ongoing process. and i think we can build on the porto result because, for example, we now have a joint declaration with the social partners on the european semester, where there is a common understanding that the european semester is also a proper place to monitor social progress and the implementation of the social pillar. we have often fought ideological trench wars here in this parliament. we can avoid this in the future because even the social partners have developed a common understanding here. and so in the future, we can focus on other things, like, for example, how can we strengthen the semester process? i do not know when the next social summit will take place, but i am quite sure that to make the glass of 'social europe' even fuller, the bottle is not the commission, not the council, but the commission is in the middle of this parliament. let's keep working on it.
on behalf of the s&d group, mr president, commissioner schmit, dear secretary of state, ana paula zacarias, ladies and gentlemen, ever since the european union agreed to implement the recovery fund, on behalf of the id group. mr president, the country from which i come, my country, is flanders. flanders is the northern part of the belgian member state and belgium is also the border between northern and southern europe. since the foundation of social security and social protection in belgium, from flanders to wallonia, from the north to the south, where there is a different culture of social protection, there have been transfers, very large transfers, transfers greater - according to academic calculations - than the transfers between west germany and east germany after the fall of the wall. there is a long debate in belgium and the conclusion of the debate on the flemish side is that social protection and social security should become an autonomous flemish competence. it makes sense. solidarity must be built up close to the community. there has to be recognition. there must be subsidiarity, at the very most, and it is therefore illogical to take these matters to a higher level, as we are doing here in europe. i therefore call upon the european parliament: stop this expansion of power. let us not allow the european union to bottle up the flemish social security system, the flemish health system and the flemish social protection system.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, it is all right to put the social dimension at the centre of the political debate, but it is nothing more than propaganda if we take into account that the economic policies of the european union are profoundly anti-social and practically deny access to social and labour rights. an effective response to the serious problems facing workers and peoples today requires radical changes in the policies of the european union, which the porto summit did not bring. and the workers took to the streets in porto on 8 may with the answers that the summit did not give and that the action plan of the so-called 'european pillar of social rights' does not present. the right to employment with rights, the general increase in wages, the eradication of precariousness, the regulation and reduction of working hours with a view to the need to reconcile family, personal and professional life, equality between men and women, the strengthening of social security, the strengthening of public services and investment in the social functions of the state, these are the demands of the workers and it is for them that we will continue to fight.
madam president, between the lisbon treaty and the european semester, a huge contradiction has been created in europe. the lisbon treaty chose to say that the charter of fundamental rights of the citizens of the european union had no legal value, it was not community law. the european semester has given the commission and the council powers to intervene in key social policies such as public services, the labour market or pension systems. today we see this contradiction live. in the debate on the pillar of social rights and at the social summit, it was said that there could be no rights at european level because this is a responsibility of the states and therefore what we have are ideas, suggestions, recommendations, priorities, commitments. it's all very generic. but when we discuss mrr and the release of funds from the recovery and resilience fund, then there is the conditionality of the european semester, the structural reforms in the labour market, the pension systems and the cuts in public services. the european commission, the european union, has no powers to directly create or enforce discipline, but it already has powers to destroy it.
madam president, madam secretary of state, mr commissioner, the porto social summit signaled the european union's withdrawal from austerity as a response to an economic and social crisis. according to the latest eurobarometer, the union's positive image reached its highest level since 2009 this spring. the citizens followed the summit in a very positive way. it would be unacceptable to waste this opportunity for people to reconcile with the european project. the union and the member states must mobilise to implement the action plan and the european pillar of social rights, the result of a historic consensus. we need long-term solutions that are socially sustainable. we refuse to return to a rhetoric of bad memory that calls for structural reforms but forgets people and their dignity. we call for national recovery plans to contribute to the objectives of social europe. social investment must be encouraged, in quality employment, the fight against poverty, health, education and housing. we must rethink the macroeconomic criteria so that we do not end up with absurd recommendations that contradict the objectives of the social pillar. the social summit was the beginning of a long journey. the european parliament will have to play a mobilizing, demanding and rigorously monitoring role in this process. we will.
madam president, recent data shows that attacks that take information systems hostage and demand ransoms from their owners increased by 150% in 2020 and the ransom value doubled. the increasing migration of our lives to the online sphere and the increase in networked products, the storage and use of a lot of data, have made the importance of cybersecurity all too evident. the european cybersecurity strategy for the decade is a key contribution to keeping the union secure in the digital age. we need to tighten rules for critical infrastructure like hospitals. we need to accelerate the training of specialists, we were 219,000 short in 2019. we need to invest in digital literacy and we also need to tackle the gender gap in cybersecurity. the creation of a genuine european cybersecurity area, with information sharing and common responses, needs to be encouraged. like offline security, cybersecurity is a central pillar for citizens to trust.
mr president, commissioner, caroline criado-perez, in her book 'invisible women', shows us how the world is largely built by and for men. airbags, for example, were not designed with women's bodies in mind and therefore did not protect them adequately from accidents at the outset. this is partly because there is a lack of women in tech fields or because the data do not reflect the characteristics of the entire relevant population. the digital transition and advances in data science and artificial intelligence make this problem even more relevant. it is therefore essential that we take measures to ensure diversity in the teams working in the digital sector and in particular in artificial intelligence where only 22% are women. only then will the online world reflect the world we live in, where 50 percent of people are women.
in writing. the proportion of women in stem fields has not increased sufficiently, therefore more girls and women need to be encouraged to enter science and technology fields. girls do better in stem subjects in the first half of school than even boys, and yet only a third of those who graduate in these fields are women. the gender gap is particularly acute in newer fields such as artificial intelligence and cybersecurity. women must be strongly involved in shaping the technologies and science that will shape our future services and working life. the role of women in the digital world requires determined work and a change in many attitudes and habits. we need to act to eliminate gender stereotypes in these areas in schools and to promote the appeal of female stem role models. at the same time, we must tackle the inequalities in employment and work-life balance. it is essential to ensure a more equal future and a more competitive eu labour market.
in writing. the underrepresentation of girls and women in science, technology, engineering and mathematics (stem) is a reality that we need to reverse. the gender gap that exists in all areas of digital technology is particularly evident in innovative technologies such as artificial intelligence and cybersecurity. in these sectors, the average global presence of women is only 12% and 20% respectively. in addition, only 18% of professionals in europe in information and communication technologies (ict) are women. more women's participation has the potential to add €16 billion to the gdp of the eu economy. stereotypes and the lack of female role models are some of the problems that contribute to the low proportion of women in these fields. but the need for stem and ict skills is increasingly growing, from healthcare to manufacturing, opening up new and well-paying employment options for everyone. in this context, it is important to encourage women and girls to pursue education and career opportunities in these areas while removing barriers, fighting stereotypes and improving access to finance and investment for women entrepreneurs.
in writing. the covid-19 pandemic has highlighted the critical role that science, technology, engineering and mathematics play in today's world. never before has the role of stem professionals been so prominent. unfortunately, only two out of five scientists and engineers are women. this gender gap is worrying, because stem careers are being touted as the jobs of the future, driving innovation, social well-being, inclusive growth and sustainable development. therefore, it is crucial to increase the participation of women and remove barriers that limit women's interests and choices in the stem sector. it is important to encourage women to take up stem studies more widely and to develop careers in these fields. i agree with many parts of the report, but unfortunately there are some problematic points in it that i could not support, such as the gender-sensitive language, the proposals for an anti-discrimination directive or a directive on women in management boards, and even the references to reproductive and sexual health and rights.
the next item is the debate on european citizens’ initiative ‘end the cage age’ (2021/2633(rsp)). i should like to remind members that for all debates in this part—session there will be no catch—the—eye procedure, and no blue cards will be accepted. furthermore, as during the last part—sessions, remote interventions from parliament’s liaison offices in the member states are foreseen.
for the committee on agriculture and rural development. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. 1.4 million citizens and 170 organisations from all 27 member states signed the end the cage age citizens' initiative. in addition to the 1.4 million signatures, i'm sure i've received several thousand emails. all together they are calling for an end to caging and higher animal welfare standards. the initiative thus not only exceeded by far the required threshold of one million signatures, but also the minimum number of signatures in 18 of the 7 required eu member states. end the cage age is the most successful of the 76 registered initiatives over the last eight years and the first ever to protect farm animals. as elected representatives of the people, we take these voices seriously. we always talk about the desired increase in citizen participation in europe with this initiative we are experiencing it. on 15 april we gave a voice to the initiators; the commission has already made a number of proposals. the commission is invited to present legislation prohibiting the use of cages for a wide range of animals, unless already prohibited. i welcome the fact that so many citizens are working for animal welfare. this shows that we as politicians have to take responsibility here. as chairman of the committee on agriculture, it is also my duty to represent our farmers. we must not forget that these already do a great deal for animal welfare on a daily basis. standards are improving all the time. it's about the right balance, like a lot of things. that is why we are calling for 2027 to be considered as a possible end date for caging. we would like to call for a detailed impact assessment, including a species-specific approach. in addition, farmers need both appropriate financial incentives and adequate compensation. it is also about the competitiveness of our european agriculture, including with regard to third countries. i therefore believe that this resolution is good for the animals, good for our farmers and a good sign to the outside world for further civic engagement in europe.
on behalf of the committee on petitions. – madam president, our petitions committee is receiving a large number of petitions from citizens and associations, who are very concerned about the various aspects of animal welfare: from ending the breeding of cattle in captivity to the protection of animals kept for breeding purposes, from the conditions of transport of animals to the banning of the cage system. today we are presenting the collective petition, in fact, the citizens’ initiative supported by over 170 organisations, and more than one million people across europe, asking to put an end to the ‘cage age’. this initiative has been submitted in the time when intensive animal husbandry is seeing greater public scrutiny at the european level than ever before. dear commissioner kyriakides, this initiative is the first valid for farmed animal welfare, and, in general, the sixth successful initiative among the 78 registered since the instrument was launched. when presenting, last december, the previous initiative – the minority safepack – i asked the commission not to disappoint over a million people waiting for legislative aspects and steps. to our regret, these steps were not undertaken, and the optional character of the commission’s action in response to citizens’ initiatives is maintained. a lack of response to successful initiatives undermines the european participatory democracy. and we know very well that trust in the european institutions is measured in their ability to listen and to act in a manner consistent with the will of the european citizens.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, less than two months ago i joined many of you at a hearing you hosted on the european citizens’ initiative ‘end the cage age’. i then listened with great interest to the organisers of the initiative, explaining its aims and aspirations, as well as the very interesting views and ideas expressed. the hearing clearly showed that the ‘end the cage age’ initiative enjoys broad support. our citizens care about animal welfare. the question for us, therefore, is not whether its aims should be addressed, but rather on how the eu should respond to achieve a phasing out of cages for certain animal species. the draft resolution you are due to vote on today listens to the concerns of our citizens and shows the path to a transition to cage-free farming as soon as feasible. our rules need to change and that is a very clear call from our citizens. during the hearing, the organisers of the citizens’ initiative also called for measures to accompany the phasing out of cages, such as financial support for farmers in respect to the transition they will go through, and an application of the same standards for imported products. the commission is looking at these requests, taking into account the need for a science-based approach and the eu’s obligation under the wto. of course, the commission’s commitment to improve welfare of animals is nothing new, and it does stretch back decades. i said that during the hearing in april. it does remain a moral, a health and an economic imperative, and for me it is a personal commitment to strive for change. our promise under the farm-to-fork strategy to undertake a thorough review of existing eu animal welfare legislation will be delivered into action. work on this has already started. even if the eu has truly done a lot in the area of animal welfare, there’s no doubt that more needs to be done and can be done and we are determined to join forces with you towards this direction. we have completed the evaluation of our 2012-2015 animal welfare strategy. the findings are now available online. they reveal an overall improvement in animal welfare across the eu in recent years. we have come a long way since the first eu legislation of animal welfare in the 1970s. equally, the evaluation points to areas where we could perform better, including simplifying the legislative framework. in addition, the fitness check of existing eu animal welfare legislation is underway and the commission will be reporting back on this at the end of this year. so your views in today’s debate, the resolution we are discussing and the follow-up we will give to this initiative later this month will be valuable inputs when we review legislation and decide on future action. independent scientific research and an analysis of the social and economic impact will be our compass when developing legislation and addressing citizens’ concerns. it’s clear that in this endeavour we need measures that will mitigate the impact on farmers when they need to make structural adaptations to the way they produce, and at the same time we acknowledge the need for sustainable food systems, the ethical dimensions around farming animals and the need to find solutions that involve farmers but society at large. in your resolution, you have highlighted the need for a more comprehensive food policy and well-functioning supply chain where all steps in the chain take their responsibility for more sustainability. it is what set in motion the farm-to-fork strategy and the actions we are discussing today in the area of animal welfare are very important building blocks in our sustainable food policy. so in ending, honourable members, i’m waiting to hear with great interest the issues you raised which will feed into our work. acting to improve the welfare of animals is an ethical, social and economic imperative. we should listen to our citizens’ voices and make this transition towards a sustainable approach as smooth as possible. this is european democracy in action at its best, and as such it deserves our support and our attention.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr pimenta, for his excellent report, and also the rapporteur, mr pimenta, for his excellent report. i want to say that i take the animal welfare citizens' initiative very seriously because it matters to me, too. and i say this to the people who write me dozens of emails, and i also want to say to them that we don't have to wait for new legislation to be passed, but we can change this situation today by taking an interest in what we buy, and buying meat and eggs from farmers in our immediate vicinity. of course, i take the work of farmers very seriously and their concerns about competition from third countries and also about whether citizens will be willing to buy eggs, even more expensive. they are concerned about the high cost and the rise in food prices. we are asked whether citizens will be willing to pay extra for more expensive eggs from alternative farms or whether we will help them to switch to other farming methods that require an initial investment. the end of cage farming is therefore not just a matter for concerned citizens and animal welfare groups, but for society as a whole and farmers in particular. i want to thank the members of the committee on agriculture and rural development for the fact that we voted overwhelmingly in favour of a resolution calling on the commission to bring forward legislation to ban cage-farming from 2027. that's the key message. equally important, we are calling for support for farmers who could make this change in farming and for the protection of european farmers from competition from third countries where they do not meet the same high standards. it is our commitment to the citizens and also to the agricultural community.
the text we are about to vote on is a success of our democratic functioning and a proof that every citizen has the right to participate in the life of the union through instruments such as the european citizens' initiative. in the face of almost one and a half million citizens calling for an end to cage farming, parliament has taken an active part in this challenge, calling on the commission to bring forward as soon as possible the legislative instruments needed to initiate the transition to cage-free farming systems. animal health, commissioner, is our health, and farmers and breeders in the union have shown that they are aware of this close interconnection by investing in the abolition of cages for many animal species and in increasingly innovative breeding systems, which have enabled the union to boast the highest standards of animal health and welfare in the world today. a further effort is now needed to ensure that the use of cages is definitively phased out, making way for more appropriate systems of confinement according to the characteristics of each animal species. it will be up to us to guide and encourage our farmers to make a leap forward in the quality of farming systems, which will benefit citizens and consumers and which will have to be defended at international level and fully respected by our trading partners.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, we are here today because over 1.3 million citizens have asked the eu to stop caging animals in the european union. in cages, animals can’t move or turn around, can’t stand up or flap their wings. they are simply unable to express their natural behaviour. this is unethical and it concerns eu citizens, so it must also concern us, policymakers. today, parliament must send a clear signal: cage farming must be a thing from the past, as from 2027 in the european union. to protect our farmers, it is also of utmost importance that imported products respect the same rules followed in the eu. but why should this ban already happen in 2027? because the ban mustn’t be too soon or too late. otherwise, those farmers who transit first will be at a disadvantage if there is a longer transition period. in fact, in the eu, we have many countries that already make certain kinds of bans in cage practices. we must then support the dates that promote a level playing field for our farmers. an important impact assessment is also relevant to guide policy—making if this system shifts, but it should be noted that it must not delay the time—frame set for the ban, or lower ambitions. so to conclude, if we want to stop cages in europe, we must call for the eu to stop supporting intensive animal farming, and to reduce the consumption of animal products.

author. madam president, i recall that in 2019, in sri lanka, after the european parliament's election observation mission, beyond the considerations on the electoral act and recommendations for the future, i mentioned how touching it had been to see the sinhalese, side by side, exercising their democratic right, regardless of their origin, religion or social position. a sign of their willingness to work together, which politicians and leaders should interpret as forcing themselves to unite the country. none of this was said by accident. the profile of who was rising to power and the highly divisive rhetoric of the election campaign made me apprehensive. over time, the rollback of constitutional safeguards, the political obstruction of accountability, the exclusionary rhetoric, the intimidation of civil society and the use of the anti-terrorism law to promote arbitrary detentions of activists, lawyers and writers have shown that my fears were well founded. in the face of this, the european union's action in defence of the universal values which guide it must not be ambiguous. all those detained under this law must be given a fair trial and, if not charged, released immediately. the timing of the assessment of the preferential instrument is an opportunity to respect sri lanka's human rights obligations.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, autism spectrum disorders are a diverse group of conditions. although there are no official records in many countries, we do know that the number of diagnosed cases is increasing significantly. one of the main difficulties is the lack of knowledge about the disorder and its causes. we stress the importance of the union investing in research in this field. although the age of detection varies from case to case, there are warning signs that indicate the need for a thorough assessment. member states should be encouraged to be able to diagnose children as early as possible. often parents turn to health systems without receiving a response. the prognosis and course of a person with asd are directly related to the care received and the timing of its onset. a person who has access to specialised and personalised care from an early age has greater and better development opportunities, quality of life and optimal social inclusion. adapting the type of schooling to the individual needs of each child is a minimum that all education systems in all member states should address, especially for students with asd. the coexistence of specialised, combined and mainstream schooling with sufficient intervention and quality resources for students with asd is essential as a measure to promote greater inclusion of these people. we denounce the tendency in many countries, such as spain, towards an egalitarian education without resources to meet special situations and without respecting the opinion of parents. the union can provide an opportunity for the exchange of good practice between member states to provide better opportunities for people with asd and to reduce all their barriers. let's make the most of it. to improve their situation, we must avoid the ideological temptation that hides behind the term 'discrimination', a real mantra of this parliamentary legislature that leads us to confrontation and victimhood, as happens with the ambiguous term 'intersectional discrimination', which destroys any possibility of equality. it is, on the contrary, desirable to approach the question from a scientific point of view, solving the practical and real difficulties faced by these people. we encourage them to establish constant communication with the institutions that live with these people and represent them in order to be able to materialize the most suitable solutions. the integration of these people into the labour market is a challenge for all of us and a necessity to enrich ourselves with their value in society. the high unemployment rate of these people tells us how little capacity we have to give them the support they need throughout their lives. let's identify the strengths of these people to help them find the jobs where these profiles can fit in the job market. studies show that digital jobs of high difficulty and demand can be performed with greater excellence by people with asd. let's provide them with specialized information to train them in the different areas of professional and personal development. these people need to strengthen their emotional and social dimension and communication skills.
on behalf of the left group. mr president, the employment figures show that there is discrimination against disabled people in the labour market and within that discrimination there is even more discrimination against people with intellectual disabilities and, in this case, autistic people. alfred einstein - who is speculated to have been an autistic person himself - once said that it was easier to disintegrate an atom than a prejudice. and that's the reality of ignorance about the diversity that exists within the autism spectrum. an ignorance that leads to the ignorance of abilities, sometimes even extraordinary, that condemns people with autism to a marginalization in all social life and, very particularly, in the labor market. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social partners are able to participate in the work of the european social fund. to do this we need active policies, policies that require states, public institutions and also private institutions, especially the largest ones, to have policies of quotas and the integration of autistic people into their staff. this also requires training policies for employers so that these institutions can properly integrate people with autism. just as we have public policies to remove architectural barriers for people with motor disabilities, we need to have public policies to remove cultural and organizational barriers to integration into the labor market for people with intellectual disabilities. we also need to make the paradigm of independent living the disability policy in the european union, and we know that there are very unequal states in the development of this policy. my country is one of the most backward in the european union in this respect. we need early childhood education through to adult vocational training for people with autism and we also need independent living support services that are compatible with the pursuit of a professional activity and the development of a full professional career. this is possible, but we need the institutions that legislate, the institutions that govern, to be the first, to be the first to apply these principles. we need integration into the general labour market, full integration into the labour market and this must start in public institutions and must be built up in society as a whole. let's do this !
mr. chairman, according to worldwide statistics, approximately one in every hundred people has been diagnosed with an authenticity spectrum disorder. so in europe, that's five million people. and even as society is becoming more open and legislators are adopting new strategies and legislation to remove barriers and improve lives for people with disabilities, when it comes to the employment of people with autism spectrum disorders, it seems like we're stuck. people with autism face high levels of discrimination on a daily basis and their employment rate is only 10%. this is very low compared to more than 40% of employment for people with other disabilities. and up to 80% of people with this diagnosis can be employed at least part-time. many times we hear stories of responsible, successful employees, even very talented people who have a great memory, can draw maps and plans with absolute accuracy, or can learn several foreign languages. however, these talents cannot be shown to the world. in slovakia, but also in other member states, there are successful initiatives aimed at employing people with autism. but these are run by non-profit organizations or initiatives of parents who want to help their children to be able to realize themselves. there is very little support from the states and the member states for such initiatives. even though these initiatives show us how it can be done. and it takes so little. supporting such initiatives, collecting data, sharing good practices, connecting jobseekers and employers, training on how to approach employees and colleagues together, and a little empathy and understanding would be enough. i trust that the european commission will do everything it can to make the strategies to promote the employment of people with autism spectrum disorders successful. because by empowering their employment, we will not only help the autistic people themselves to fulfill their potential, but we will fulfill our commitment as a society to the convention on the rights of persons with disabilities.
mr president, i would like to thank you. are you a person with autism? then you certainly have some unique skills that the rest of us don't. but unfortunately, there are skills that are all too often seen as a challenge rather than a potential. and that's one of the reasons why only one in ten europeans with autism has a job to look up to. a job with colleagues, with responsibilities and with the opportunity to expand. we must change this by creating a labour market framework that allows the unique and overlooked talents to flourish. before the coronavirus hit, i was on a business trip with the specialists. it's a great company that finds special talents for the jobs that lack unique labor. with the help of companies like the specialists, workplaces in europe can be helped to employ those on the autism spectrum who are able to work. here, the candidate is dressed to prepare him or her for the job market, and then the workplace can create a space where these unique talents can flourish. they can do this, for example, by setting up traineeships with guidance and support. but the workplace also needs to think in new ways, including by creating new roles that match the skills and competencies that people on the autism spectrum actually have. they are not to be employed under different conditions, but they may need to have a slightly different working environment. in this way we can ensure full employment, employment which creates value for the employees as well as for the workplace. the companies that see this potential in the different employees are not just socially responsible. they're smart, too. they've got some very loyal and capable employees because they gave them a chance. autism must never be a barrier to people's ability to become part of the community in the workplace. that is why we must enable all employees, all people, to fulfil their unique potential.
mr president, according to the world health organization, one in every 270 people has an autism spectrum disorder. these disorders are diverse, and the abilities and needs of people with autism vary and may evolve over time. some can live independently, and others need constant, lifelong support. people with autism are often stigmatized and discriminated against, facing difficulties or deprivation of health care, education, employment, or activities in their communities, such as cultural or sports. it is essential that they share the same rights and privileges as the whole population to the extent of their possibilities, taking into account their best interests. these rights must be enhanced, protected and enforced by appropriate legislation in each state. this is advocated in the charter for persons with autism, adopted in the form of a written declaration by the european parliament on 9 may 1996. 25 years later, these statements remain accurate and unfortunately timely. in fact, there has been very little progress in guaranteeing the rights and improving the living conditions of people with autism. it is urgent to achieve high levels of inclusion in school and society, thereby facilitating inclusion in the labour market. both at school and at work, equal conditions of access must be guaranteed, without discrimination, and the necessary accommodation or other specific support must be provided. we need to respect the characteristics and the pace of people with autism, without looking at immediate outcomes that are measurable on the scale of pseudo-normality or according to productivity standards. in the area of health, access to follow-up and the provision of technical, medicinal or therapeutic facilities that promote autonomy and quality of life, healthy living without pressure and without the need to choose medicines, technical aids or consultations on the basis of disposable income, which may not be sufficient to cover significant and very costly expenses essential for the lives of these people and for their integration into society, the family and the world of work. in short, it is urgent to ensure a set of rights in education, work, social protection and access to health, including mental health, technical aids and therapies essential to increasing levels of independence, autonomy, inclusion and quality of life.
on behalf of the s&d group, madam president, commissioner elisa ferreira, fellas, we did it! far from the spotlight that accompanied the establishment of the multiannual financial agreement, we have done all the silent work that is less understood and which has enabled us to give substance to these regulations which will now, in fact, allow all these resources to be used, which represent solidarity but also the promotion of cohesion in the context of the european internal market. a real opportunity for our regions, more necessary than ever, in the wake of this pandemic that we have also begun to face with the crii regulations through the cohesion funds of the structural funds. this is where we started and it is also with the resources of cohesion that we will face and respond to this pandemic, because cohesion policy is, in fact, the most important instrument for promoting economic convergence in the european area. it was therefore worth the dedication of everyone, from our committee chair to our commissioner, to all the other rapporteurs, to get to the end and deliver these results so that european funds can now reach the member states quickly and be applied for the benefit of people and regions. i had particular responsibilities in the negotiation of interreg. i want to highlight a few important aspects. european territorial cooperation brings regions, people and organisations closer together and thus gives a greater and deeper spirit to cooperation between member states and regions. the dialogue and the spirit of commitment in this process have led to a for these regions alone. these are important improvements. other funds could be set aside, but the most important thing is to get these funds into the field now for the benefit of the people of europe.
in writing. cohesion policy aims to promote the harmonious development of all eu member states and regions, to strengthen economic, social and territorial cohesion, and to contribute to reducing disparities between the levels of development of the various regions. this instrument is and must remain the main investment policy in the european union, in employment and in overall growth, but it is important to adapt this policy to the new times. it is essential to focus on more flexibility and less red tape in accessing european funds, to channel cohesion policy towards projects that contribute to a smarter europe, with a focus on digitalisation and the creation of new jobs; on sustainable growth, investing in the circular economy, the fight against climate change, the energy transition and renewable energies. it is also urgent to commit ourselves to a more social europe, by putting the european pillar of social rights into practice and to a europe that leaves no one behind. cohesion policy must be an instrument of european solidarity and support development strategies at local, regional and national level, taking into account the specificities of the different regions, such as the outermost regions, and ensuring that we do not have a europe at different speeds.

on behalf of the left group. other mr president, under the fascist dictatorship in portugal, abortion was punishable in any circumstances by a prison sentence of two to eight years. it was estimated that there were 100,000 clandestine abortions a year, the third leading cause of maternal death. in democracy, thanks to the struggle of women, voluntary termination of pregnancy was decriminalized in 2007 and the data show that since then there has been a gradual reduction in the number of abortions. those who oppose the decriminalization of voluntary termination of pregnancy know well that banning it is not effective in combating abortion. it only makes it clandestine, unprotected and dangerous to the physical and mental health and sometimes to the very lives of women, but only to those who do not have the means to do so in a safe place in another country. sexual and reproductive health and rights are not limited to giv. defending them also requires strengthening public services, namely a quality, free public health service and access to reproductive health services, including maternal and newborn health care, services for the prevention and treatment of sexually transmitted diseases, free access to contraceptives, family planning services, the implementation of sex education, the rights of lgbtqi+ persons. we support this report because it reflects decades of women's struggle for their rights. the way is forward, not backward. don't count on us to get you back to the old days.
on behalf of the id group. mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, supercomputers, a kind of supercomputer, are now one of the keys to digital sovereignty. climate science, cyber security, artificial intelligence, medical research - these are all areas where supercomputers are becoming indispensable. today, only 5% of supercomputers are european and none reach the processing power of the hexaflop, i.e. more than one billion billion operations per second. the creation of the european high-performance computing industry is therefore a priority, and our sovereignty is at stake. and i admit that we are happy that this european joint undertaking project for high performance computing is coming to an end, after almost 8 years of waiting. however, the european union persists in its adherence to this ideology of free competition, which is responsible for the destruction of its industry and know-how. of the seven supercomputers ordered by the european commission, only four came from a european company, in this case atos, and three from hewlett packard in the us or fujitsu in japan. how can you, in the report we are considering today, boast about the need for the european union to be independent in terms of computing power and at the same time have supercomputers built by foreign companies? in addition, it is imperative that the european union recreates a european industry for physical computer equipment. it is inconceivable, as is the case with atos, that the components used are supplied solely by predominantly american companies such as amd, intel or nvidia. the european union also needs to invest heavily in semiconductors. we can no longer depend on asian chip foundries, as the current shortage shows. finally, the european union blames a major technological backlog on the ideological blindness and incompetence of some governments. free competition, when it is aimed at strategic sectors, is nonsense. only independence and sovereignty can enable us to regain our technological place in the concert of nations.
member of the commission. – mr president, the motion for a resolution of the european parliament makes a number of valuable suggestions. we know that many of them reflect a similar approach as the council, and we expect them to be reflected in the final regulation that is planned for adoption as early as july. for example, the importance of the smes’ access and support for supercomputing, which was already at the heart of the commission’s proposal, is further enhanced. the proposals of the parliament to simplify the administrative overheads for implementing the actions is reflected in the central financial management, which is a major breakthrough. we also welcome the suggestion to increase involvement of the users in the joint undertaking’s infrastructure activities by creating a user forum. this is consistent with the opinion of the council, that has strengthened the user participation by highlighting that the design of supercomputers should be based on the user needs. i would like to reiterate our appreciation to the parliament for this timely and valid input that will allow a continuity of the ongoing activities of the joint undertaking and start of the new ones without any disruption.
