mr president, i would like to thank you for your service. education and culture are europe's most important resources and the guarantee of its competitiveness. it is therefore important that member states provide quality and sustainable education and training that is accessible to all, and the european education area programme is an important help in this regard. everyone has the right to education. this is the basis of social cohesion. education spending is an investment in our future and should be a priority for member states. the european education area can provide more opportunities, mobility and international experience, while respecting the competences of the member states in the field of education policy. the eot is important for higher education vocational training, for university cooperation, for the recognition of diplomas and qualifications. the hungarian higher education model change, which aims to improve the competitiveness of universities and increase their funding, can serve as a good example, as it seeks to create a more predictable operating environment, to provide students with a better quality education. the main objective is to make universities central to research and development and innovation, and to increase the level of knowledge of foreign languages. it is essential to pay attention to the teaching of national minority and regional languages in the context of the eot, as this is also part of equal opportunities.
mr president, commissioner, first of all i would like to congratulate mrs šojdrová on her comprehensive report. i shall focus on the need to strengthen the teaching of european integration in schools. to this end, i think it is particularly useful to include in the curricula of secondary or upper secondary education a specific subject on the european union covering the background, objectives and functioning of the union's institutions. the council of may 2018 already approved recommendations along these lines, which i have been advocating for many years. in my country, spain, there are regions where this european subject is already a reality. for example, in the community of madrid pupils have the option of studying a subject on the european union in the third and/or fourth year of compulsory secondary education. in galicia too, pupils can study a subject on european values. to value something, you need to know it. i therefore believe that it is appropriate to introduce this subject in education programmes in order to give citizens a better understanding of the process of european integration. the erasmus experience is very useful, but i think it is also necessary to encourage young people to have a better knowledge of the european union and its institutions.
mr president, i would also like to begin by thanking my colleague mrs šojdra. in general, access to education is covered by our highest document, the charter of human rights of the european union, and education is a significant value of the european union. in general, investing in people always pays off, let alone in education. and i think that an educated person is less able to control themselves, to resist misinformation. simply an educated person is our imaginary weapon, and that's why i support this holistic approach, of course, the lifelong learning approach, the fight against misinformation that i've already talked about. let's all work together on the value of education. if we can't even agree on that value, then i wonder what we're left with. i wholeheartedly support this report and thank you again for your attention.
madam president, we reject any tendency to commercialise knowledge, education and research and we oppose the vision that emanates from the policies of the european union. as in the conception of the so-called european higher education area or the bologna process, it is waving at concepts such as mobility, lifelong learning, the recognition of skills, qualifications, diplomas and degrees, concealing that this process limits access to education, turns it into a commodity, clusters it into blocks that are more easily privatizable, making it difficult for those with less economic conditions to access education. we believe that cooperation between member states is necessary, but we must never forget that it is the responsibility of each country to educate its citizens, and that it is its responsibility to guarantee universal and free public access, which meets the needs of society and its development and not the interests of the large economic groups. education is a right for all, not a privilege for the few.
mr president, commissioner, all europeans must have access to the latest generation of covid-19 treatments. unfortunately, today that is not happening. every day, hundreds of romanians die in hospitals in the absence of adequate treatment. this situation can't go on. the european union must intervene where such problems exist. the european approval of new medicines, including those based on monoclonal antibodies, is a good solution and creates the conditions for saving many lives. that is why i propose that we jointly purchase these treatments at european level, as we have done for the vaccine. this will ensure that all europeans have access to these treatments in a fair and affordable way. let's show the human face of europe and make this european procurement procedure for all state-of-the-art treatments fast!
mr. chairman, colleagues, in order to maintain balance and to spoil the impression of both the praise in the last two debates, i cannot help but criticize the behaviour in this room today in the talks, in the debate on the situation on the polish-belarusian border. what's happening there is not a migrant crisis. what's happening there is not a humanitarian crisis. what's going on there is a security crisis. two regimes are organizing and directing people to the external border of the european union in order to violate it and to threaten the security of the european union. unfortunately, the people in this room didn't understand that, and instead, they got into a domestic political battle. they rushed to clean up their own accounts and to seek some small domestic political gain by criticizing the polish government. instead of doing that, they should have supported him and i continue to argue that we should support the polish government, which is protecting the borders of its country and the european union in the same way that the bulgarian government, the greek government, the italian government, the spanish government, no matter who is in power at the moment, were protecting them. there is no reason not to support these governments because they are protecting the borders of the european union.
mr president, the european union presented itself in glasgow at cop 26 with the false image of the climate champion. the approach to climate change, whether in the field of combating or adapting, is characterised by insufficient and inadequate measures, unrelated to genuine social justice objectives and with questionable environmental outcomes, based on market solutions such as emissions trading or green taxation, which have already revealed their ineffectiveness and perversity. the objective 55 package retains all the centrality of this type of approach. the closure of the matosinhos refinery in portugal is an example of the consequences of this path. production capacity is destroyed and emissions are limited to crossing the border, moving a few kilometres. we need another way. a path that involves public control of strategic sectors such as energy, the encouragement of local production and consumption, fair regulation of international trade, mobility policies that enhance public transport, a normative approach to emission reduction as opposed to a market approach. capitalism is not green.
madam president, commissioner, last week i was part of a parliamentary delegation to the special committee on artificial intelligence and we had the opportunity to discuss in depth technology cooperation with members, presidency services, the department of state and commerce, stakeholders and various think tanks. we have converged on the purpose of accelerating innovation. we even converge on the values we need to protect, in the name of a democratic model for the digital. but there is still a huge gap in the regulatory tools that we should use to do this, so that data is equally protected, or in the risk approach of ai. we therefore need to be open on both sides of the atlantic to building bridges and common solutions, which do not have to be exactly the same, but which, in the end, allow us, as the vice-president said, a digital space of trust.
in writing. the pharmaceutical strategy is one of the pillars of the european health union and seeks to ensure access to innovative medicines at affordable prices for patients; to address unmet medical needs, for example in the areas of antimicrobial resistance, cancer and rare diseases; to support the competitiveness, innovation and sustainability of the eu pharmaceutical industry and the development of high-quality, safe, effective and greener medicines; to strengthen crisis preparedness and response mechanisms and address security of supply; and to ensure a strong eu voice in the world by promoting a high level of quality, efficacy and safety of medicines. we aim, among other things, to: ensure that patients are at the centre of all policies; safeguard the public interest, particularly where there is public funding and incentives, in terms of accessibility, pricing of medicines, transparency and traceability of investments; establish fair pricing and reimbursement policies that do not compromise the sustainability of health systems; implement intellectual property policies that put public health first; and promote more joint public procurement. only then can we ensure that everyone has access to the medicines they need, regardless of their illness, age, location, or economic status.
madam president, for my country, the european union has already been a reference point in environmental law and has already been a factor in modernising some of the actions of the authorities in portugal in this area. however, this line of action of the european union has been gradually degraded. during this mandate we have submitted to the commission numerous complaints about various watercourses in portugal, rio ave, ribeira de travassos and others, which the commission knows constitute violations of the water framework directive, and we have not had any satisfactory response from the commission. the same applies to the attacks in the ria formosa nature park and the guadiana river basin, protected areas of community interest. the european union continues to have the floor, but it is taking less and less concrete action to protect these environmental rights and to enforce community law. it would be as much concerned as it is in other areas, such as labour law, where it has no competence, but it is putting pressure on the member states far beyond what the law allows it to do.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this is an agreement for reform and not an agreement for a break. it's reform in the right way. it continues to look at agriculture from an economic point of view, it supports investment and it supports income, but it also looks at it from a social and environmental point of view. it is an agreement which encourages young farmers and points out new practices to them. the cap, the common agricultural policy, must continue to be an important policy of cohesion between the countryside and the city. you cannot help but look at farmers as the real agents of this change. but, commissioner, what a welcome, now in 2022 we will have a crucial year to see whether the secondary legislation that is coming, which is very important, does not throw out the window what has already entered the farmers' door. we must be very careful about that. the commission cannot afford such temptations and the member states must have great ambition in their strategic plans if farmers are to continue to have hope. and that is the ambition of the member states, and that is the ambition which i also expect from the next government in my country, portugal.
mr president, as we vote on this cap today, we must not lose sight of its main objective: supporting a fair standard of living for 10 million farmers, safeguarding a safe food supply for 450 million people, and sustaining healthy and vibrant rural communities. these are traditional goals of cap, but we’re also seeking to significantly to address our environmental challenges. this can only be done in an effective and practical way by involving farmers, who must be at the heart of driving this change. there is significant responsibility on member states in the development of their strategic plans. it’s vital that farmers have a major input into these, so that there’s major buy—in from farming. they have to be sensible and achievable and make a meaningful difference to the environment and to farm viability. they must provide a wide range of eco schemes that give farmers options to ensure that they can develop to deliver their environmental responsibility and also protect farm incomes and the multiplier that is in the economy, while also investing in the long—term future of the next generation of young farmers who will lead the way forward into the future. however, i firmly believe that we cannot deliver it solely through the cap. we need to look at other issues, such as carbon farming, how our lands can sequester carbon, how we can improve our biodiversity and increase our renewable energy sector for ever—increasing energy demands. while cap gives farmers certainty, it’s only one step and we need to ensure a viable future for farming.
mr president, commissioner, after three and a half years since the european commission presented its legislative proposals, we are finally voting today on the new common agricultural policy. i am aware that this new cap is not perfect, but i am also aware that this new cap guarantees the legal and financial security of our primary sector. it maintains the food production capacity of the european union, while ensuring that our farmers and herders do so on fair economic terms and respecting the environmental objectives of the green deal, while keeping in mind the social sustainability of our rural environment. i would like to stress that even if the cap is not perfect, i would like to appeal to the thousands of men and women who lay down their lives every day in the fields so that we can have basic foodstuffs of the highest quality in our supermarkets to understand that a cap is necessary. that is why my party, ciudadanos, will vote in favour.
i was taking my two daughters to school this morning and was looking at why some political groups and some members of the european parliament are in favour of this common agricultural policy. it is totally incomprehensible to me how one can vote for a common agricultural policy which directs most of its funds to those who do not need them - therefore 80% of direct payments will go to the richest 20% of households in agriculture - which does not preserve biodiversity, so it is actually against the prairie to prairie strategy, it is against the climate act and it continues to finance highly polluting industries such as intensive livestock farming. it is totally incomprehensible to me how anyone can vote in favour of this common agricultural policy, and i will in good conscience vote against it because i really want to preserve the planet, i want to preserve the guarantee of a future of biodiversity for my children and for all the people of this united europe, and therefore i will vote against this common agricultural policy.

mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i think it is important to say that it is extremely important that we discuss the status of the energy union in this parliament once a year to sort of take stock of this very important project. and of course, it is gratifying to see how renewable energy is for the first time overtaking fossil fuels as the most important source of energy in europe in 2020. on the other hand, it is a little sad to see the eu's increasing dependence on energy imports from outside. the energy union was designed to do the exact opposite, namely to make us independent and free of putin's gas and the oil of the middle east sheikhs. and how do we do that? we are doing this by expanding renewable energy even faster than we have been able to do so far. and we do that, of course, by being even better at saving energy than we've been able to do. what does it take? it requires permits to develop with solar and wind. we see how difficult it is to get the necessary permits for wind farms, whether on land or at sea, all over europe. we see how the member states are holding back when it comes to implementing what we have already agreed. the commission has already done so. we're gonna have to get better at that. and then we must finally ensure that we have a real internal market where electricity can also flow freely and across national borders. it doesn't today. that is how we can create a real energy union.
mr president, the european citizens are now experiencing the practical consequences of an energy policy inspired by absurd dogmas: deregulation of everything, privatisation of everything, dismantling of public monopolies... we're seeing the result. the rise in energy prices is hitting consumers hard. last night, the megawatt hour of electricity was trading at 300 euros in france, 309 in belgium, 280 in germany. two years ago, the average price in europe was eur 34. these absurd prices are also caused by a market based on an absurd rule. when demand is high and the cheaper sources of electricity such as hydro, wind and nuclear are no longer sufficient, gas is used. the gas, as you know, is 70 per cent higher, and we can see the practical consequences. for a frenchman, the bill has gone from 319 euros in 2007 to 501 euros today. the group of member states that last month brutally rejected calls for energy market reform will have to take responsibility for the tens of millions of europeans who are now living in poverty. so yes, we have to act. yeah, we need to clean up. yes, the electricity market in europe needs to be reformed.
the commission has already done so. the state of the energy union report does contain many good points. let us also show the positive side of our european energy balance. in glasgow, it became clear who was ahead in the rankings of countries for climate protection. almost all the countries here are european countries. in glasgow, it became clear who was really driving the paris climate agreement and who wanted to finance the global climate fund, who invented emissions trading and who wanted to create global climate clubs. so we europeans have no reason to hide our light under the bushel. no, we must continue our path of energy transition consistently. to do that, we need higher co2 targets. but also the honesty that we need much, much more primary energy for electrification than the commission advises. but we also need a taxonomy that invests not just in cheap champagne, but in a realistic energy transition, that is, in technologies that make the energy transition affordable and safe. there must be no discrimination here, either for new gas-fired power stations with a low hydrogen content or for new generations of nuclear power. this also means regulating the networks in such a way that they can build on the gas infrastructure. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption by the end of the year. the energy transition is in line with the paris guidelines, but without ideology, it is safe and affordable.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i think this report tells us that we are on the right track. it's showing us lights and shadows and we have to work on some critical elements. i will mention two of the critical elements. the first is the renewable energy issue. the production of renewable energy is increasing, and fortunately, commissioner, it will not increase at the rate needed to meet the 2030 targets. we need to know this. my country needs 40 new gigawatts of renewable energy. how is that possible in seven years, from now to 2030? there is a problem of slow implementation of the plants and of the extension of the existing ones. it is a problem that europe must also face, in order to speed up, to open dialogues with local authorities, in short to be able to go faster in the production of renewable energy. and then there's the issue of efficiency. there's a great, great potential that's not being used. companies say - i think of cooling large infrastructure, etc. - that there is potential in existing technologies, but it costs to introduce them. so we have to work to be upstream of technological innovation if we want to increase energy efficiency. finally, there is also the problem of the urgency of the union's transition. i'm thinking gas, which has already been recalled. we need to be more interventionist like europe in managing the gas crisis and the cost of the bills.

member of the commission. mr president, mr minister, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to thank you for your speeches in this debate on the state of the rule of law in slovenia. interinstitutional dialogue on the rule of law situation throughout the union is very important and i stand, as i said, at the full disposal of your parliament in this respect, whether in plenary or in committee. in our work in the commission on the rule of law, we apply the principle of non-regression, and, of course, we ask to develop different elements to promote the independence of the judiciary, but also to guarantee some principles, such as the primacy of eu law and the binding character of all the decisions of the court of justice in europe. it is the reason why we are organising a dialogue in the european institutions, but also in the member states, on the basis of the rule of law report. the goal is to improve the situation in all the member states. next year, as you know, we will include recommendations in our report. we also have direct contact with the member states to resolve concrete issues such as, in slovenia, the funding of the press agency and the appointment of the european delegate prosecutors. i have said that i had many contacts with the prime minister and the minister of justice in slovenia about these two problems to have the opportunity to improve the situation and again to solve those two issues. we continue to monitor the situation of the presidency, but now we have a five-year appointment of the two delegate prosecutors. so it is possible to work with a complete composition of the eppo, at least with delegate prosecutors in all the participating member states. i have said that it is, of course, a voluntary choice of all the member states to take part or not, but i have said to the non-participating member states that it will be important to think about their participation in order to be able to fight better, perhaps, against all the fraud and abuse concerning the use of the eu budget. to develop a real culture of the rule of law in the whole european union, i have also tried to organise visits to the member states to have discussions with parliaments, governments, judicial authorities, civil society, students or the press. it is very important to try to develop such a culture in all the member states. of course, i thought i should stress that we will continue to use all the instruments at the commission's disposal to protect and promote the rule of law. we are going to the european court of justice with infringement proceedings or requests for daily financial sanctions. we are also going to the council on the basis of article 7, and we will do the same, if necessary, on the basis of the new conditionality mechanism. but again, we give further priority to the dialogue, but we are using all the instruments at our disposal. in conclusion, i want to thank you in this parliament for your support in this ongoing work to promote the rule of law, because, again, what we want to achieve is a non-regression principle and to see in all the member states how it is possible to improve the situation concerning the rule of law.
the chairman of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to reassure you, as i did at the beginning, that the presidency and the council as a whole attach great importance to respect for the rule of law and the protection of fundamental rights. the institutions of the european union and the member states share the responsibility to respect, protect and promote our common values, as enshrined in the treaties. the debate highlighted some shortcomings in the area of the rule of law in slovenia. let me address some of them. the reduction of resources for the judicial system was highlighted. the budget for the judicial system has been constantly increasing over the last years, and it is important that the budget for 2022 for individual institutions within the judicial system remains unchanged or has not been reduced compared to the previously agreed budgetary frameworks. the same shall apply for the financial year 2023. the government of the republic of slovenia has prioritised the financing of measures needed to combat the consequences of the pandemic and for investments in the preparation of the 2022 2023 budget. this is also reflected in the budget of the ministry of justice, which is also responsible for the investments necessary for the functioning of the judiciary, so that the increase in investment funds will also provide better working conditions for the judiciary, which the presidency welcomes. i repeat that on 8 november 2021 the government of the republic of slovenia and the slovak press agency concluded a public service financing contract, providing the agency with the appropriate financial means. slovenia is also aware of certain shortcomings in the efficiency of the analysis of the causes of the situation is currently being prepared and will be made public by the supreme court shortly. the appointment of prosecutors was also delayed. let me say that the department of justice is currently preparing amendments to national legislation that will help to optimise these procedures. the appointment of prosecutors is expected to be considered by the government shortly. as regards the european public prosecutor's office, the council will continue to monitor its activities closely and will provide it with all the necessary assistance. in doing so, good evening, colleagues. the sitting is resumed. the next item is the debate on the commission statement on a european action plan against rare diseases (2021/2940(rsp)).
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members of parliament, i want to thank you for putting european action and cooperation on the agenda today on the issue to do with rare diseases, and i want to particularly convey my thanks to honourable members ries and bilbao barandica for this very important debate. for ourselves and for those of us who know the area of rare diseases, we can understand that, given the limited number of patients and the scarcity of relevant knowledge and expertise, eu action is even more important in rare diseases, and this is where there is real added value. that is why we have been active in this area for over 20 years. if i may just share with you, i have worked in the area of rare diseases with children, and i know the need to raise awareness on this, but i also know the loneliness faced by families when they have to deal with a child with a rare disease and not knowing where to turn. so it’s time we developed a wide range of activities to support the member states and eu level cooperation. the eu has really done a great deal over the last 20 years, and its response has focused on some key elements. first of all, promoting the development of national rare diseases plans and strategies, which need to be in place; establishing and supporting european reference networks (rfns), which are possibly one of the best examples of eu cooperation, action and support; supporting the definition and inventory of rare diseases, which broadens the knowledge base; providing incentives for the development of medicines for rare diseases; and empowering patient organisations. the eu orphan regulation has clearly stimulated research and development of medicines for rare diseases, but when we look at the numbers, we can see that more needs to be done, since 95% of rare diseases still do not have treatment, and orphan medicines are not accessible to patients equally in all member states. that is why, as part of the eu pharmaceutical strategy, an important report will be voted upon in this house today. we will review the current rules to foster innovation, especially where it is needed most, and to make medicines more available across the eu. the determination to ensure that all rare disease patients, children and adults, have access to the best knowledge, diagnosis and treatment is at the very core of our efforts. the european reference networks are key to achieving this goal. it’s a truly innovative system, a model of virtual healthcare and collaboration, bringing together the most specialised centres across the eu to speed up diagnosis, to improve standards of care and to generate knowledge for patients living with rare and complex diseases. their guiding principle is quite a simple one: that it is the knowledge that travels, not the patient. the patient doesn’t have to travel, bringing those who are rare together, and this is really important. funding is key, and the eu4health programme includes the european reference networks as one of its specific objectives and will contribute to further evolution of the system. i also want to take this opportunity to emphasise the substantial eu investment into research on rare diseases, with more than eur 1.8 million allocated to 320 multinational consortia. horizon europe, including the proposed european partnership on rare diseases, will continue to support this effort. early next year, i will be here in this plenary to present to you a proposal to launch a european health data space. a lack of data is a crucial challenge for rare diseases, and the european health data space will make it easier to gather data from all corners of the eu and to make it available to researchers working on innovative treatments. many rare disease patients also actively want to share their own data for research and development for this reason. the european health data space will make it much easier for them to do so. in 2022, we will conclude the evaluation of the cross-border healthcare directive, including its provisions on the cooperation on rare diseases and european reference networks. it will provide us with important evidence and inform our assessment of the current approach to rare diseases. there will be two additional processes that will inform that assessment. the evaluation of the third health programme, which funded key actions in the field of rare diseases, and the outcomes of the ‘rare 2030’ pilot project, which has been proposed by this house. we’re going to be building on all these collective lessons, and the conclusions will then be discussed in the steering group on promotion and prevention, and with all relevant stakeholders at the end of next year. if appropriate, we will revise our policy framework for rare diseases in 2023, in addition to the upcoming reform of legislation for medicines for those with rare diseases and for children at the end of next year. i’m a believer that we need to evaluate and where we need to change, to bring about change. honourable members, there are concrete plans that will improve our approach to rare diseases. i am really looking forward to hearing you today, to having the opportunity to discuss this with you, and to really bringing together all the expertise and knowledge. because working together with all the stakeholders – including patients, families, doctors and others – we can really bring tangible benefits to citizens. thank you very much, and i look forward to hearing your views.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. in the eu, we are almost 450 million people, but this varies greatly between member states. some member states have a population of over 80 million, others of half a million. and world-class care requires both resources, experience and specialist expertise. the ability of each member state to build up that expertise for the very rarest diseases is not easy. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we coordinate at eu level in order to help the smallest countries and those with the very rarest diseases. joint procurement can help to reduce prices and better ensure the availability of rare medicines, and joint resources can build a knowledge bank that reduces the time to the right diagnosis and to the right action. it shouldn't take five years to get the right diagnosis, and we need to find treatments for more than the six percent today who are getting proper, good treatment for rare diseases. i hope that the european commission will take up the court's conclusions and follow parliament's call to draw up a concrete action plan with clear objectives. we owe it to the 30 million people who already live with a rare disease.
on behalf of the renew group. madam president, madam commissioner, i am speaking to you tonight with this mother in mind, one of the many mothers i met yesterday morning in the european parliament. she works in the parliament's research department and her son has a very rare metabolic disease. so in her case, the diagnosis was made and it's a win. the treatment exists and it's very rare. as you said, commissioner, 95% of rare diseases are still untreated. it's after that that mom gets complicated. this belgian family had to move to germany to find the only specialist who could prescribe and administer this treatment. the priority today is therefore to move beyond the silos and to think about the scale of a life, that of the patients and their families. diagnosing, treating and facilitating access to care are one and the same and these patients, who are among the most vulnerable, have a right to them. this lack of coherence between european health policies was highlighted by rare 2030, a pilot project i launched in 2017 with several colleagues to imagine, together with researchers and patients, the policies of the future for rare diseases. this unprecedented two-year experiment was managed by your services, madam commissioner, and the diagnosis is clear: we need a european action plan to unify our policies and bring them coherence in the medium and long term. the planets are aligned; parliament is with you and the member states are with you. postponing the question to 2023 is in fact referring it to the next commission. and another belgian mother, isabelle, will conclude my speech for me: "there is no plan b", she told me, "there is no life b for a child who is fighting a rare disease". in other words, we must act now.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i think that the european health union is ready for an action plan on rare diseases. and as you know, i’m a strong advocate for rare diseases patients, especially the youngest concerned by them. about 75% of rare diseases affect children, and today paediatric cancers are unfortunately still the leading cause of death in children aged above one year, in europe. rare cancer patients often face numerous challenges, like the difficulty in accessing timely diagnosis and specialised health care services, adequate treatments or social services. in order to tackle this issue, we should have a more holistic approach to rare diseases and should take into account the social aspects of a patient’s life. rare disease patients often feel alone, and their quality of life is greatly reduced. people living with rare diseases should have the right to live a normal life, and we need to create equal opportunities, as well as social protection and inclusion for patients and their families. as a former teacher, i am particularly attached to the educational aspect of children. schoolkids with rare diseases still face inaccessibility to facilities, non-adapted teaching methods and are often behind in their education programme due to the frequency of medical appointments and their special needs. so a new action plan would therefore address areas such as research, diagnosis, treatment, social care, education, inclusion, as well as data collection compliant with the gdpr. data is crucial and we should leverage european reference networks in the area of rare diseases to optimise data for patients and social benefit. as a shadow for the upcoming european health status-based file, i am calling for a data space that is seamlessly connected to the european reference networks so that we can create better health care passwords and clinical research, because unfortunately, data on rare disease patients are still scattered amongst different healthcare systems. collecting this data could help us better understand the specific diseases and develop appropriate treatment. ultimately, data can help us increase the quality of life of millions of people suffering from rare diseases.

last one is 12 years old, and that's a shame. the needs of patients with rare diseases require a greater level of cross-border cooperation in healthcare. despite the existence of a crossborder healthcare directive to facilitate such cooperation, obstacles persist that not only make it difficult to access healthcare in another eu country, but also leave an administrative, financial and madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the light of new developments in research, science and technology, the current european policies on rare diseases are out of date. despite their low prevalence, rare diseases affect some 30 million europeans. that's 30 million people with no answers and unmet health needs. we therefore need a new european action plan for rare diseases. this new action plan should be based on well-defined objectives and targets and follow the latest recommendations. it should bring together policies and initiatives at national, regional and local level on rare diseases in a single framework. it should also take greater advantage of the digital age, of health data for more information, better research, more diagnostic tools and better treatments. a new european action plan for rare diseases across the board that will make us more competitive in innovation and research, new therapies and technologies and that will allow us to respond adequately to current needs, such as earlier diagnoses and more appropriate therapies. in line with the recent un resolution, we need a european strategy that goes beyond the provision of healthcare, that looks at health in its entirety, including the social and economic components, and that thereby responds to the many challenges faced by those with rare diseases in the european union. it must be a new plan of action that leaves no citizen behind. that's 30 million reasons multiplied by their families and caregivers.
madam president, to successfully combat rare diseases, we have to overcome many and enormous difficulties. yet, i acknowledge progress achieved until now. the existing european joint programme on rare diseases is an example of a very good european cooperation, bringing together all stakeholders engaged in this area. i think that a revised action plan is necessary. in particular, commissioner, i appeal to you and to your other colleagues to ensure broadening the scope of eu finances, in particular to those who are in action – parents, caretakers, as well as doctors directly engaged in providing treatment to patients of rare diseases.
madam president, many thousands of people in the european union are affected by rare diseases, requiring a comprehensive response. i have been following the difficulties of the approximately 500 families in portugal affected by familial amyloid paramyloidosis, also known as the disease of the little feet, a rare hereditary and degenerative disease for which there are few answers. this is a paradigmatic example. with a highly successful drug for treating the disease, the commercial logic behind its distribution makes it completely inaccessible to many of these families. only by strengthening the capacity for intervention and public investment to ensure a universal and affordable national health service, scientific research capacities to develop treatments, production and distribution capacities for medicines that do not subject patients to the whirlwind of multinational pharmaceutical companies, can we respond to the needs and desires of these patients. in addition to the necessary support for parents whose children are affected by rare diseases, initiatives and programmes aimed at eliminating the stigma that some of these diseases, often through ignorance, cause in others, need to be promoted. the european union's action plan, which has always been so much in favour of liberalization and divestment, will hardly provide the necessary response.
the debate on the european action plan against rare diseases is a clear indicator that europe is making positive progress in trying to reduce disparities between member states. the current situation with regard to the neonatal diagnosis of rare diseases, when viewed from one member state to another, proves that the only way forward in building a fairer and more supportive europe is to identify good practices and to standardise good procedures. in this respect, i am very proud to mention portugal as a very positive example, which has carried out neonatal diagnoses of 27 rare diseases. it is therefore important to continue to build a european union also in the field of health. it is proven that this is one of the major concerns of european citizens and that it goes far beyond crisis situations such as the current pandemic, or the prevention and treatment of cancer (clearly understood as a priority by the european commission), extending to all areas of health.

in writing. the rapid development of communication technologies unfortunately opens up new avenues for violence against women and girls, exposing them to greater risks of abuse in virtual form: cyberbullying, cyberstalking and other types of online threats, such as cyberbullying. it is estimated that one in ten women has experienced some form of cyber violence since the age of 15. the 'online gender-based violence' should be considered as a continuation of the 'offline' gender violence and is often directed against adolescents, women belonging to vulnerable categories (e.g. migrants), people from the lgbtiq community. to ensure more effective and uniform protection for women and girls, action is needed on several fronts: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of children against cybercrime, including the prevention of cybercrime and the protection of victims. the fight against such a dramatic and at the same time transversal criminal phenomenon is no longer just a political choice but a moral imperative for the whole union.
in writing. according to the european institute for gender equality (eige), one in ten women under 15 has experienced online harassment. unfortunately, harassment is even more common among younger people, with one in five young women aged 18-29 in the eu reporting having experienced online harassment, according to the european fundamental rights agency (fra). on 9 december 2021, the commission finally presented its long-awaited initiative to add hate speech to the list of eu crimes. this important initiative must be taken forward quickly. i have already been pleading with my colleagues in the house for a year with the president of the commission to criminalise hate speech on sexual grounds. i have also written about this topic many times before and have also proposed changes to the eu's future digital legislation to give social media platforms a responsibility to prevent and remove hate speech against women from their channels. our lives have increasingly moved online. the coronavirus pandemic has only accelerated this development. i'm afraid the hate speech has followed. we need to have zero tolerance for all hate speech, and social media platforms need to be more effective in removing all hate speech.
the next item is the debate on the report on the situation of women and men in the european union 2018-2020 (2021/2020) (a9-0351/2021) by mrs sandra pereira, on behalf of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities.
madam president, we have discussed in the plenary today the work we have been doing for months on the report on inequalities between men and women in the european union for the period 2018-2020. women continue to be the main victims of existing inequalities, the gender pay gap, poverty and precarious work. we believe that economic independence is crucial for women's emancipation and for achieving equal participation in society. in this sense, we wanted to focus on labour and social issues and the eradication of the different forms of violence that women face on a daily basis in various contexts. member states should strive to implement equality policies that advance solutions that defend women's rights. concrete measures are needed which, through employment policies, eliminate discrimination in access to employment and strengthen employment with rights, collective bargaining and promote a general increase in wages and pensions, eliminating existing and unacceptable disparities and demanding the improvement of living conditions. greater action, awareness and monitoring at workplaces is needed to ensure better working conditions for women, paying attention to working time, the fulfilment of maternity and paternity rights and the reconciliation of work, family and personal life. we cannot dismiss the existing inequalities from the neoliberal public policies imposed by the european union, which have led to increased unemployment, deregulation of the labour market, deregulation of working hours, increased precariousness and low wages affecting women in particular. similarly, cuts in public services, especially in health, education and social benefits, further exacerbate the multiple forms of discrimination and inequality that women face. it is not enough to defend the reconciliation of personal, family and professional life if labour rights are not respected, if working hours are completely unregulated and if wages are barely paid at the end of the month. it is not enough to defend the right to health and sexual and reproductive rights, an area where there have been setbacks in several member states, if there are no public services to provide such high quality and free care. it is not enough to advocate a comprehensive education capable of combating stereotypes if the conditions for such responsibility do not exist in the public school and if it lacks the technical and human resources. it is not enough to advocate the fight against violence against women if there are no immediate, local social responses that allow women to free themselves from the oppressions they have suffered. it is not enough to advocate equal opportunities and access to careers if there are no public networks of supporting infrastructure for childcare, such as daycare, in old age or in the case of disability or other needs. the different forms of violence against women must also be recognized as extreme expressions of inequality and discrimination which are manifested in humiliation, in disregard for the physical and psychological integrity of the women who suffer them. violence at work, domestic violence, dating violence, lack of health care, including sexual and reproductive rights, female genital mutilation, sexual and moral harassment, prostitution or the commercialisation of women's bodies as sex objects are forms of violence that undermine the dignity, social status and rights of all women. finally, a word of solidarity with the women's organizations who fight every day to make equality a reality in law and in life, and we welcome their valuable intervention in defending women's rights. before i close, i should like to thank the shadow rapporteurs from the various political groups, their assistants and members of the secretariat, and of course charlotte, leire and filipa, who have worked with me, for their cooperation. we have managed to get a text which, we believe, provides a good diagnosis of the reality of the gender gap in the european union in the period 2018-2020.
madam president, there are persistent inequalities in the european union, inequalities that resist the law, that resist intervention. in wages, for example, 14% less per hour than men. in the distribution of parttime work: 31% of women compared with 8% of men. and many other numbers that we've heard here today. and there are new inequalities, as we showed in the previous debate. in other words, gender equality is far from being achieved in the european union, however much the contrary may be said here. it is a centuries-old culture that we are changing, and cultures, unfortunately, do not change by law: they require ambition, persistence, determination. we will be ambitious, persistent and determined in this fight. in addition to passing laws, we must ensure that they produce results and change reality. let's not keep arguing about the same thing. and we need to apply a gender perspective to all relevant public policies to that end.
respected. when we talk about poverty and do statistics, it is easy to see that more women than men are among the poorest citizens. after giving birth to children, caring for their upbringing, taking sick leave to care for their sick ones, caring for aging and infirm parents, perhaps caring for a sick husband, or perhaps even abandoning them, they have grown old themselves. despite working at work, at home, and perhaps elsewhere, their pension, in old age, barely covers the basic necessities of life. sadly, their pension is rarely enough to pay for care in a nursing home. this is especially true of women with less education and those in low-paid occupations. for being good mothers, wives, daughters, and daughters-in-law who did not think about retirement when it came to helping family members, they may be punished with a lower pension and a lower standard of living in their old age. unfortunately, we also have a number of jobs and occupations that are less well-paid because they are predominantly performed by women. it is sad when caring for cars or machines is better paid and valued than caring for a person, a family and a home. i wish you all a merry and blessed christmas, and you too, commissioner.

madam president, the answer to the structural problems facing some countries, such as portugal, is not to adopt the so-called 'structural reforms imposed by the european union', but to adopt a policy that focuses on national production. only then can we stop being a country dependent on the surpluses of the great powers, a country with a deficit and a perpetual debt, a country which undervalues its internal market and whose exports are subordinated to the interests of the big multinationals. this is the way to free ourselves from low wages and pensions, unemployment and precariousness, the emigration of young people, and the worsening poverty of life. it is the way to exploit the interior and its enormous potential, to combat the depopulation and economic desertification which, together with the lack of public investment, impose obstacles to small and mediumsized enterprises and make life difficult for those who live there. the necessary substitution of imports by national production will also have the capacity to fix populations, especially young people, who will be able to dream of a future in their own country, in their own land.
madam president, this parliament has been debating digital platforms a lot from the perspective of labour rights and has reached joint positions which are important, but there has been much less debate on what digital platforms mean from the point of view of competition, a matter which is usually of great concern to the european commission and of little concern when digital platforms are involved. with digital platforms, we have different conditions in terms of taxation, in terms of regulation, in terms of labour rights. digital platforms dump like perhaps no other private company in the european economic area. how long will it be before the european commission - which sees competition problems in everything within the european economic area - starts to see competition problems in the way digital platforms operate in sectors that already exist?
madam president, after the gdpr, which has served as a benchmark worldwide for the protection of personal data, we are now taking an equivalent step with dna for the rules of competition. a step to protect sellers who see their products copied and customers stolen by the big platforms and to protect consumers forced to buy multiple services even when they only want to enjoy one. it's a result we have to celebrate! the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the rules are applied in a fair and transparent manner. will we have the means to cope with the major technologies, especially in terms of interoperability? will we be fast enough to bring competition back to digital markets for the benefit of consumers and innovation? just as the pudding is proved by eating it, the law will show its usefulness if it is properly applied. you can count on this parliament to check the results and fine-tune the regulation if it proves, as i have argued here today, that we really need to go further.
mr president, we are here today to discuss the european year of youth, but 2022 will also be the year of important decisions, as we expect to reach crucial conclusions on the future of europe. making 2022 the european year of youth and completing the conference on the future of europe cannot be a coincidence. as a young politician and president of the youth of the european people’s party, it is a privilege to live in a union that leads the fight against climate change and shapes an economy not for today, but for future generations. a key cause of younger generations. the environmental and digital transitions are shifting the way our society works, but we cannot deny that we are still trying to recover from a crisis that is not yet over. in fact, we don’t know when it will be over. yet, every uncertainty can be a tremendous possibility. every adversity must be a new opportunity. the implementation of next generation eu needs to be a tool at the service of young europeans, providing educational training, ensuring economic growth and delivering well-paid and quality jobs. young europeans want a europe where each generation lives better than the generation before. we – politicians – must take this as a central rule and place the hopes and fears of next generations at the heart of what we do.
in writing. other on 15 september 2021, the president of the european commission, ursula von der leyen, announced that 2022 would be the european year of youth. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules. in the creation of a genuine european education area, we now see that study abroad is becoming more and more common, facilitated by the erasmus programme, and consequently there is a need to ensure mutual and automatic recognition of diplomas, qualifications and periods of learning. we must also ban the practice of unpaid traineeships, which puts our young people in precarious situations and affects unequally those from the most vulnerable social groups. special attention should be paid to young people from the outermost regions who are often unequally affected, whether in the cost of travel, in the search for housing or in access to the various educational activities, which are more prevalent in urban areas. youth represents the future of the european project and we must therefore guarantee free movement, which will promote the growth of knowledge and the sharing of cultures. we must do it without leaving any young people behind.
mr president, mr high representative, cristiana chamorro, arturo cruz, félix maradiaga, juan sebastián chamorro, miguel mora, medardo mairena and noel vidaurre are the seven presidential candidates who were detained before the last elections in nicaragua, in unacceptable conditions, together with hundreds of activists, journalists and businessmen. some might think that after the theatrical nicaraguan elections, this repressive drift of daniel ortega would subside, but it did not. a few weeks ago the former nicaraguan ambassador to the organization of american states, edgar parrales, was arrested, which demonstrates ortega's unwillingness to change his autocratic behaviour. we know that the only solution to this deep political crisis is an inclusive national political dialogue. but first of all, we ask you, mr high representative, to be uncompromising in defending human rights and in demanding the release of political prisoners.
on 7 november, nicaragua held elections. a simulation, a sort of election designed to perpetuate daniel ortega in power. seven presidential candidates have been arrested, intimidated and persecuted. the same happened to about 40 opposition leaders, student leaders, journalists, human rights defenders and businessmen. i join all those who have called for the immediate and unconditional release of all arbitrarily detained political prisoners. i join my voice to all those who are calling for free and fair elections, the restoration of the rule of law and an end to repression in nicaragua. i conclude with the conviction that sooner or later the autocratic regime of daniel ortega will be defeated.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, just a few years ago, russia illegally annexed crimea and backed violent separatists that control parts of eastern ukraine. these were concrete actions against an independent country, a partner, violating its sovereignty and international law. once again, now, russia is sending strong signals that it may commit yet another violation with a largescale military build-up along the border with ukraine. deploying, for the second time this year, about 100 000 soldiers and military equipment at that border, which makes it clear as a threat to ukraine and a threat to the stability of the region. whether the intention is a full-scale military invasion or some kind of hybrid attack, or to intimidate and destabilise ukraine even further, we must not tolerate it. the european union, let us be clear, together with its transatlantic partners, must be ready to make full use of severe sanctions that will tell the russian autocratic regime the price they must pay if they proceed with this line of behaviour. we therefore call on the european summit, this thursday, to prepare a strong package of sanctions, including at least instruments such as the freezing of the european assets of people associated with the kremlin – and i said, at least. if putin and the russian oligarchy only know the language of power and money, they must know how much they will lose if they do not behave differently from what we have seen these last few weeks and months. we would like to establish a normal cooperation with russia. we have sent all the signs in the past, but it would be beneficial for everyone if that could be done with russia engaging in a completely different path that respects international law and the stability of our neighbourhood. that’s certainly the only condition in which we could establish such a normal cooperation, but we are really far from that at this moment.
madam president, the growing pressure caused by the increased russian military presence on the border with ukraine on the doorstep of the european union defies all limits. respect for territorial integrity and the inviolability of borders are principles enshrined in the helsinki final act, which russia flagrantly violated when it annexed crimea. we cannot tolerate any more provocations. ukraine has the sovereign right to make its decisions on defence and foreign relations without fear that its choices may jeopardise its autonomy. it is always necessary to have a dialogue between two parties, and mr putin has been destroying all possibilities for dialogue. the time has come for us to face up to the application of tougher sanctions, together with the united states and the united kingdom, and to start thinking deeply about the establishment of an effective common defence policy.
president, ladies and gentlemen, mr high representative, the developments on the russian-ukrainian border are in fact confirming what we have known for some time: we need a united and effective foreign policy, and our security and defence policy is about much more than just the military aspect. as disturbing as these developments are, we must never give in to those who have no respect for the sovereignty of a sovereign country. ukraine is an important partner of the eu and nato and it is inconceivable that the eu or nato would reject ukraine's future accession a priori because russia does not want to respect the sovereignty of this country. with this attitude we are obliged to ourselves to put in place effective sanctions and to pay particular attention to the trusted transatlantic cooperation in dealing with this crisis. but this crisis is also a crisis characterized by a total lack of trust between rivals. as long as mutual understanding and trust have any relevance in our relationship with russia, we must continue to invest in that and continue to commit to some form of dialogue that we can have. talking to each other can without compromising our principles hopefully contribute to de-escalation and who knows what else. but that only makes sense if putin believes in our power and if we are credible. but we have to take care of that ourselves.
vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, i’m sorry for having been unable to follow the whole debate, as i had to attend to ghana’s president. but my friend and colleague janez lenarčič has been representing the commission and attending as you participate in this debate. from what i heard, it’s enough for me to take stock of this discussion, and i can tell you that, based on this discussion, we will continue taking steps to increase our deterrence, as i said at the beginning of my intervention, and avoid any kind of military action starting to happen: to be prepared for the worst and to expect the best, as i said in my previous intervention. well, what does it mean, after listening to all of you? it means first that we – parliament, the commission, the high representative, the european union council, the european council and the governments of all the member states – will send a united and strong message to the russian leadership, indicating that we are ready to respond decidedly to any kind of military action against ukraine, should it happen. and we will send an equally clear message of commitment to ukraine’s sovereignty and territorial integrity. we should also reconfirm our support to achieve full implementation of the minsk agreements in order to resolve the conflict in eastern ukraine. third, we will continue working in close coordination and cooperation with our transatlantic and like—minded partners, as we did in liverpool at the g7 meeting. allow me to stress the fact that the challenges that ukraine is facing go well beyond the military dimension, not least the classical military dimension. they are part of a wider hybrid concerted aggression, which involves also energy, disinformation and cyberattacks – to mention just some of the new characteristics of these new challenges. therefore, we will not spare efforts to help to further strengthen the resilience of ukrainians, of ukraine, its people and government, across all sectors such as democracy, human rights, rule of law, people to people contact, trade, energy, cybersecurity, environment, public health and countering disinformation. this is our commitment, and i am very happy to see that, in this parliament, everybody – or almost everybody, with very few exceptions – is closely supporting this approach.

the next item is the report by mr gabriele bischoff, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on democracy in the workplace: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of workers' rights in the event of a dispute between the employer and the employee. i would remind members that there is no catch-the-eye procedure for all the debates of this part-session, nor will there be any blue-card questions. in addition, as in recent rounds, remote assistance is provided by the european parliament liaison offices in the member states. i would also remind you that the speakers in the chamber will continue to speak from the centre stage. i therefore invite you to consult the list of speakers and to approach the podium when your time to speak is imminent.
member of the commission. – mr president, today’s discussion is a topic which is at the heart of the european union’s values. it comes at a time of enormous challenges, as the rapporteur has just said. the challenges of green and digital transformations while dealing with the aftermath of the covid-19 pandemic. therefore, i would like to thank the rapporteur, ms gabriele bischoff, the shadows and the committee on employment and social affairs for this report, which provides a valuable contribution to our work and reflections on the role of social dialogue in the context of company governance. our policy responses will have to address major labour market and social challenges, especially when it comes to ensuring access to the labour market and fair working conditions for future generations. there are many elements contributing to enhanced democracy at the workplace. information and consultation, direct participation, indirect participation to workers’ representatives, board-level representation, financial participation and collective bargaining. they are all aspects that belong to what we consider to be the social market economy. the report touches on many of these topics, because democracy at work cannot be achieved through one tool or legal instrument only. it requires a combination of legal provisions on how companies are governed, a functioning and well-developed social dialogue, but also good faith and trust. social dialogue is one of the cornerstones of democracy at work, and the covid-19 crisis showed that social dialogue is an essential tool, also for crisis management and for finding effective mitigation and recovery policies. we need a strong involvement of social partners to make sure that the transitions to a climate-neutral and digital economy will be successful. this means that their impact on jobs and on the organisation and strategies of companies affects all the stakeholders. at the same time, the implementation of the european pillar of social rights action plan and the achievement of the eu 2030 targets on employment skills and poverty reduction also needs a strong involvement of social partners. to support social dialogue at european and national level, the commission will present a new social dialogue initiative in autumn 2022, and we will of course ensure close involvement of social partners in the preparations. we all know about the challenges national social dialogue is facing. to address these challenges, the commission is also planning to propose a council recommendation on the role of social partners accompanying the social dialogue initiative. there are obviously limits for any eu action targeting national social dialogue given the autonomy of social partners, but the eu has several instruments at its disposal to support member states, such as our funding instruments for supporting capacity-building. there is no effective social dialogue without representative social partners’ organisations on the workers’ side, as well as on the employers’ side. an extensive legal framework is in place at the eu level and the workers’ involvement in companies. it continues to apply when companies undergo restructurings or adaptations, whether it is in reaction to the pandemic, to globalisation, digitalisation and greening of the economy, or any other change in a company’s market or strategy. i wrote a letter to all member states at the end of last year to stress these precise points. when facing an emergency such as the pandemic and the huge changes in working conditions that it has caused, it is all the more important that workers’ rights to information and consultation are respected, even if the form may have to be adapted, for instance with virtual instead of physical meetings. the first 2013 fitness check of the three directives on worker information and consultation at national level led to an overall positive assessment, and this general conclusion was shared by the european social partners, as well as by the member states. but what concerns representation of workers on companies’ boards? harmonisation in this area was attempted in the past without success. now we have 27 different systems, and it is quite difficult to come to a solution given that this requires unanimous adoption in council. there will be perhaps one day an intergovernmental conference to change this because unanimity here is obviously blocking any progress. i also want to say what you have noticed in your report is that it has to be guaranteed that if companies transform or become more european because they may change the place they operate from, the acquis in terms of co- determination existing in one member state should not be lost or diluted. the commission will assess the need for more harmonised eu rules on employee participation in the context of the evaluation of the 2019 directive on cross-border convergence, mergers and divisions. one of the key topics of the report is, of course, the european works council directive. the commission reviewed its implementation in 2018 and concluded not to launch a further revision of the 2009 recast directive, which was a result of delicate compromise, but to rather support the implementation of the existing rules and pursue in compliance on a case-by-case basis. i fully concur with the importance of awareness-raising in this area. the commission already funds an annual european works council conference organised by the european trade union confederation. we stand ready to discuss with social partners how to improve the uptake of the european works councils, as around half of the eligible multinationals currently have one, and how to maximise their potential for workers and employers. apart from already mentioned planned and ongoing initiatives, i would like to recall that the proposal for a directive for adequate minimum wages in the european union includes provisions requiring all member states to make efforts to improve the coverage of collective bargaining and strengthen social dialogue. the proposal for a directive on improving working conditions in platform work, adopted last week, introduces an obligation on digital labour platforms to inform and consult their workers and workers’ representatives of substantial changes to automated monitoring and decision-making systems, thus enabling platform workers to negotiate the algorithm. also, digital labour platforms will have to create digital communication channels to which platform workers and their representatives will have the possibility to exchange information and organise themselves. another element of the platform work package is the draft guidelines on the application of eu competition law to collective agreements, which aim to make it easier for self-employed people to engage in collective bargaining. i am aware that the european parliament will soon start to prepare a legislative own-initiative report on the revision of the european works council directive. for the sustainable corporate governance initiative envisaged in the first quarter of 2022, we are looking into effective, efficient, proportionate and feasible solutions on possible eu-wide rules on corporate due diligence to address adverse impacts in the company’s own operations and its value chains. the economy is changing fast and steadily, and we need more innovation and investments in people skills, and therefore there is a real need for rethinking companies’ governance and purpose, and your report surely contributes to this reflection.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the right-wing parties like to present themselves as the voice of the small people against the out-of-touch bureaucrats in brussels and the hip urban chic. but if the small people themselves are to have a voice, if workers are to be involved in the huge transformational decisions of the economy that are right now before us, then suddenly we are hearing very different voices. many employees still have no or only limited opportunities to have a real influence on company decisions. we have to take everyone with us, especially in times of change like this. strengthening democracy at work across the eu is key to this. instead of telling people the fairy tale that everything will just stay the way it is, they need to be better engaged. the committee on employment and social affairs has adopted a number of proposals for the creation of a european employment agency. we are facing massive changes in the way we work and produce. digitalisation processes, but also climate change, will profoundly change our society and the world of work. and, no, climate action and good, reliable jobs are not incompatible, quite the opposite. the transition to a sustainable economy is creating new jobs, especially in the industrial sector. but yes, that is only possible with change and restructuring. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social partners are fully involved in the implementation of the directive. the european union can and must play a role in this and provide a framework, especially when it comes to crossborder participation. and last but not
